Degeneration of 7 7 -dimensional minimal hypersurfaces which are stable or have bounded index
Nick Edelen

Abstract
. A 7 7 -dimensional area-minimizing embedded hypersurface M 7 M^{7} will in general have a discrete singular set, and the same is true if M M is locally stable provided ℋ 6 ​ ( sing ​ M ) = 0 \mathcal{H}^{6}(\mathrm{sing}M)=0 . We show that if M i 7 M_{i}^{7} is a sequence of 7D minimal hypersurfaces which are minimizing, stable, or have bounded index, then M i → M M_{i}\to M can limit to a singular M 7 M^{7} with only very controlled geometry, topology, and singular set. We show one can always “parameterize” a subsequence i ′ i^{\prime} with controlled bi-Lipschitz maps ϕ i ′ \phi_{i^{\prime}} taking ϕ i ′ ​ ( M 1 ′ ) = M i ′ \phi_{i^{\prime}}(M_{1^{\prime}})=M_{i^{\prime}} . As a consequence, we prove the space of smooth, closed, embedded minimal hypersurfaces M M in a closed Riemannian 8 8 -manifold ( N 8 , g ) (N^{8},g) with a priori bounds ℋ 7 ​ ( M ) ≤ Λ \mathcal{H}^{7}(M)\leq\Lambda and index ⁡ ( M ) ≤ I \mathrm{index}(M)\leq I divides into finitely-many diffeomorphism types, and this finiteness continues to hold if one allows the metric g g to vary, or M M to be singular.



1.
Introduction

We are interested in the space of
7
-dimensional embedded minimal hypersurfaces which are area-minimizing, stable, or have bounded index.

An
n
-dimensional area-minimizing hypersurface
M^{n}
will in general have a
(n-7)
-dimensional singular set
\overline{M}\setminus M
, where by
\overline{M}
we denote set-theoretic closure. The same regularity holds if
M
is locally stable for the area-functional
[
SS81
]
,
[
Wic14
]
, provided one knows a priori that
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\overline{M}\setminus M)=0
(that is,
M
has no junctions modeled on half-planes meeting along an edge). A significant goal in minimal surface theory is to understand the nature of the singular set, and the structure of
M
nearby.

Deep work of
[
Sim95
]
,
[
NV20
]
have shown that the singular set of
M^{n}
as above is in fact countably-
(n-7)
-rectifiable, with locally-finite
(n-7)
-dimensional area. Rectifiability is the best one can hope for, as
[
Sim21b
]
has constructed remarkable examples of stable, embedded minimal
(n\geq 8)
-dimensional hypersurfaces in smooth manifolds
(\mathbb{R}^{n+1},g)
with singular set being any arbitrary preassigned closed subset of
\{0^{8}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-7}
. Moreover, at scale
1
these minimal surfaces can be made to look arbitrarily varifold-close to
(\text{Simons' cone})\times\mathbb{R}^{n-7}
, and the metric
g
made to look arbitrarily smoothly close to Euclidean.

The examples of
[
Sim21b
]
suggest there is little hope, for smooth metrics at least, of understanding the fine-scale structure of an area-minimizing hypersurface
M^{n}
from only the knowledge of its tangent cones or its behavior at large scales. Said differently, if
M_{i}
is a sequence of area-minimizing
(n\geq 8)
-dimensional hypersurfaces varifold-converging to some
M
, then one cannot hope to understand the regular or singular structure of
M_{i}
based on
M
.

On the other hand, we shall show in this paper that these questions have satisfactory answers in the “edge case” when
n=7
. (If
n\leq 6
then area-minimizing
M_{i},M
are all regular, and convergence
M_{i}\to M
is always smooth.) In dimension
n=7
, the singular set is discrete, and any tangent cone has a smooth link. Profound works of
[
AA81
]
,
[
Sim83a
]
imply that near any such isolated singular point,
M
is a
C^{1}
perturbation of its tangent cone, giving a very precise fine-scale picture of
M
at every point.

The Hardt-Simon foliation
[
HS85
]
,
[
BDGG69
]
gives examples of smooth, entire, area-minimizing hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
which are asymptotic to singular cones. So, somewhat analogous to the
n\geq 8
case, two area-minimizing
7
-hypersurfaces that look very close at scale
1
can have very different regular or singular structure at scale
\epsilon
. However, in sharp contrast to the higher-dimensional case, when
M^{7}
resembles a Simons cone
\mathbf{C}^{3,3}
or
\mathbf{C}^{2,4}
one can still give a precise description of
M
:
[
ES19
]
(see also
[
SS86
]
) has shown that, besides the cone itself, leaves of the foliation are the only other possible pictures for
M
. In other words, if
M_{i}^{7}
limit to a singular
M^{7}
, then near any singularity of
M
modeled on a minimizing quadratic cone, the
M_{i}
are locally
C^{1,\alpha}
perturbations of either the cone, or a leaf of the foliation.

Our goal is to prove a similar result for arbitrary stable
7
-dimensional singularities. Precisely, we answer the following question:

Question 1.1
.

Let
g_{i}\to g
be a sequence of metrics on
B_{1}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
, and
M_{i}^{7}
be a sequence of stable embedded minimal hypersurfaces in
(B_{1},g_{i})
with discrete singular set
\overline{M_{i}}\setminus M_{i}
, which converge as varifolds in
B_{1}
to some minimal surface
M
in
(B_{1},g)
. How can the geometry and topology of the
M_{i}
degenerate near a singular point of
M
?

Near any regular point of
M
, the sheeting theorem of
[
SS81
]
implies the
M_{i}
converge smoothly with possible multiplicity, and the a priori bound of
[
NV20
]
implies that only finitely-many singular points of
M_{i}
can collapse into a singularity of
M
. So the interesting behavior is in the regular structure of the
M_{i}
as it collapses into a singular point of
M
(we mention that our results do not rely on the estimates of
[
NV20
]
, but rather obtain
[
NV20
]
’s bound in dimension
7
as a corollary of our main theorem).

Aside from being interesting in its own right, understanding how a sequence of minimal surfaces
M_{i}
can behave as they approach a possibly singular limit is important for several reasons. If one wants to answer quantitative or global questions about moduli spaces of minimal surfaces (e.g. how many diffeomorphism types are there, which geometric quantitaties are bounded, what sort of compactness theory holds), one needs to know how the geometry of the
M_{i}
can degenerate along a limit. If one wants to answer questions about the singular set (e.g. bounding the dimension or measure of certain strata), one needs to know how these singularities can behave when passing to limits.

Even if one is attempting to understand the behavior of a
fixed
minimal surface
M^{n}
near some singular point, if a tangent cone
\mathbf{C}
at that point itself has more than one singularity, then one is confronted with variants of Question
1.1
. For example, if
n=8
and for some sequence of dilations
r_{i}\to 0
, the rescaled
M_{i}:=(M-p)/r_{i}
converge to
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}^{3,3}\times\mathbb{R}
, then near any point in
\mathbf{C}
away from the origin, the
M_{i}
will resemble a largely arbitrary sequence of minimal surfaces converging to
\mathbf{C}
.

We answer Question
1.1
in Theorem
2.1
, where we show that the geometry, topology, and singular set of the
M_{i}
is precisely controlled near any singular limit point of
M
. In fact we answer Question
1.1
assuming the
M_{i}
are only locally stable, but with a uniform bound on the index of the stability operator. It turns out the techniques required to deal with stable singularities extend with relatively little effort to deal with points of index concentration.

We prove Theorem
2.1
by demonstrating a “cone-decomposition” for the
M_{i}
, analogous to the bubble-tree decompositions for harmonic maps
[
SU81
]
,
[
Par96
]
, or “neck-decompositions” for GH-limits of spaces with non-negative Ricci
[
CN15
]
,
[
JN21
]
: we show there is a finite, predetermined pool of “smooth models” (entire minimal surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
which are minimizing, stable, or having bounded index), so that every
M_{i}
breaks up into a controlled number of annular “cone regions” wherein
M_{i}
is a multi-graph over some fixed stable cone, and a controlled number of “smooth regions” wherein
M_{i}
is a (scaled-down) multi-graph over one of the smooth models.

Unlike the finiteness theorems of
[
CKM17
]
,
[
CN15
]
, we have no classification of tangent cone beyond being stable and with smooth cross section, and in particular we cannot assume our cones are “integrable through rotations.” A key technical issue we must overcome therefore is demonstrating that the cone regions do not “rotate” through families of possibly pathological cones. We do this in Sections
6
,
11
, using the Lojasiewicz-Simon inequality of
[
Sim83a
]
.

Additionally, we show that, up to passing to a subsequence, we can find bi-Lipschitz maps
\phi_{i}
on the ambient space (e.g. the unit ball
B_{1}
), which “parameterize” the
M_{i}
in the sense that
\phi_{i}(M_{1})=M_{i}
, and which have uniformly bounded Lipschitz constant. Due to the presense of multiplicity, the construction of these maps (Section
8
) turns out to be fairly technical.

A consequence of our theorem is the following finitness result for minimal hypersurface in an
8
-dimensional closed manifold. See Theorem
2.4
for a more precise version, which additionally allows the metric to change and gives quantitative bounds on the diffeomorphisms/bi-Lipschitz maps.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(N^{8},g)
be a closed,
8
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with
C^{3}
metric
g
, and take
\Lambda,I\geq 0
. There is a number
K(N,g,I,\Lambda)
so that every
C^{2}
, closed, embedded minimal hypersurface
M\subset(N,g)
having
\mathcal{H}^{7}(M)\leq\Lambda
and
\mathrm{index}(M)\leq I
fits into one of at most
K
diffeomorphism classes. If
M
is not closed but
\overline{M}\setminus M
is discrete, then
\overline{M}
fits into one of at most
K
bi-Lipschitz equivalence classes.

The only known area-minimizing hypercones in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
are the Simons’ cones
\mathbf{C}^{2,4}
and
\mathbf{C}^{3,3}
, so for area-minimizing
M_{i}^{7}
it is conceivable that Question
1.1
can be entirely addressed using the methods of
[
ES19
]
. On the other hand, the Simons’ cone
\mathbf{C}^{1,5}\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
is known to be stable but not area-minimizing (it is minimizing on only one side), and more generally
[
ES19
]
cannot handle higher-multiplicity singularity models. To compare and contrast: in
[
ES19
]
we address only multiplicity-one area-minimizing quadratic cones, but we do so in any dimension, and we can give a priori
C^{1,\alpha}
bounds. In this paper, we can deal with any stable hypercone with possible multiplicity, but only for
n=7
, and we can only give a priori
C^{1}
bounds on our maps.

In general there is not much progress on variants of Question
1.1
for singular surfaces, in large part because (as illustrated by
[
Sim21b
]
’s examples) the singular or regular nature of
M_{i}
can drastically change in the limit. A notable exception is when singularities of
M
are modeled on a union of three half-planes. This kind of singularity does not typically arise from the “usual” minimization via integral currents or sets of locally-finite-perimeter, but does arise naturally in so-called
(\mathbf{M},\epsilon,\delta)
-minimizers.
[
Sim93
]
has shown that if
M_{i}\to M
, and every singularity of
M
has this type, then the
M_{i}
are
C^{1,\alpha}
perturbations of
M
. These “
\mathbf{Y}
-type” singularities are very special because they enforce the same singular structure on
M_{i}
. In a similar vein,
[
CES22
]
showed the same regularity holds if
M
has “tetrahedral-type” singularities, and the
M_{i}
secretly have an orientation structure attached to them.

All the above mentioned results (like those in this paper) are local, allowing for
M_{i}
to be minimal in only a unit ball
(B_{1},g_{i})
, for some sequence of metric
g_{i}
close to Euclidean. Let us mention that
[
Wan20
]
has proved a
global
isolation theorem for certain
M
in a (fixed) closed
(N^{n+1},g)
. Analogous to
[
Whi94
]
’s results for strictly stable smooth minimal surfaces,
[
Wan20
]
showed that if
M
is strictly stable and has only isolated singularities modelled on strictly-minimizing smooth cones, then
M
is area-minimizing in some neighborhood
U
, and in fact is the unique multiplicity-one minimal surface in
(U,g)
, so if
M_{i}\to M
are all minimal in
(N,g)
then one must have
M_{i}=M
for
i>>1
. (If
M
is not strictly stable, or some singularity is not strictly minimizing, then this isolation can fail).

The past several years has seen significant study of the behavior of
(n\leq 6)
-dimensional embedded minimal hypersurfaces with bounded index (see e.g.
[
Sha17
]
,
[
CKM17
]
,
[
BS18
]
,
[
Car17
]
,
[
Li17
]
,
[
Son20
]
), motivated largely by various min-max constructions. In this case, if
M_{i}\to M
and the
M_{i}
have uniformly bounded index
I
, then
M
is entirely smooth, but convergence may fail to be smooth at a collection of at most
I
points of “index concentration.” Question
1.1
in this setting has been very satisfactorily answered by
[
CKM17
]
,
[
BS18
]
, using variants of a bubble-tree type decomposition.
[
CKM17
]
’s approach is well-adapted for
M_{i}
without area bounds, while
[
BS18
]
gives additional information on total curvature and curvature concentration. Most of the methods in these works rely very heavily on the planar nature of singularities and smoothness of the limit. Our theorems specialize to the
n\leq 6
case, and give an alternate method of proof to several of the results in
[
CKM17
]
,
[
BS18
]
, but we emphasize that the real interest in this work is for singular
M
.

Singular
7
-dimensional minimal hypersurfaces with bounded index arise naturally in both Almgren-Pitts (
[
Li20
]
) and Allen-Cahn (
[
Hie18
]
,
[
Gas20
]
) min-max constructions. By carefully understanding the behavior of the min-max surface and sweepout near its singular points,
[
CLS20
]
and
[
LW20
]
have recently shown that for a generic metric on a closed
N^{8}
there is a smooth, closed, embedded minimal hypersurface. In a similar vein, there are numerous perturbative existence results, which state that given a suitable minimizing or stable
M^{7}\subset(N^{8},g)
, then by slightly perturbing the boundary of
M
(
[
HS85
]
) or the ambient metric
g
(
[
Sma93
]
,
[
Wan20
]
), one can obtain a smooth
7
-dimensional minimizing or stable hypersurface.

[
Whi91
]
has shown that for a closed manifold
N
, the space of ordered pairs
(g,M)
consisting of
C^{k}
metrics
g
on
N
, and
C^{j,\alpha}
minimal surfaces
M
in
(N,g)
, can be given a Banach manifold structure, and moreover that the projection
(g,M)\to g
is Fredholm with index
0
.
[
Whi91
]
’s theorem implies some remarkable genericity and finiteness results, for example: for a generic “bumpy”
C^{3}
metric
g
on
N^{7}
(in the sense of Baire) there exist only finitely-many stable hypersurfaces
M^{6}\subset(N,g)
with
\mathcal{H}^{6}(M)\leq\Lambda
. White’s “bumpy” metric theorem is a major ingredient in many generic min-max results.

A natural question garnering recent interest is whether a similar manifold structure holds on the spaces of pairs of metrics and
possibly singular
minimal surfaces.
[
Wan20
]
has made some headway on this question, by developing a robust linear theory for Jacobi fields on minimal hypersurfaces with isolated singularities, and using this to show that for certain
M^{n}\subset(N^{n+1},g)
(having bounded index, only strongly-isolated, strictly-minimizing singularities, and a few other technical conditions), every
C^{4}
metric peturbation
g^{\prime}
admits a minimal surface
M^{\prime}\subset(N,g^{\prime})
which is close to
M
.

One can view our Theorem
2.4
as a step towards the manifold-structure problem from the opposite perspective, by showing that nearby metric-surface pairs
(g^{\prime},M^{\prime 7})
with controlled index in a closed
N^{8}
can be “parameterized” using finitely-many model surfaces. It would be very interesting if bumpiness results similar to those of
[
Whi91
]
held in the singular setting also, e.g. if for a generic
C^{3}
metric
g
on a closed
N^{8}
, there are only finitely-many area-minimizing or stable hypersurfaces
M^{7}\subset(N,g)
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{7}(M)\leq\Lambda
and
\dim(\mathrm{sing}M)=0
.

Finally, let us mention that the basic ideas that go into proving the main Theorem
7.1
are fairly general, relying only on good compactness and partial regularity theorems, a monotonicity formula, and the existence of a Lojasiewicz-Simon-type inequality for singular models. One could likely adapt the method of this paper to prove (for example) that there are only finitely-many isotopy classes of smooth energy-minimizing maps
(M^{3},g_{M})\to(N^{n},g_{N})
having energy
\leq\Lambda
, where
M^{3}
is a closed
3
-dimensional manifold with smooth metric
g_{M}
, and
N^{n}
a closed
n
-dimensional manifold with analytic metric
g_{N}
.

I am indebted to Otis Chodosh, Luca Spolaor, Gabor Szekelyhidi, and Brian White for many useful discussions, and to Andrew Putman for patiently answering my naive questions about mapping class groups. Part of this work was conceived while visiting Stanford University and Big Basin State Park. I thank them both for their hospitality, and wish Big Basin a speedy recovery.

2.
Main theorems

To more easily state our results we require a little notation. Let
(N^{8},g)
a Riemannian manifold with
C^{3}
metric
g
. Since many of our results involve changing the metric, we will typically explicitly specify the metric dependence. Let us write
\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}
for the
7
-dimensional Hausdorff measure in
(N,g)
,
\mathrm{Ric}_{g}
for the Ricci curvature,
B_{r}^{g}(a)
for the open geodesic ball centered at
a
, and
d_{H,g}
for the Hausdorff distance. If
\phi:N\to N
, we write
\mathrm{Lip}_{g}(\phi)
for the Lipschitz constant of
\phi
w.r.t.
g
. If
U\subset N
, then
\overline{U}
denotes set-theoretic closure.

If
N=U\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
, all measurements, derivatives, norms, etc. will be taken w.r.t. the Euclidean metric
g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
unless explicitly stated otherwise. So, if
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
,
\mathcal{H}^{7}
will always denote the Euclidean Hausdorff measure,
B_{r}(a)
the Euclidean open
r
-ball centered at
a
,
A_{R,r}(a)=B_{R}(a)\setminus\overline{B_{r}(a)}
for the open annulus,
d_{H}
the Euclidean Hausdorff distance, and
\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)
for the Lipschitz constant w.r.t.
g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
. Similarly, if
M
is a
C^{1}
embedded submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, we write
T^{\perp}M
for the Euclidean normal bundle of
M
,
u:M\to M^{\perp}
for a section of this normal bundle, and
\nabla
the connection induced by the Euclidean connection on
T^{\perp}M
.

If
M
is a
C^{1}
embedded minimal hypersurface of
(N,g)
, define
\mathrm{reg}M
to be the set of points
x\in\overline{M}
having the property that in some open neighborhood
U
of
x
,
\overline{M}\cap U
is a closed (as sets) embedded submanifold of
U
. Define
\mathrm{sing}M=\overline{M}\setminus\mathrm{reg}M
. Of course
\mathrm{sing}M\subset\overline{M}\setminus M
, but there could be points in
\overline{M}\setminus M
that “secretly” are regular points, and we want to make the notion of singular set canonical. There is no loss in always assuming that
M=\mathrm{reg}M
.

Let us write
T^{\perp_{g}}M
for the normal bundle of
M
with respect to
g
, and
u:M\to M^{\perp_{g}}
for a section of this normal bundle, and
\nabla^{g}
the connection induced by
g
on the normal bundle. For
M
being
C^{2}
, write
A_{M,g}
for the second fundamental form of
M\subset(N,g)
. We define
\mathrm{index}(M,N,g)
to be the maximal dimension of subspace
\subset C^{1}_{c}(M,M^{\perp_{g}})
on which the quadratic form

(1)

Q_{M,g}(u,u)=\int_{M}|\nabla^{g}u|^{2}-|A_{M,g}|^{2}|u|^{2}-\mathrm{Ric}_{g}(u,u)d\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}

is strictly negative definite. If
M
is stable for the area functional in
(N,g)
, then
Q_{M,g}\geq 0
.

Define
\mathcal{M}_{7}(N^{8},g)
to be the space of
C^{2}
embedded minimal hypersurfaces in
(N,g)
with the property that
\overline{M}\setminus M
consists of a discrete set of points. It follows by work of
[
SS81
]
,
[
Wic14
]
that if
M
is any
C^{2}
embedded minimal hypersurface in
(N,g)
satisfying
\mathrm{index}(M,N,g)<\infty
and
\mathcal{H}^{6}(\overline{M}\setminus M)=0
, then
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(N,g)
. See Section
4
for more details. We define
\mathcal{C}
to be the set of smooth (away from
0
), stable minimal hypercones in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
.

If
M
is a
C^{1}

n
-dimensional embedded hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we define
\theta_{M}(a,r)=\omega_{n}^{-1}r^{-n}\mathcal{H}^{n}(M\cap B_{r}(a))
for the density ratio of
M
. More generally, if
V
is an
n
-varifold with mass measure
\mu_{V}
, then
\theta_{V}(a,r)=\omega_{n}^{-1}r^{-n}\mu_{V}(B_{r}(a))
. If
M=\mathbf{C}
is a cone, then we write
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0,1)
. If
M
is a
C^{1}
embedded hypersurface in
(N^{8},g)
, write
[M]_{g}
for the
7
-varifold obtained by integrating over
M
, i.e. with mass measure
\mu_{[M]_{g}}=\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}\llcorner M
.

Our main result is local, and has two parts: first, Theorem
7.1
, which builds a “cone decomposition” for a minimal surface
M
with bounded index and area sufficiently nearby singular cone; and second, Theorem
8.1
, which “parameterizes” minimal surfaces admitting cone decompositions. Together, they say:

Theorem 2.1
.

Given
\Lambda,I\geq 0
, and
\sigma,\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{10^{3}(I+1)})
, then there is a finite collection of “
(\Lambda,\beta,\sigma)
-smooth models”
\mathcal{S}
(Definition
7.0.1
), and constants
K
,
\delta>0
, all depending only on
(\Lambda,I,\sigma,\beta)
, so that the following holds.

Take
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
so that
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\Lambda
. Let
g_{i},g
be
C^{3}
metrics on
B_{1}(0)
such that
|g_{i}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\delta
,
g_{i}\to g
in
C^{2}(B_{1})
, and let
M_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1},g_{i})
be a sequence of minimal surfaces satisfying

d_{H}(M_{i}\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1})\leq\delta,\quad\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g_{i}}(M_{i}\cap B_{1})\leq\Lambda,\quad\mathrm{index}(M_{i},B_{1},g_{i})\leq I.

Then, after passing to a subsequence, we can find a radius
r\in(1-200\sigma(I+1),1)
, so that every
M_{i}\cap(B_{r}(0),g_{i})
admits a “
(\Lambda,\beta,\mathcal{S},K)
-cone decomposition” (Definition
7.0.4
).

Moreover, passing to a further subsequence, we can find bi-Lipschitz maps
\phi_{i}:B_{r}\to B_{r}
and a constant
C
so that:

(1)

\phi_{i}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{r})=\overline{M_{i}}\cap B_{r}
, and
\phi_{i}(\mathrm{sing}M_{1}\cap B_{r})=\mathrm{sing}M_{i}\cap B_{r}=\mathrm{sing}M_{i}\cap B_{(1-\sigma)r}
;

(2)

\phi_{i}
restricts to a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
B_{r}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{1}\to B_{r}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{i}
;

(3)

\mathrm{Lip}(\phi_{i})\leq C
;

(4)

there is a stationary integral varifold
V
in
(B_{1},g)
of the form
V=m_{1}[M_{1}^{\prime}]_{g}+\ldots+m_{k}[M_{k}^{\prime}]_{g}
, for
M_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1},g)
and
m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, so that
[M_{i}]_{g_{i}}\to V
as varifolds in
B_{1}
, and in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
B_{1}\setminus(\mathrm{sing}V\cup\mathcal{I})
for some set
\mathcal{I}
of at most
I
points;

(5)

there is a Lipschitz
\phi_{\infty}:B_{r}\to B_{r}
such that
\phi_{\infty}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{r})=\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{r}
and
\phi_{i}\to\phi_{\infty}
in
C^{\alpha}(B_{r})
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Remark 2.2
.

In certain circumstances (when singularities of the
M_{i}
are “integrable through rotations”) then one can arrange each
\phi_{i}
to be globally
C^{1,\alpha}
for some
\alpha>0
. Ditto for Theorems
2.4
,
2.6
. See Remarks
8.2
,
8.8
.

Remark 2.3
.

Theorem
2.1
(and Theorems
2.4
,
2.6
) also hold for smooth,
n
-dimensional minimal hypersurfaces, when
2\leq n\leq 6
. In this case we additionally obtain the curvature bounds

(2)

\int_{M_{i}\cap B_{r}}|A_{M_{i},g_{i}}|^{n}d\mathcal{H}^{n}_{g_{i}}\leq C

for
C
depending only on
(\Lambda,I,\beta,\sigma)
(recovering the total curvature bound of
[
BS18
]
). (
2
) is a consequence of a more general Dini-type estimate, see Remark
6.7

There are two key ideas that go into proving Theorem
2.1
(or, rather, Theorem
7.1
), both heavily indebted to the Lojasiewicz-Simon inequality
[
Sim83a
]
. The first is that the set of densities for smooth, embedded, stable minimal cones in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
forms a discrete set. This allows us to prove Theorem
7.1
by induction on
\Lambda
, reducing to one of the base cases of either
\mathbf{C}
being a multiplicity-one plane (in which case the result follows by Allard’s theorem
[
All72
]
), or
\mathbf{C}
being a plane-with-multiplicity and the
M_{i}
being stable (in which case the result follows by Schoen-Simon
[
SS81
]
).

The second key idea is captured in Theorem
6.3
, where we show that if a minimal surface
M
in
B_{R}(a)
has very small density drop in some annulus
B_{R}(a)\setminus B_{\rho}(a)
, then in this annulus
M
is graphical over some fixed cone,
irrespective
of what
M
looks like inside
B_{\rho}(a)
. We need this estimate to rule out the cones “rotating” in annular regions obtained from Theorem
7.1
. To prove Theorem
6.3
we adapt the decay/growth estimates of
[
Sim83a
]
, which loosely say that a non-conical minimal graph over a cone must quantitatively decay or growth either polynomially or logarithmically from one scale to the next.

The third, let’s say key half-idea, involved in Theorem
2.1
is in constructing the parameterizations
\phi_{i}
(Theorem
8.1
). The construction turns out to be fairly technical, because the presence of multiplicity means that the
\phi_{i}
will only be
C^{0}
perturbations of the identity, rather than
C^{1}
perturbations. So one cannot use “naive” gluing methods to piece together the maps, but must instead use very strongly the exact structure of the
\phi_{i}
.

Theorem
2.1
implies the following global finiteness theorem in a closed Riemannian
8
-manifold.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
(N^{8},g)
be a closed
8
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with
C^{3}
metric
g
. Given
\Lambda,I\geq 0
, there is a number
\delta(N,g,\Lambda,I)>0
so that the following holds.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be any set of
C^{3}
metrics on
N
satisfying
|g-g^{\prime}|_{C^{3}(N,g)}\leq\delta
for every
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
. Then we can find a constant
C(N,g,\Lambda,I,\mathcal{G})
, and
finite
collections of metrics
\{g_{v}\}_{v}\subset\mathcal{G}
, and minimal surfaces
\{M_{v}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(N,g_{v})\}_{v}
, with the properties:

\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g_{v}}(M_{v})\leq\Lambda,\quad\mathrm{index}(M_{v},N,g_{v})\leq I,

so that if
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(N,g^{\prime})
for some
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
satisfies
\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g^{\prime}}(M)\leq\Lambda
,
\mathrm{index}(M,N,g^{\prime})\leq I
, then there is a
v
and a bi-Lipschitz mapping
\phi:(N,g)\to(N,g)
such that:

(1)

\phi(\overline{M_{v}})=\overline{M}
,
\phi(\mathrm{sing}M_{v})=\mathrm{sing}M
;

(2)

\phi
restricts to a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
N\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{v}\to N\setminus\mathrm{sing}M
;

(3)

\mathrm{Lip}_{g}(\phi)\leq C
.

If
M
is area-minimizing, then one can assume
M_{v}
is area-minimizing too.

Remark 2.5
.

[
Wan20
]
has shown that if
(N^{8},g)
is closed and
g
is
C^{4}
, then given any
\epsilon>0
and any
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(N,g)
having bounded index which is orientable, “non-degenerate,” and with the property that every singular point is modelled on a strictly stable and strictly minimizing cone (e.g. a minimizing Simons’ cone), then there is a
C^{4}
neighborhood of metrics
\mathcal{G}\ni g
so that for each
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
, one can find an
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(N,g^{\prime})
with bounded index satisfying
d_{H,g}(M,M^{\prime})\leq\epsilon
.

We also have a corresponding finiteness result in Euclidean space.

Theorem 2.6
.

Given
\Lambda,I\geq 0
, we can find a finite collection
\{M_{v}\}_{v}\subset\mathcal{M}_{7}(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
, and an increasing function
C_{\Lambda,I}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, with the properties

(3)

\theta_{M_{v}}(0,\infty)\leq\Lambda,\quad\mathrm{index}(M_{v},\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})\leq I,

so that given any other
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
satisfying (
3
), we can find a number
\lambda>0
, a locally bi-Lipschitz map
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{8}\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
, and a
v
, so that

(4)

\displaystyle\phi(\overline{M_{v}})=\lambda\overline{M},\quad\phi(\mathrm{sing}M_{v})=\lambda\mathrm{sing}M_{v},

(5)

\displaystyle\phi|_{\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{v}}\text{ is a $C^{2}$ diffeomorphism},

(6)

\displaystyle\text{and }\mathrm{Lip}(\phi|_{B_{r}(0)})\leq C_{\Lambda,I}(r)\text{ for every $r$}.

If
M
is area-minimzing (resp. is an area-minimizing boundary), we can assume
M_{v}
is area-minimizing (resp. is an area-minimizing boundary). If the tangent cone of
M
at infinity is multiplicity-one (e.g. as when
M
is an area-minimizing boundary), then
\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)\leq C=C_{\Lambda,I}(10)
on all of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
.

Remark 2.7
.

[
Cha97
]
(see also
[
Whi89
]
) has shown that if
\mathbf{C}^{7}\in\mathcal{C}
is strictly-minimizing, then for every “slowly-decaying Jacobi field”
v
of
\mathbf{C}
there is a area-minimizing hypersurface
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
which is asymptotic to
\mathrm{graph}_{\mathbf{C}}(v)
. In particular, if
I=\mathrm{index}(\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1},S^{7},g_{\mathrm{round}})
, this gives an
I
-parameter family of distinct, area-minimizing hypersurfaces asymptotic to
\mathbf{C}
. Theorem
2.6
implies that this family has bounded geometry and topology.

Lastly, let us mention that our smallness estimate of Theorem
6.3
allows us to sharpen the Corollary
[
ES19
, Corollary 3.4]
. For a minimal surface
M
in a non-Euclidean metric, in
[
ES19
]
we could not rule out the possibility that at different radii
M
resembled different rotations of the Simons’ cone (which was still sufficient to characterize singular nature of
M
). Though we still cannot get the effective
C^{1,\alpha}
estimates of
[
ES19
, Theorem 3.1]
in Euclidean space, here we can at least show the cones do not rotate. See
[
ES19
]
for more details on background and notation. The proof of Theorem
2.8
(given in Section
13
) is something of a baby version of the proof of our main Theorem
7.1
, and in fact it might be helpful for the reader to read this proof first.

Theorem 2.8
.

Take any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\mathbf{C}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a minimizing quadratic hypercone. Let
\{S_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
be the leaves of the Hardt-Simon foliation of
\mathbf{C}
, so that
S_{0}=\mathbf{C}
. Given any
\epsilon>0
, there is a
\delta(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
so that the following holds. Let
g
be a
C^{3}
metric on
B_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
satisfying
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\delta
. Let
V
be an integral stationary
n
-varifold in
(B_{1},g)
satisfying

(7)

\displaystyle d_{H}(\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1})\leq\delta,\quad(1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\theta_{V}(0,1/2),\quad\theta_{V}(0,1)\leq(3/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0).

Then we can find an
a\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in SO(n+1)
, satisfying

(8)

|a|+|q-Id|+|\lambda|\leq\epsilon,

and a
C^{2}
function
u:(a+q(S_{\lambda}))\cap B_{1/2}(a)\to S_{\lambda}^{\perp}
such that

(9)

\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{1/4}=\mathrm{graph}_{a+q(S_{\lambda})}(u)\cap B_{1/4},\quad|x-a|^{-1}|u|+|\nabla u|+|x-a||\nabla^{2}u|\leq\epsilon.

If
\mathbf{C}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a general smooth (away from
0
) strictly-stable and strictly-minimizing hypercone, then the same conclusion holds if one additionally assumes
\mathrm{spt}V
lies to one side of
\mathbf{C}
.

3.
Outline of proof

The key to our argument is the observation (by no means our own) that the set of densities of smooth stable minimal
7
-dimensional hypercones is a discrete set, which a direct consequence of the Lojasiewicz-Simon inequality
[
Sim83a
]
and standard compactness/regularity theory for stable minimal hypersurfaces. At some level this is saying that the “complexity” of the singular models
\mathbf{C}
is quantized. We prove our main decomposition Theorem
2.1
by induction on the density (including multiplicity) of the cone
\mathbf{C}
.

Our basic idea is very simple. Unfortunately (fortunately?) there is a highly non-trivial complication, due to the nature of “cone regions,” but this point is not immediately obvious so let us ignore it for the moment. Let us illustrate our idea by proving that if
M_{i}
is a sequence of smooth, area-minimizing boundaries in
B_{1}
converging to an area-minimizing cone
\mathbf{C}
, then after passing to a subsequence all the
M_{i}\cap B_{1/2}
are diffeomorphic to each other. (So, everything is mulitplicity-one and converges smoothly on the regular part).

Let us suppose, towards a contradiction, that none of the
M_{i}\cap B_{1/2}
are diffeomorphic. Write
1=\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}<\ldots
for the densities of area-minimizing cones, and suppose
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=\theta_{k}
. If
k=0
then
\mathbf{C}
is the plane, and so our assertion follows by Allard’s theorem. Let us assume by induction our assertion holds for every
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\theta_{k-1}
.

The
M_{i}
converge smoothly to
\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1}
away from
0
. For any
a\in B_{1/2}
we can consider the smallest radius
\rho
so that
A_{1/2,\rho}(a)\cap M_{i}
is a “
\mathbf{C}
-cone region.” Let’s be intentionally vague for the moment, and just think of this as an annulus where
M_{i}
looks very close to a cone, which may be
\mathbf{C}
or may be some other
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
diffeomorphic to
\mathbf{C}
.

Let us pick
\rho_{i}
to be the least such radius among all possible
a\in B_{1/2}
, and pick
a_{i}
realizing this point. Smooth convergence to
\mathbf{C}
on compact subsets of
B_{1}\setminus\{0\}
implies
\rho_{i}\to 0
and
a_{i}\to 0
. Since all the
M_{i}\cap A_{1/2,\rho_{i}}(a_{i})
, being
\mathbf{C}
-cone regions, are diffeomorphic to
\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2}
, our contradiction hypothesis implies that none of the
M_{i}\cap B_{2\rho_{i}}(a_{i})
are diffeomorphic.

Now consider the rescaled surfaces
M_{i}^{\prime}=\rho_{i}^{-1}(M_{i}-a_{i})
. After passing to a subsequence, we get convergence
M_{i}^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
, an area-minimizer in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
. By construction,
M^{\prime}\cap A_{\infty,1}(a)
is a
\mathbf{C}
-cone region, and so
M^{\prime}
is smooth outside
B_{1}
. Any tangent cone at infinity of
M^{\prime}
is a cone
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
very near to
\mathbf{C}
, having the same density
\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
.

On the other hand
M^{\prime}
cannot be smooth, because then
M_{i}^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
smoothly on compact sets, contradicting the fact that none of the
M_{i}^{\prime}\cap B_{2}
are diffeomorphic. So there are (finitely-many) singularities of
M^{\prime}
in
B_{1}
. I claim that any such singularity
x
has density
\theta_{M^{\prime}}(x)<\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
. Otherwise, if we had
\theta_{M^{\prime}}(x)\geq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, then monotonicity would imply that
M^{\prime}=x+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
. But then, for
i
large each
M_{i}\cap A_{1/2,\rho_{i}/2}(a_{i}+\rho_{i}x)
would be a
\mathbf{C}
-cone region, contradicting our choice of
\rho_{i}
as the least possible such radius.

So in fact every singularity of
M^{\prime}
has density
\leq\theta_{k-1}
. In a small ball
B_{r_{x}}(x)
around every singularity
x
,
M^{\prime}
looks scale-invariantly close to a cone
\mathbf{C}_{x}
having density
\leq\theta_{k-1}
, and so by induction (if we take
r
sufficiently small and pass to a subsequence in
i
), all the
M_{i}^{\prime}\cap B_{r_{x}}(x)
are diffeomorphic. Since
M_{i}^{\prime}
converge smoothly to
M^{\prime}
away from the singular set, we deduce all the
M_{i}^{\prime}\cap B_{2}
are diffeomorphic. This is a contradiction.

OK so what’s wrong with that argument. The catch is in exactly how we can define a “cone region,” and how these are glued to the other parts of
M_{i}
. When we do this contradiction argument, without using any additional quantitative estimates, we can only pass information between finitely-many scales, e.g. between scale
\rho
and
\rho/2
. You will find that, no matter how you define cone region (by density drop, by graphicality, etc.), you will have to allow the model cone to change with the radius. This argument will only show that for a given
M=M_{i}
, for every radius
r\in[\rho_{i},1/2]
, there is a cone
\mathbf{C}_{r}
diffeomorphic to
\mathbf{C}
(in the sphere), and having the same density, so that
M\cap A_{r,r/8}(a_{i})
is a small graph over
a_{i}+\mathbf{C}_{r}
. In certain special cases, such as when
\mathbf{C}
is planar with possible multiplicity (
[
Whi18
]
,
[
BS18
]
) or strictly-minimizing with multiplicity-one (
[
ES19
]
,
[
Wan20
]
), one can rule out the
\mathbf{C}_{r}
changing, since there are good barriers which pass information across scales by “trapping”
M
to stay close to the original
\mathbf{C}
. For more general
\mathbf{C}
this is not (to my knowledge) available.

The changing cones starts to becomes a problem because, although the
M_{i}\cap A_{1,\rho_{i}}(a_{i})
are still all diffeomorphic to an annulus
\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2}
, we lose control over how this annulus is glued into the region
M_{i}\cap B_{\rho_{i}}(a_{i})
. Actually, for our one single step of induction, this is not strictly speaking an issue, because we could push all the “rotation” of the cones out to scale
\approx 1
. Once you start attempting to induct however, this is a problem, since we need to be able to glue the cone regions at both ends.

Precisely, we require the following. Let
\phi_{ij}:M_{i}\cap A_{1/2,\rho_{i}}(a_{i})\to M_{j}\cap A_{1/2,\rho_{j}}(a_{j})
be our diffeomorphisms. By our convergence and our choice of subsequence, there is a fixed cone
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
diffeomorphic to
\mathbf{C}
so that all the
M_{i}\cap A_{1,1/2}
are small graphs over
\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2}
, and all the
M_{i}^{\prime}\cap A_{2,1}
are small graphs over
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap A_{2,1}
, and therefore we obtain diffeomorphisms
\psi_{i}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2}\to M_{i}\cap A_{1,1/2}
,
\psi_{i}^{\prime}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap A_{2,1}\to M_{i}^{\prime}\cap A_{2,1}
, which are all very small perturbations of the identity. To adequately construct diffeomorphisms from
M_{i}
to
M_{j}
, we require that all the

\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\phi_{ij}\circ\psi_{i}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2}\to\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2}

are isotopic,
and
all the

\psi_{j}^{\prime-1}\circ\eta_{a_{j},\rho_{j}}\circ\phi_{ij}\circ\eta_{a_{i},\rho_{i}}^{-1}\circ\psi_{i}^{\prime}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap A_{2,1}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap A_{2,1}

are isotopic, where we write
\eta_{x,r}(z)=(z-x)/r
.

Let us illustrate this with two (would-be) examples. In the first, take
\mathbf{C}
a hypercone in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and identify
S^{3}\setminus\{pt\}
with
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Think of the link
\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}
as a genus-two surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, that looks like the number
8
speared onto the
z
-axis. Suppose there is a smooth family of smooth, minimal cones
\mathbf{C}_{t}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, so that as
t
increases the cross section
\mathbf{C}_{t}\cap\partial B_{1}
keeps one handle fixed, and rotates counter-clockwise the other handle around the
z
-axis, and does so periodically in the sense that
\mathbf{C}_{t+i}=\mathbf{C}_{t}
for every integer
i
.

Consider now the surface
M
defined by
M\cap\partial B_{r}=\mathbf{C}_{-\sigma\log r}\cap\partial B_{r}
for some small
\sigma>0
, so that
M\cap A_{1,0}
is a
\mathbf{C}
-cone region. The annuli
M_{i}:=M\cap A_{1,e^{-i/\sigma}}
are all diffeomorphic, and in fact
(e^{i/\sigma}M_{i})\cap A_{1,1/2}=(e^{j/\sigma}M_{j})\cap A_{1,1/2}
for integers
i
,
j
, but for any two diffeomorphisms
\phi_{ij}:M_{i}\to M_{j}
,
\phi_{ij^{\prime}}:M_{i}\to M_{j^{\prime}}
(with
j\neq j^{\prime}
) which are isotopic as maps
A_{1,1/2}\cap M_{i}\to A_{1,1/2}\cap M_{j}\equiv A_{1,1/2}\cap M_{j^{\prime}}\equiv A_{1,1/2}\cap M_{1}
, the rescaled diffeomorphisms

\eta_{0,e^{-j/\sigma}}\circ\phi_{ij}\circ\eta_{0,e^{-i/\sigma}}^{-1},\eta_{0,e^{-j^{\prime}/\sigma}}\circ\phi_{ij^{\prime}}\circ\eta_{0,-i/\sigma}^{-1}:M_{1}\cap A_{1,1/2}\to M_{1}\cap A_{1,1/2}

cannot also be isotopic.

The second example is simpler, but avoidable. If the
M_{i}
had only bounded index, then
\mathbf{C}
could be a multiplicity-two plane. As the radius
r
decreases in any fixed cone region
M_{i}\cap A_{1/2,\rho_{i}}(a_{i})
, one could imagine the equatorial spheres
\mathbf{C}_{r}\cap\partial B_{r}
rotating
180^{o}
, with the effect that the
M_{i}\cap\partial B_{1/2}(a_{i})
is glued to
M_{i}\cap\partial B_{\rho_{i}}(a_{i})
via the antipodal map. In this case in fact the antipodal map and the identity map are the only two possibilities, so one could avoid this by passing to a further subsequence.

It turns out that the second example more or less captures the behavior if all the
\mathbf{C}_{r}
are legitimately rotations of
\mathbf{C}
(so
\mathbf{C}
is “integrable through rotations”). In this case, one can arrange for the
\phi_{ij}|_{\partial B_{r}}
to be small perturbations of a rotation and dilation, and so after passing to a further subsequence we get our required isotopies. For more general cones
\mathbf{C}
this could fail, as illustrated by the first example.

Instead, we can show that in fact the cones
\mathbf{C}_{r}
cannot change at all. In Theorem
6.3
, we adapt the Lojasiewicz-Simon decay-growth estimates
[
Sim83a
]
to prove that while the density drop is small,
M
must stay graphical over the same cone,
independent
of whatever happens in smaller regions. The intuition is that a minimal graph over a cone
\mathbf{C}
must either grow or decay polynomially or logarithically (in
r
) from one scale to the next, and most importantly (as proved in
[
Sim83a
]
) if
u
is not already 1-homogenous then there is a lower/upper bound to this growth/decay. If
u
starts very small but then becomes too big, the graph of
u
will start “eating” up a definite amount of density at each scale drop. We highlight that this decay-growth theorem requires no a priori knowledge of the singular set. We also mention that the estimate holds in any dimension or codimension, and for any surface with (small)
C^{2}
mean curvature.

So, a posteriori after having constructed the cone regions by our contradiction argument, we can use Theorem
6.3
to show that they are legitimately graphs over a single cone. This allows our original contradiction argument to go through. A minor modification of the above argument can also deal with
M_{i}
having bounded index.

So far, that deals with smooth
M_{i}
. But of course the
M_{i}
could be singular, and in this case it’s not really clear what
M_{i}
being diffeomorphic means. To adequately capture both the regular and singular structure, we build
global
diffeomorphisms
\phi_{ij}:B_{1/2}\to B_{1/2}
, which take
M_{i}
to
M_{j}
. Actually, the
\phi_{ij}
will only be globally bi-Lipschitz maps, and be smooth diffeomorphisms away from the singular set. Getting better regularity of the
\phi_{ij}
at singular points seems to be more or less equivalent to knowing all the singular cones are integrable through rotations.

Section
8
is devoted to constructing these maps. Multiplicity is a significant technical thorn here. If everything were single-sheeted, the section would be trivial (since all our maps would be
C^{1}
perturbations of the identity), but since we have no control over how much sheets are squished together, gluing the various maps together is significantly more technical (since our maps are only
C^{0}
perturbations of
id
). The key idea is that two
C^{1}
diffeomorphisms
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, which coincide with the identity outside
[-1,1]
, are trivially isotopic just by taking an appropriate convex combination of the two. By extension, if two
C^{1}
diffeomorphisms act by moving along the flowlines of some fixed, smooth vector field (having no closed orbits), and agree outside some small set, then these can be isotoped together along the flowlines. Even if the vector fields are different, but very close in
C^{1}
, this will work. We then build our parameterizations
\phi
to have this structure.

4.
Preliminaries

We will mostly work in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
. If
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
, then generally all norms, lengths, areas, volumes, etc. we use in
U
are taken with respect to the Euclidean metric
g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
unless otherwise specified. Write
\overline{U}
for the set-theoretic closure of
U
. We write
B_{r}(a)
for the (Euclidean) open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
centered in
a
, and we define the open annuli
A_{R,r}(a)=B_{R}(a)\setminus\overline{B_{r}}(a)
,
A_{R,0}(a)=B_{R}(a)\setminus\{a\}
, and
A_{\infty,r}(a)=\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus\overline{B_{r}(a)}
.

We write
B_{r}(U)=\{x:\mathrm{dist}(x,U)<r\}
for the open
r
-tubular neighborhood of
U
, where
\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,U)
the Euclidean distance to
U
. Define
d_{H}(U,U^{\prime})
to be the Hausdorff distance, i.e. the least
r
for which
U\subset B_{r}(U^{\prime})
and
U^{\prime}\subset B_{r}(U)
. Write
\mathcal{H}^{n}
for the
n
-dimensional Hausdorff measure, and
\omega_{n}=\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n})
for the volume of the unit
n
-ball. Let
\eta_{x,r}(y)=(y-x)/r
be the translation/dilation map.

We write
v\cdot w
to indicate the usual Euclidean inner product, and
|v|=(v\cdot v)^{1/2}
for the usual Euclidean norm of a vector. If
T(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})
is a mult-linear map to
\mathbb{R}^{P}
, we write
|T|=\sup_{|v_{i}|\leq 1}|T(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})|
for the operator norm. If we need to make explicit a metric
g
, we will write
|v|_{g}=g(v,v)^{1/2}
for the
g
-length of
v
. If
\phi:(N,g)\to(N^{\prime},g^{\prime})
is a
C^{1}
map between Riemannian manifolds, then for
x\in N
we define
|D\phi|_{x}|=\sup\{|D\phi|_{x}(v)|_{g^{\prime}}:v\in T_{x}N,|v|_{g}\leq 1\}
.

Given a
C^{1}
embedded
n
-submanifold
M
in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, we write
TM
,
T^{\perp}M
for the tangent, normal bundles of
M\subset(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
, and for each
x\in M
we identity
T_{x}M
,
T_{x}^{\perp}M
with a subspace of
(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
in the obvious fashion. We write
u:M\to M^{\perp}
for a section of the normal bundle
T^{\perp}M
, and
\nabla
for the induced connection derivative on
T^{\perp}M
. Given such a
u
, we write
\mathrm{graph}_{M}(u):=\{x+u(x):x\in M\}
. Define the
C^{k}
and
C^{k,\alpha}
norms of
u
w.r.t.
\nabla
. If
M_{i}
is a sequence of embedded submanifolds, we say
M_{i}\to M
in
C^{k}(U)
(with multiplicity
m\in\mathbb{N}
) if there is a set
U^{\prime}\supset U
, and a sequence of
C^{k}
functions
\{u_{ij}:M\cap U^{\prime}\to M^{\perp}\}_{j=1}^{m}
, so that for all
i>>1
we have

M_{i}\cap U=\cup_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{graph}_{M}(u_{ij}),\quad|u_{ij}|_{C^{k}(M\cap U^{\prime})}\to 0\text{ as }i\to\infty.

For
x\in M
and
M
as above, define the
C^{k}
regularity scale
r_{k}(x,M)
to be the largest radius
r
, so that for some
n
-plane
P
we can find a
C^{k}
function
v:(x+P)\cap B_{r}(x)\to P^{\perp}
so that

M\cap B_{r}(x)\subset\mathrm{graph}_{x+P}(v),\quad\sum_{i=0}^{k}r^{1-i}|D^{i}v|\leq 1.

We define the global regularity scale
r_{k}(M)=\inf_{x\in M}r_{k}(x,M)
.

Let
g
be a
C^{2}
metric on
U
. Write
B^{g}_{r}(x)
for the ball of radius
r
centered at
x
with respect to the metric
g
,
\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}
for the Hausdorff measure w.r.t.
g
, and
B^{g}_{r}(U)=\{x:\mathrm{dist}_{g}(x,U)<r\}
for the
g
-geodesic open
r
-tubular neighborhood around
U
. If
M
is an embedded
C^{1}
hypersuface of
U
, we write
T^{\perp_{g}}M
for the normal bundle of
M
w.r.t.
g
, and identity each
T_{x}^{\perp_{g}}M
with a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
. Write
u:M\to M^{\perp_{g}}
for a section of normal bundle, and
\nabla^{g}
for the induced connection derivative on
T^{\perp_{g}}M
induced by the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g}
of
g
.

If
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|\leq\delta
, then for every
x\in B_{1}
and
r<(1-\delta)(1-|x|)
, we have

(10)

B^{g}_{(1-\delta)r}(x)\subset B_{r}(x)\subset B^{g}_{(1+\delta)r}(x).

If
M
is an embedded
C^{1}
hypersurface of
U
, then we can express

(11)

\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}(M)=\int_{M}F(g(x),T_{x}M)d\mathcal{H}^{7}(x)

for some analytic functional
F(g,V)
(c.f.
[
SS81
, Remark 1]
). We have
|F(g,V)-1|\leq c|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|
, and so we get

(12)

(1-c\delta)\mathcal{H}^{7}(M)\leq\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}(M)\leq(1+c\delta)\mathcal{H}^{7}(M).

for some absolute constant
c
. In the special case when
M=\{(x,u(x)):x\in U^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{7}\}
for some Lipschitz function
u
defined on a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{7}
, then we can rewrite (
11
) as
Fd\mathcal{H}^{7}=F_{0}(g(x,u(x)),Du|_{x})dx
for
F_{0}
an analytic function satisfying
F(g_{\mathrm{eucl}},z)=\sqrt{1+|z|^{2}}
.

We say a
C^{2}
embedded hypersurface
M
is minimal in
(U,g)
if the first variation of (
11
) vanishes at
M
, i.e. for all
X\in C^{1}_{c}(U,\mathbb{R}^{8})
, we ask

\displaystyle 0=\delta_{g}M(X)

\displaystyle:=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}(\phi_{t}(M))

(13)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\mathrm{div}_{M,g}(X)d\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}

where
\phi(x,t)=x+tX
. In the last equality
\mathrm{div}_{M,g}(X)|_{x}=\sum_{i}g(e_{i},\nabla^{g}_{e_{i}}X)
, where
e_{i}
is an
g
-ON-basis for
T_{x}M
at
x
. We write
\mathcal{M}_{7}(U,g)
for the space of
C^{2}
embedded minimal hypersurface in
U
such that
\mathrm{sing}M
is a discrete set.

When
M=\{(x,u(x)):x\in B_{1}\}
for some
C^{2}
function
u:B_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{7}\to\mathbb{R}
, then by considering the structure of
F_{0}
, and provided
|u|_{C^{1}(B_{1})}\leq 1
and
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{1}(B_{1}^{7}\times[-2,2])}\leq\delta
for some absolute constant
\delta
, the
u
will satisfy a PDE of the form

a_{ij}D^{2}_{ij}u+b_{i}D_{i}u=f_{i}D_{i}g+f_{8}D_{8}g,

where
a_{ij},b_{i},f_{i},f_{8}
are analytic functions of
g(x,u(x)),Dg|_{(x,u(x))},Du|_{x}
satisfying
1/2\leq a_{ij}\leq 2
. In particular, by standard elliptic theory if
g\in C^{k}
then
u\in C^{k,\alpha}
for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and for every
\theta\in(0,1)
we have the estimate

(14)

|u|_{C^{k,\alpha}(B_{\theta})}\leq c(||u||_{L^{2}(B_{1})}+|Dg|_{C^{k-1}(B_{1})}),

where
c=c(\theta,\alpha,k,|u|_{C^{1,\alpha}(B_{1})},|Dg|_{C^{k-1}(B_{1}\times[-2,-2])})
. Typically we will apply (
14
) in normal coordinates centered at some point on the graph of
u
, so the restrictions on
g
and
u
are never an issue.

For
M
minimal in
(U,g)
, we say
M
is stable in
(U,g)
if the second variation of (
11
) is non-negative at
M
. In other words, we ask

\delta^{2}_{g}M(X,X):=\left.\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{t}}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}(\phi_{t}(M))\geq 0,\quad\forall X\in C^{1}_{c}(U,\mathbb{R}^{8}),

where
\phi(x,t)=x+tX
. It is well known that if
u=\pi^{\perp_{g}}_{M}(X):M\to M^{\perp_{g}}
, then
\delta^{2}_{g}M(X,X)=Q_{M,g}(u,u)
for
Q
being the quadratic form in (
1
). Here
\pi_{M}^{\perp_{g}}
denotes the
g
-orthogonal projection onto
T^{\perp_{g}}M
. Let us define
\mathrm{index}(M,U,g)
to be the maximal dimension of subspace
V\subset C^{1}_{c}(M\cap U,M^{\perp_{g}})
on which
Q_{M,g}
is strictly negative definite, i.e. so that
Q_{M,g}(\phi,\phi)<0
for all
\phi\in V
. Observe the trivial inclusion
\mathrm{index}(M,U^{\prime},g)\leq\mathrm{index}(M,U,g)
if
U^{\prime}\subset U
.

We note that by
[
Wic14
, Section 18]
, if
\mathrm{index}(M,U,g)=0
and
\mathcal{H}^{6}(U\cap\overline{M}\setminus M)=0
, then
U\cap\overline{M}\setminus M
consists of isolated points, and hence by an elementary cutoff function argument
M
is stable in
(U,g)
. This motivates our definition of
\mathcal{M}_{7}
.

If
M
is closed (as sets) in
U
, then
Q_{M,g}
can be written

Q_{M,g}(u,u)=-\int_{M}g(u,\Delta_{g}u+|A_{M,g}|^{2}u+Ric_{g}(u,\cdot))d\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}=:-\int_{M}g(u,L_{M,g}u)d\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g},

where
\Delta_{g}
is the connection Laplacian on
T^{\perp_{g}}M
.
L_{M,g}
is a self-adjoint elliptic operator on
C^{2}_{c}(M,M^{\perp_{g}})
. In this case, for every
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U
with
C^{1}
boundary intersecting
M
transversely,
\mathrm{index}(M,U^{\prime},g)
is finite and consists of the number of negative eigenvalues for
L_{M,g}
under Dirichlet zero boundary conditions on
M\cap U^{\prime}
.

As discussed in the Introduction, singular minimal surfaces with finite index, in the sense that we’ve described above, arise naturally in various min-max constructions (see e.g.
[
Li20
]
,
[
Hie18
]
,
[
Gas20
]
). We note that if
M
is a minimal cone in
(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
having
\mathcal{H}^{6}(\overline{M}\setminus M)=0
, it’s easy to check that either
M
is stable, or
\mathrm{index}(M,\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})=\infty
. So any kind of finite index must be “macroscopic.” This can be made more precise below.

For any
x\in U
, we define the “stability radius”
r_{s}(x)\equiv r_{s}(x,M,U,g)
to be the largest radius
r_{s}
with the property that
B_{r_{s}}(x)\subset U
, and
\mathrm{index}(M,B_{r_{s}}(x),g)=0
. Essentially the same quantity, as well as the below Lemma
4.1
, appeared in
[
Son20
]
. Since it is very short we include a proof here for the readers’ convenience. We remark that
[
Wan20
, Corollary 3.8]
has shown a converse to Lemma
4.1
, i.e. if
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(U,g)
and
r_{s}>0
everywhere in
U
then
M
has bounded index on compact subsets.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
M
be a
C^{2}
embedded minimal hypersurface in
(U,g)
, for
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
open and
g
a
C^{2}
metric on
U
. Suppose
\mathrm{index}(M,U,g)<\infty
and
\mathcal{H}^{6}(U\cap\overline{M}\setminus M)=0
. Then
r_{s}>0
on
U
, and (hence)
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(U,g)
.

Proof.

If
x\in M
, then this follows trivially by the Poincare inequality in
B_{r}(x)\cap M
, for
r
small. Suppose
x\in\overline{M}\setminus M
but
r_{s}(x)=0
. Then by our hypothesis and
[
Wic14
]
(as in our discussion above), we must have
\mathrm{index}(M,B_{r}(x),g)>0
for every
r>0
small. We can therefore find a
C^{1}_{c}
functions
u_{r}:M\cap B_{r}(x)\to M^{\perp_{g}}
with
Q_{M,g}(u,u)<0
and
\mathrm{spt}u_{r}\cap\overline{M}\setminus M=\emptyset
. But then for any such
r
we can find an
r^{\prime}>0
with
\mathrm{spt}u_{r}\cap B_{r^{\prime}}(x)=\emptyset
, and thereby construct an infinite-dimensional subspace
V\subset C^{1}_{c}(M,M^{\perp_{g}})
on which
Q_{M,g}
is strictly negative definite. This is a contradiction, so we must have
r_{s}(x)>0
.
∎

It will be convenient to use the notion of varifold. Recall that if
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
, then an integral
7
-varifold
V
in
(U,g)
is a Radon measure on the Grassmanian bundle
G_{7}(TU)\equiv U\times\mathrm{Gr}(7,8)
that takes the form

(15)

V(\phi(x,S))=\int_{M_{V}}\phi(x,T_{x}M_{V})\theta_{V}(x)d\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}(x)

for some countably
7
-rectifiable set
M_{V}
, and some positive, integer-valued
\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}
-measurable function
\theta_{V}
. The mass measure
\mu_{V}=\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}\llcorner\theta_{V}\llcorner M_{V}
is the pushforward of
V
under the projection map
G_{7}(TU)\to U
. We write
\mathrm{spt}V=\mathrm{spt}\mu_{V}
, and define
\mathrm{reg}V
to be the set of points
x\in\mathrm{spt}V
having the property that for some
r>0
,
\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{r}(x)
is a closed (as sets) embedded hypersurface of
B_{r}(x)
. We define the closed set
\mathrm{sing}V=\mathrm{spt}V\setminus\mathrm{reg}V
. If
V_{i}
,
V
are
7
-varifolds in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
and
V_{i}\to V
as varifolds, we say
V_{i}\to V
in
C^{k}(U)
if for some
U^{\prime}\supset U
and for
i>>1
, the
\mathrm{spt}V_{i}\cap U^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{spt}V\cap U^{\prime}
are
C^{k}
embedded hypersurfaces, and
\mathrm{spt}V_{i}\to\mathrm{spt}V
in
C^{k}(U)
.

If
M
is a
C^{1}
hypersurface of
U
, then
M
induces a natural varifold
[M]_{g}
by taking in (
15
)
M_{V}=M
and
\theta_{V}=1
. Write
[M]\equiv[M]_{g_{\mathrm{eucl}}}
. Given any
C^{1}
, proper map
f:(U,g)\to(U^{\prime},g^{\prime})
, we define the pushforward
f_{\sharp}V
to be the integral varifold
f_{\sharp}V
defined by

(f_{\sharp}V)(\phi(x,S))=\int\phi(f(x),Df|_{x}S)J_{S}f(x)dV(x,S),

where, given any
g|_{x}
-ON basis
e_{i}
for
S
,
J_{S}(x)=\det(g^{\prime}|_{f(x)}(Df|_{x}(e_{i}),Df|_{x}(e_{j}))^{1/2}
. Equivalently, by the area formula, in the notation of (
15
)
M_{f_{\sharp}V}=f(M_{V})
and
\theta_{f_{\sharp}V}(x)=\sum_{y\in\theta^{-1}(x)}\theta_{V}(y)
. Similarly,
f_{\sharp}[M]_{g}=[f(M)]_{g^{\prime}}
.

V
is stationary in
(U,g)
if the first variation
\delta_{g}V
vanishes, i.e. if for all
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U
and
X\in C^{1}_{c}(U^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{8})
, we have

(16)

0=\delta_{g}V(X):=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mu_{(Id+tX)_{\sharp}V}(U^{\prime})=\int_{U^{\prime}}\mathrm{div}_{S,g}(X)dV(x,S),

where
\mathrm{div}_{S,g}(X)=\sum_{i}g(e_{i},\nabla^{g}_{e_{i}}X)
for any
g|_{x}
-ON basis of
S
. If
M
is a minimal surface in
(U,g)
, then
[M]_{g}
is stationary in
(U,g)
.

Let
V
be a stationary integral varifold in
(B_{1},g)
, and suppose
g
is a
C^{2}
metric satisfying
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|+|Dg|\leq\Gamma|x|
. Then by (
15
), (
16
) we have the inequality

\displaystyle|\delta_{g_{\mathrm{eucl}}}V(X)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int\mathrm{div}_{T_{x}M_{V},g_{\mathrm{eucl}}}(X)d\mu_{V}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\Gamma\int|x||DX|+|X|d\mu_{V}(x)\quad\forall X\in C^{1}_{c}(B_{1},\mathbb{R}^{8}),

for
c
an absolute constant. Therefore by plugging in
X=\phi(|x|/\rho)x
for
\phi
being (a suitable smooth approximation for)
1_{(-\infty,1]}
, we obtain the monotonicity

(17)

\int_{A_{r,s}}\frac{|\pi^{\perp}_{V}(x)|^{2}}{|x|^{7+2}}d\mu_{V}(x)\leq(1+c\Gamma r)^{7+1}\theta_{V}(0,r)-(1+c\Gamma s)^{7+1}\theta_{V}(0,s).

for all
0<s\leq r\leq 1
, for
c
an absolute constant, and for
\theta_{V}(x,r)=\omega_{7}^{-1}r^{-7}\mu_{V}(B_{r}(x))
the density ratio of
V
. Here
\pi^{\perp}_{V}(x)
is the
Euclidean
projection of the position vector
x
onto the
Euclidean
normal direction
T^{\perp}_{x}M_{V}
. Of course if
g=g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
, then (
17
) reduces to the usual sharp monotonicity formula. We note also that, since
X
is smooth near
0
and
X(0)=0
, (
17
) continues to hold if
V
is only stationary in
(B_{1}\setminus\{0\},g)
, provided
\mu_{V}(B_{1}\setminus\{0\})<\infty
, in which case
V
extends to a stationary integral varifold in
(B_{1},g)
.

Suppose now only
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{2}(B_{1})}\leq\delta
. For each
x\in B_{1}
and
r<(1-\delta)(1-|x|)
we can find a
C^{2}
coordinte transformation
z=\phi_{x}(y)
in
B_{r}(x)
, so that the
(z^{i})
form a choice of normal coordinates of
g
at
x
. Applying the previous paragraph in this coordinate chart, and using (
10
), (
12
), we obtain the monotonicity

(18)

\theta_{V}(x,s)\leq(1+c_{0}\delta r)\theta_{V}(x,r)\quad\forall x\in B_{1},0<s<r<(1-\delta)r,

where
c_{0}
is an absolute constant. (
18
) implies that
\theta_{V}(x):=\lim_{r\to 0}\theta_{V}(x,r)
exists, is upper-semi-continuous, and (since
V
is integral)
\geq 1
on
\mathrm{spt}V
. Moreover, given any
\sigma<1
and
x\in\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{\sigma}
, we have the Ahlfors regularity

(19)

\frac{1}{2}\leq\theta_{V}(x,r)\leq\frac{1+c_{0}}{(1-\sigma)^{n}}\mu_{V}(B_{1}),\quad 0<r<\min\{1-|x|,\frac{1}{c_{0}\delta}\}.

We define
\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(U,g)
to be the class of stationary integral
7
-varifolds
V
in
(U,g)
with the property that
\mathrm{sing}V
is a discrete set of points. Every such
V
satisfies
\mathrm{reg}V\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(U,g)
, and more generally has the following structure.

Lemma 4.2
.

A varifold
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(U,g)
if and only if for every
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U
there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
so that we can write

(20)

V\llcorner U^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}[M_{i}]_{g}

for
m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
M_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(U^{\prime},g)
, and with
\overline{M_{i}}
being disjoint.

Proof.

Let us write
\mathrm{reg}V\cap U^{\prime}=\cup_{i}M_{i}
for each
M_{i}
being a connected, disjoint,
C^{2}
, embedded hypersurface of
U^{\prime}
. Since
\cup_{i}(\overline{M_{i}}\setminus M_{i})\subset\overline{\mathrm{reg}V}\setminus\mathrm{reg}V=\mathrm{sing}V
, each
\overline{M_{i}}\setminus M_{i}
consists of a discrete set of points. By the constancy theorem (
[
Sim83b
, Chapter 8, Theorem 4.1]
)
\theta_{V}
is locally constant on each
M_{i}
, and hence
\theta_{V}|_{M_{i}}=m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
. By stationarity we get that each
M_{i}
is minimal in
(U^{\prime},g)
, and hence
M_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(U^{\prime},g)
. By applying (
19
) to each stationary varifold
[M_{i}]_{g}
, we get that the collection
\{M_{i}\}_{i}
is finite, and by the maximum principle of
[
Ilm96
]
, the
\{\overline{M_{i}}\}_{i}
are disjoint. This proves the “only if” part of the Lemma; the “if” part is trivial.
∎

Remark 4.3

(A note on scaling)
.

Let
B_{R}(a)\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
and
g
be a
C^{2}
metric on
B_{R}(a)
. If
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{R}(a),g)
, and we define
g^{\prime}=g\circ\eta_{a,R}^{-1}
and
V^{\prime}=(\eta_{a,R})_{\sharp}V
where we view
\eta_{a,R}
as a map
(B_{R}(a),g)\to(B_{1}(0),g^{\prime})
(i.e. so
not
an isometry), then
V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{1}(0),g^{\prime})
, and
\mu_{V^{\prime}}(U)=R^{-n}\mu_{V}(\eta_{a,R}^{-1}(U))
for all
U\subset B_{1}
. We shall always interpret the rescaling
(\eta_{a,R})_{\sharp}V
in this fashion. Of course if
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{R}(a),g)
, then
(\eta_{a,R})_{\sharp}[M]_{g}=[\eta_{a,R}(M)]_{g^{\prime}}
.

There is good compactness theory for stationary integral varifolds, but for our purposes we will make use of the following compactness theory for
\mathcal{ISV}_{7}
. Given
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(U,g)
we define
\mathrm{index}(V,U,g):=\mathrm{index}(\mathrm{reg}V,U,g)
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
g_{i},g
be
C^{2}
metrics on
U
, such that
g_{i}\to g
in
C^{2}(U)
. Let
V_{i}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(U,g_{i})
be a sequence satisfying

(21)

\sup_{i}\mu_{V_{i}}(U)<\infty,\quad\mathrm{index}(V_{i},U,g)\leq I<\infty

for some
I\in\mathbb{N}
.

Then after passing to a subsequence, we can find a
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(U,g)
, and at most
I
points
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathrm{spt}V\cap U
, so that:
V_{i}\to V
as varifolds in
U
;
V_{i}\to V
in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
U\setminus(\mathrm{sing}V\cup\mathcal{I})
;
\mathrm{index}(V,U,g)\leq I
; and
\mathrm{index}(V_{i},B_{r}(y),g_{i})\geq 1
for every
r>0
,
y\in\mathcal{I}
,
i>>1
. Additionally, for any collection of disjoint balls
\{B_{r_{\alpha}}(x_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha}\subset U
, we have

(22)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{index}(V_{i},B_{r_{\alpha}}(x_{\alpha}),g_{i})\leq I\quad\forall i.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}
consist of the points
y\in U
with the property that
\limsup_{i}r_{s}(y,V_{i})=0
. Trivially we have
\mathrm{index}(V_{i},B_{r}(y),g_{i})\geq 1
for every
r>0
,
y\in\mathcal{I}
,
i>>1
. We first show
\#\mathcal{I}\leq I
. Suppose, on the contrary, we can find
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{I+1}\}\subset\mathcal{I}
. Then for
i>>1
, we can find radii
r_{i}
so that
\{B_{r_{i}}(y_{j})\}_{j}\subset B_{1}
are disjoint, and
C^{1}_{c}
functions
u_{ij}:\mathrm{reg}V_{i}\cap B_{r_{i}}(y_{j})\to\mathrm{reg}V_{i}^{\perp_{g_{i}}}
so that
Q_{\mathrm{reg}V_{i},g_{i}}(u_{ij},u_{ij})<0
. These functions are trivially linearly-independent, and form a strictly-negative-definite subspace for
Q_{\mathrm{reg}V_{i},g_{i}}
, and hence
\mathrm{index}(V_{i},U,g_{i})\geq I+1
. This is a contradiction. An essentially verbatim argument shows (
22
).

Take any
x\in B_{1}\setminus\mathcal{I}
. There is a radius
r=r_{x}>0
so that, after passing to a subsequence,
B_{2r}(x)\subset U
and every
V_{i}
is stable in
(B_{r}(x),g_{i})
. Each
\mathrm{reg}V_{i}\cap B_{r}(x)
is orientable (being a set-theoretically-closed embedded hypersurface in the simply-connected region
B_{r}(x)\setminus\mathrm{sing}V_{i}
), and so by
[
SS81
, Theorems 1 and 2]
, (
11
), and
C^{0}
convergence
g_{i}\to g
, after passing to a subsequence we can find a
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{r}(x),g)
so that
[\mathrm{spt}V_{i}\cap B_{r}(x)]_{g_{i}}\to V
as varifolds in
B_{r}(x)
, and
\mathrm{spt}V_{i}\to\mathrm{spt}V
in
C^{1,\alpha}
on compact subsets of
B_{r}(x)\setminus\mathrm{sing}V
for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Passing to a further subsequence, (
14
) and our
C^{2}
convergence
g_{i}\to g
imply that
\mathrm{spt}V_{i}\to\mathrm{spt}V
in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
B_{r}(x)\setminus\mathrm{sing}V
. By (
20
) and (
19
), we can pass to yet a further subsequence, and modify the multiplicities of
V
, so that
V_{i}\to V
as varifolds in
B_{r}(x)
.

The previous paragraph implies that there is an
V_{x}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{r}(x),g)
so that
V_{i}\to V_{x}
as varifolds in
B_{r}(x)
, and
V_{i}\to V_{x}
in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
B_{r}(x)\setminus\mathrm{sing}V_{x}
. Now by considering a countable subcover of
\{B_{r_{x}}(x)\}_{x\in U\setminus\mathcal{I}}
, and applying a diagonalization argument, we can obtain an
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(U\setminus\mathcal{I},g)
so that
V_{i}\to V
as varifolds in
U\setminus\mathcal{I}
, and in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
U\setminus(\mathrm{sing}V\cup\mathcal{I})
. Lower-semi-continuity of mass implies
\mu_{V}(U\setminus\mathcal{I})<\infty
, so by (
17
)
V
extends to a stationary integral varifold in
(U,g)
, and
V_{i}\to V
as varifolds in
U
.

We show
\mathrm{index}(V,U,g)\leq I
, which will by Lemma
4.1
imply
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(U,g)
. Suppose, otherwise: there is a linear subspace
V\subset C^{1}_{c}(\mathrm{reg}V,\mathrm{reg}V^{\perp_{g}})
of dimension
I+1
, so that
Q_{\mathrm{reg}V,g}(u,u)<0
for all
u\in V
. Let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{I+1}
be a basis for
V
. Then there is a
q<0
so that

(23)

Q_{\mathrm{reg}V,g}(\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}u_{j},\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}u_{j})\leq q<0

for all collections
\lambda_{j}
satisfying
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}^{2}=1
.

Take
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a fixed, smooth, increasing function satisfying

\eta|_{(-\infty,1/4]}\equiv 0,\quad\eta|_{[3/4,\infty)}\equiv 1,\quad|\eta^{\prime}|\leq 10,

and then for
\sigma>0
define
\phi_{\sigma}(x)=\eta(\sigma^{-1}\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{I}))
, and
u_{j,\sigma}=\phi_{\sigma}u_{j}
. Given any
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U\setminus\mathcal{I}
, then
u_{i,\sigma}=u_{i}
on
U^{\prime}
for
\sigma
sufficiently small. Since
\mathcal{I}
is discrete (codimension
7
), (
19
) implies
Q_{\mathrm{reg}V,g}(u_{j,\sigma},u_{k,\sigma})\to Q_{\mathrm{reg}V,g}(u_{j},u_{k})
as
\sigma\to 0
, for any
j,k
. We deduce (after taking
\sigma
sufficiently small, and replacing
q
in (
23
) with
q/2
), that it suffices to assume that the
u_{i}
are supported away from
\mathcal{I}
.

Now
C^{2}
convergence
V_{i}\to V
implies that we can find open sets
\mathrm{spt}u_{j}\subset W_{j}\subset U\setminus(\mathcal{I}\cup\mathrm{sing}V)
, and
C^{2}
functions
w_{ij}:W_{j}\cap\mathrm{reg}V\to\mathrm{reg}V^{\perp_{g}}
such that

\mathrm{reg}V_{i}\cap W_{j}\supset\mathrm{graph}_{\mathrm{reg}V}(w_{ij})\cap W_{j}=:G_{ij}

and
|w_{ij}|_{C^{2}}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
. Define
u_{ij}\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathrm{reg}V_{i},V_{i}^{\perp_{g_{i}}})
by setting
u_{ij}(y=x+w_{ij}(x))=\pi_{G_{ij}}^{\perp_{g_{i}}}(u_{j}(x))
for
y\in G_{ij}
, and
u_{ij}=0
else. Here
\pi^{\perp_{g_{i}}}_{G_{ij}}|_{y}
denotes the
g_{i}
-orthogonal projection to the normal space
T_{y}^{\perp_{g_{i}}}G_{ij}
.

Since
\mathrm{index}(V_{i},U,g)\leq I
, and the
u_{ij}
are linearly independent for
i>>1
, we can find numbers
\lambda_{ij}
so that
Q_{\mathrm{reg}V_{i},g_{i}}(\sum_{j}\lambda_{ij}u_{ij},\sum_{j}\lambda_{ij}u_{j})\geq 0
, and (WLOG)
\sum_{j}\lambda_{ij}^{2}=1
. Passing to a subsequence, we get
\lambda_{ij}\to\lambda_{j}
for each
j
, and hence by
C^{2}
convergence
V_{i}\to V
,
g_{i}\to g
, we get

Q_{\mathrm{reg}V_{i},g_{i}}(\sum_{j}\lambda_{ij}u_{ij},\sum_{j}\lambda_{ij}u_{ij})\to Q_{\mathrm{reg}V,g}(\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}u_{j},\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}u_{j})\geq 0.

However this contradictions (
23
). We must therefore have
\mathrm{index}(V,U,g)\leq I
.
∎

5.
Stable cones

In this section we work in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
. We let
\mathcal{C}
be the collection of stable minimal hypercones
\mathbf{C}^{7}
in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
which are smooth, closed (as sets), embedded hypersurfaces away from
0
. By
[
SS81
]
,
[
Wic14
]
, if
\mathbf{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
is a smooth, stable, embedded hypersurface and
\mathcal{H}^{6}(\overline{\mathbf{C}}\setminus\mathbf{C})=0
, then
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
. Note that each link
\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}
is necessarily orientable (as a closed, embedded, codimension-one submanifold of a simply-connected manifold) and connected (by the maximum principle, or more generally by Frankel’s theorem).

Given
\Lambda>0
, let
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{C}
denote the collection of
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
with
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\Lambda
. Given
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
, define
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
to be the collection of
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
with property that
\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, and there is a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
\phi:\partial B_{1}\to\partial B_{1}
such that
\phi(\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1})=\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap\partial B_{1}
. Of course the condition
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
is both symmetric and transitive.

Given
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
and function
u:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{r,s}(0)\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}
, let us define the conical graph

G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap A_{r,s}(0)=\left\{\frac{x+u(x)}{|x+u(x)|}|x|:x\in\mathbf{C}\cap A_{r,s}(0)\right\}.

If
a\in\mathbb{R}^{8}
and
u
is defined instead on
(a+\mathbf{C})\cap A_{r,s}(a)
, we define

G_{a+\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap A_{r,s}(a)=a+G_{\mathbf{C}}(u(a+\cdot))\cap A_{r,s}(0).

When
|u|_{C^{1}}
is small this notion of graph is effectively equivalent to the “usual” graph
\mathrm{graph}_{a+\mathbf{C}}(u)
, but is more convenient to work with in the conical setting. Similarly, if
\Sigma
is a
C^{2}
closed embedded hypersurface in
\partial B_{1}
, and
v:\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\perp}
, then let us write

G_{\Sigma}(v)=\left\{\frac{\theta+v(\theta)}{\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}}:\theta\in\Sigma\right\}

for the spherical graph

The key facts we will use about
\mathcal{C}
are listed below.

Theorem 5.1
.

The following are true.

(1)

Given any sequence
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
with
\sup_{i}\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(0)<\infty
, there is a subsequence
i^{\prime}
and cone
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
so that
\mathbf{C}_{i^{\prime}}\cap\partial B_{1}
converges to
\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}
smoothly with multiplicity-one, and moreover so that
\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{i^{\prime}}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
for all
i^{\prime}
.

(2)

The set of densities
\{\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0):\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}\}
forms a discrete set
1=\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\ldots
.

(3)

Given
\Lambda>0
, there is an
\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda)>0
so that if
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
satisfy

d_{H}(\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap\partial B_{1})\leq\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0},

then
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
, and in fact we can write

\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=G_{\mathbf{C}}(v),\quad|x|^{1-k}|\nabla^{k}v|\leq c(\Lambda,k)\epsilon

for a smooth,
1
-homogenous function
v:\mathbf{C}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}
.

(4)

Additionally, for
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
we have
r_{3}(\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1})\geq\epsilon_{0}
.

Proof.

The main ingredient is the Lojasiewicz-Simon inequality of
[
Sim83a
]
. Let
\Sigma
be any smooth, closed, embedded minimal submanifold of
S^{7}
. Then
[
Sim83a
, Theorem 3]
implies there is a
\sigma(\Sigma,\mu)>0
and
\theta(\Sigma)\in(0,1/2)
so that given any
C^{2,\mu}
function
u:\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\perp}
(for
\mu\in(0,1)
) with
|u|_{C^{2,\mu}(\Sigma)}\leq\sigma
we have the inequality

|\mathcal{H}^{6}(G_{\Sigma}(u))-\mathcal{H}^{6}(G_{\Sigma}(0))|^{1-\theta}\leq||\mathcal{M}(u)||_{L^{2}(\Sigma)},

where
\mathcal{M}(u)
is the negative
L^{2}(\Sigma)
-gradient of
u\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{6}(G_{\Sigma}(u))
(see Appendix
11
). In particular,
G_{\Sigma}(u)
is minimal in
S^{7}
precisely when
\mathcal{M}(u)=0
, in which case we have
\mathcal{H}^{6}(G_{\Sigma}(u))=\mathcal{H}^{6}(\Sigma)
. Of course minimality of
\Sigma
in
S^{7}
is equivalent to minimality of the cone over
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
.

The secondary ingredient is smooth, multiplicity-one compactness for
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
. Let us first note that every smooth, closed, minimal
6
-submanifold in
S^{7}
is necessarily connected and orientable. Given a sequence
\mathbf{C}_{i}
as in the Theorem, then by the usual varifold compactness, sheeting, and dimension reducing argument (e.g.
[
SS81
, Theorem 2]
or Lemma
4.4
) we can find a
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
so that
\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{C}
smoothly with multiplicity
m
away from
0
. However since each
\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap\partial B_{1}
,
\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}
is connected while
\partial B_{1}=S^{7}
is simply-connected, we must have
m=1
. Theorem
5.1
follows in an obvious way from these two ingredients.
∎

We require some auxilary Lemmas. Our first shows how the sequence compactness of Theorem
5.1
implies a topological compactness as well.

Lemma 5.2
.

Take
\Lambda>0
, and a mapping
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}\mapsto\delta_{\mathbf{C}}\in(0,\infty)
assigning to every cone in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
a positive “radius.” Then we can find a finite collection
\mathbf{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{C}_{N}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, so that for every
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
we have

d_{H}(\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1},\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap\partial B_{1})<\delta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}

for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be the space of stationary integral
6
-varifolds in
S^{7}
(stationary with respect to the spherical metric), having total mass
\leq 2\Lambda
, and let this space have the topology induced by varifold convergence. The usual compactness theorem for stationary varifolds
[
Sim83b
, Chapter 8, Theorem 5.8]
implies
\mathcal{V}
is sequentially compact, and if we include
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
into
\mathcal{V}
by identifying
\mathbf{C}
with the varifold
[\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}]
, then by Theorem
5.1

\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
is closed in
\mathcal{V}
.

Define the metric
d
on
\mathcal{V}
as follows: fix a countably-dense subcollection
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}\subset\{\phi\in C^{0}(B_{2}):|\phi|_{C^{0}(B_{2})}\leq 1\}
, and then given
V,W\in\mathcal{V}
set

(24)

d(V,W)=\sum_{i}2^{-i}\left|\int\phi d\mu_{V}-\int\phi d\mu_{W}\right|.

Since any
V\in\mathcal{V}
is uniquely determined by its mass measure
\mu_{V}
, since
\mathcal{V}
is sequentially compact, and since every
V\in\mathcal{V}
has
\mu_{V}(S^{7})\leq 2\Lambda
, it’s straightfoward to verify that the varifold topology on
\mathcal{V}
coincides with the topology induced by
d
. Moreover, the monotonicity formula and sequential compactness imply: given any
\epsilon>0
, there is a
\delta>0
so that for any
V,W\in\mathcal{V}
:

(25)

d(V,W)<\delta\quad\implies\quad d_{H}(\mathrm{spt}V,\mathrm{spt}W)<\epsilon.

A converse relation holds if
V,W\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{V}
.

By (
25
) we can find an
\epsilon_{\mathbf{C}}>0
for each
\mathbf{C}
so that

(26)

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{C}}

\displaystyle:=\{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}:d([\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}],[\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap\partial B_{1}])<\epsilon_{\mathbf{C}}\}

(27)

\displaystyle\subset\{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}:d_{H}(\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap\partial B_{1})<\delta_{\mathbf{C}}\}

(of course
\mathrm{spt}[\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}]=\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}
since
\mathbf{C}
is smooth away from
0
). So the
U_{\mathbf{C}}
form an open cover of
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{V}
. Since compactness and sequential compactness are equivalent for a metric space, and
\mathbf{C}_{\Lambda}
is closed in
\mathcal{V}
, we can find a finite subcollection
U_{\mathbf{C}_{1}},\ldots,U_{\mathbf{C}_{N}}
which covers
\mathbf{C}_{\Lambda}
. The Lemma then follows by (
25
).
∎

The second is a quantification of the fact that none of the cones in
\mathbf{C}
have any translational symmetry.

Lemma 5.3
.

Given
\epsilon>0
,
\Lambda>0
there is a
\beta(\Lambda,\epsilon)
so that if
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, and
a\in\mathbb{R}^{8}
satisfy

d_{H}((a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap A_{1,1/2},\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2})\leq\beta,

then
|a|\leq\epsilon
,
d_{H}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1})\leq\epsilon
, and
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)
. If
\mathbf{C}
is planar, then we require
a\in{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Suppose, towards a a contradiction, the lemma failed: there was a sequence
\mathbf{C}_{i},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{l}
,
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{8}
, so that

(28)

d_{H}((a_{i}+\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime})\cap A_{1,1/2},\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap A_{1,1/2})\to 0,

but either
|a_{i}|>\epsilon
,
d_{H}(\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\cap B_{1})>\epsilon
, or
\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(0)\neq\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(0)

Passing to a subsequence, as in Theorem
5.1
we get smooth, multiplicity-one convergence
\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
for
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
. By Theorem
5.1
,
\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
and
\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)
for
i>>1
. In particular, the
\mathbf{C}_{i}
are planar iff
\mathbf{C}
is planar.

Assume first
\mathbf{C}
is non-planar. If we had
\limsup_{i}|a_{i}|=\infty
, then after passing to a subsequence Theorem
5.1
implies
a_{i}+\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}
converges in
C^{2}
to a plane on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, which implies by (
28
) that
\mathbf{C}_{i}
converges to a plane also, which is a contradiction. So we can assume
a_{i}\to a
also. From (
28
) we get
a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}
, which implies
a=0
since
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(x)<\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
for
x\neq 0
. We therefore have
a=0
,
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
, which is a contradiction.

Assume
\mathbf{C}
is a plane. We have

(29)

\displaystyle|a_{i}|

\displaystyle=d(0,a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

(30)

\displaystyle\leq d_{H}((a_{i}+\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime})\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1})

(31)

\displaystyle\leq cd_{H}((a_{i}+\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime})\cap A_{1,1/2},\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2})\to 0,

so
a_{i}\to 0
. As before we deduce
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}
, which is a contradiction. This proves Lemma
5.3
.
∎

The third says that we can regraph over nearby cones.

Lemma 5.4
.

Given
\Lambda>0
, there is a
\gamma(\Lambda)
so that if
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{8}
,
0\leq\tau,\beta\leq\gamma
are such that

|a|\leq\tau,\quad d_{H}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap\partial B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1})\leq\tau,

and
v:(a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap A_{1,1/2}(a)\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\perp}
is a
C^{2}
function satisfying
|v|_{C^{2}}\leq\beta
then we can write

\left(G_{a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(v)\cap A_{1,1/2}(a)\right)\cap A_{1-a,1/2+a}(0)=G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap A_{1-a,1/2+a}(0),

for
u:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1-a,1/2+a}(0)\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}
a
C^{2}
function satisfying
|u|_{C^{2}}\leq c(\Lambda)(\beta+\tau)
.

Proof.

Follows by Theorem
5.1
:(
3
),(
4
) and the inverse function theorem (c.f. Section
8
).
∎

6.
Cone regions

Here we define various notions of “cone region,” and prove a key smallness estimate in Theorem
6.3
. Ultimately, we will only be interested in strong-cone regions of hypersurfaces, but we will use cone regions, weak-cone regions, and their varifold formulations as intermediary constructions. Since we want to keep track of multiplicity it is most useful to phrase these in terms of varifolds.

Definition 6.0.1

(Weak cone region)
.

Let
g
be a
C^{2}
metric on
B_{R}(a)\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
, and
V
an integral varifold in
(B_{R}(a),g)
. Take
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\beta,\tau,\sigma\in[0,1/4]
. We say
V\llcorner(A_{R,\rho}(a),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta,\tau,\sigma)
-weak-cone region
1
1

1

Let us emphasize that, although for ease of notation we write
A_{R,\rho}(a)
, the actual annulus in which
V
resembles a cone will be some perturbation of
A_{R,\rho}(a)
contained in
A_{R,\rho}(a)
.
if the following occurs: For every
r\in[\rho,(1-\sigma)R)\cap(0,\infty)
, there is an
a_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{8}
with
B_{r}(a_{r})\subset B_{R}(a)
, a cone
\mathbf{C}_{r}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
, and
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{r,j}:(a_{r}+\mathbf{C}_{r})\cap A_{r,r/8}(a_{r})\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}_{r}\}_{j=1}^{m}
satisfying

(32)

r^{-1}|u_{r,j}|+|\nabla u_{r,j}|+r|\nabla^{2}u_{r,j}|\leq\beta,\quad j=1,\ldots,m,

so that
V
is a multi-graph:

(33)
