V\llcorner A_{r,r/8}(a_{r})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{a_{r}+\mathbf{C}_{r}}(u_{r,j})\cap A_{r,r/8}(a_{r})]_{g},

and the density ratios are close to constant:

(34)

m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)-\beta\leq\theta_{V}(a_{r},r)\leq m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)+\beta.

Additionally, we ask that for all
\rho\leq r\leq s\leq\min\{2r,(1-\sigma)R\}
we have

(35)

|a_{r}-a_{s}|\leq\tau s,\quad d_{H}(\mathbf{C}_{r}\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}_{s}\cap B_{1})\leq\tau.

When there is ambiguity we may be explicit and write
a_{r}=a_{r}(V)
,
\mathbf{C}_{r}=\mathbf{C}_{r}(V)
to specify the varifold in question.

Provided
\beta
,
\tau
,
\sigma
are sufficiently small, then after shrinking our radius and enlarging
\beta
, every weak-cone region is actually a “cone region.”

Definition 6.0.2

(Cone region)
.

In the notation of the above, we say
V\llcorner(A_{R,\rho}(a),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-cone region if for every
r\in[\rho,R]\cap(0,\infty)
there is as
\mathbf{C}_{r}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
and
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{r,j}:(a+\mathbf{C}_{r})\cap A_{r,r/8}(a)\to\mathbf{C}_{r}^{\perp}\}_{j=1}^{m}
so that (
32
), (
33
), (
34
) hold with
a_{r}\equiv a
.

A much more non-trivial theorem, and the main theorem of this section, says that provided
\beta
is sufficiently small, every cone region is a “strong-cone” region (for some larger choice of
\beta
).

Definition 6.0.3

(Strong cone region)
.

In the notation of the above, we say
V\llcorner(A_{R,\rho}(a),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-strong-cone region if there are
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{j}:(a+\mathbf{C})\cap A_{R,\rho/8}(a)\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}\}_{j=1}^{m}
so that (
32
), (
33
), (
34
) hold for every
r\in[\rho,R]\cap(0,\infty)
with
a_{r}\equiv a
,
\mathbf{C}_{r}\equiv\mathbf{C}
, and
u_{r,j}\equiv u_{j}
.

It will be convenient to also define cone regions for hypersurfaces:

Definition 6.0.4

(Cone regions for
M
)
.

If
M
is a
C^{2}
hypersurface in
B_{R}(a)
, then in the notation of the above we say
M\cap(A_{R,\rho}(a),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-(strong-)cone region if
[M]_{g}\llcorner(A_{R,\rho}(a),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-(strong-)cone region.

A few remarks. If
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{R}(a),g)
, and
V\cap(A_{R,\rho}(a),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-cone region, then
V
is regular in
A_{R,\rho}(a)
by the maximum principle and the fact that
\dim(\mathrm{sing}V)=0
, and any two graphs in (
33
) either coincide or are disjoint. If
\rho=0
, then the maximum principle due to
[
Ilm96
]
implies
V=m[\mathrm{spt}V]_{g}
in
B_{R}(a)
, and
[\mathrm{spt}V]_{g}\cap(A_{R,0}(a),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},1,\beta)
-cone region. (
33
) is just saying that
\mathrm{spt}V\cap A_{r,r/8}(a_{r})
splits into at most
m
conical graphs, but for various reasons it is useful to keep track of the multiplicity of these graphs.

The following lemma proves that weak-cone regions are effectively cone regions, after recentering/rescaling, and makes explicit that weak-cone regions can be recentered, and that extending the recentered region to smaller inner radii is equivalent to extending the original cone region. These last facts are important in our contradiction argument later on.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
V\llcorner(A_{R,\rho}(a),g)
be a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta,\tau,\sigma)
-weak-cone region, for
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
. Assume
\sigma\in[0,1/4]
,
\tau\leq\sigma/10
. Then:

(1)

We have
|a_{r}-a_{s}|\leq 5\tau\max\{s,r\}
for all
r,s\in[\rho,(1-\sigma)R]
. Hence, the point

a_{\rho}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}a_{\rho}&\rho>0\\
\lim_{r\to 0}a_{r}&\rho=0\end{array}\right.

exists, and satisfies
|a_{\rho}-a|\leq 2\sigma R
.

(2)

Take
R^{\prime}\leq(1-2\sigma)R
. Given any
r\in[\rho,(1-\sigma)R^{\prime}]
, and
\rho^{\prime}\leq r
, then
V\llcorner(A_{R^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}}(a_{r}),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta,\tau,\sigma)
-weak-cone region
\iff

V\llcorner(A_{R,\rho^{\prime}}(a),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta,\tau,\sigma)
-weak-cone region. In particular,
V\llcorner(A_{R^{\prime},\rho}(a_{\rho}),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta,\tau,\sigma)
-weak-cone region.

(3)

There is a
\gamma(\Lambda)>0
so that if, additionally,
\beta,\tau\leq\gamma
, then
V\llcorner A_{(1-3\sigma)R,\rho}(a_{\rho})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,c(\Lambda,m)(\beta+\tau))
-cone region.

Proof.

Item
1
follows easily from (
35
) and (
36
). We prove Item
2
. To prove the direction
\impliedby
if will suffice to show that
B_{R^{\prime}}(a_{r})\subset B_{R}(a)
, and that for every
s\in[\rho^{\prime},(1-\sigma)R^{\prime}]
we have
B_{s}(a_{s})\subset B_{R^{\prime}}(a_{r})
. The first inclusion follows because

(36)

|a-a_{r}|\leq|a-a_{(1-\sigma)R}|+|a_{(1-\sigma)R}-a_{r}|\leq\sigma R+5\tau(1-\sigma)R\leq 2\sigma R.

To prove the second inclusion, we observe for any
s
as above that

s+|a_{s}-a_{r}|\leq s+5\tau\max\{s,r\}\leq(1+5\tau)(1-\sigma)R^{\prime}<R^{\prime}.

The direction
\implies
is trivial if
\rho^{\prime}\geq\rho
. If
\rho^{\prime}<\rho
, then it suffices to verify that
B_{s}(a_{s})\subset B_{R}(a)
for
s\in[\rho^{\prime},\rho]
. But by assumption and (
36
) we have

B_{s}(a_{s})\subset B_{R^{\prime}}(a_{r})\subset B_{R}(a).

This proves Item
2
.

To prove Item
3
, we first observe that by Item
1
, (
36
) and our assumption
\tau\leq\sigma/10
, we get for every
r\in[\rho,(1-3\sigma)R]\cap(0,\infty)
:

(37)

\displaystyle B_{r}(a_{\rho})\subset B_{(1+10\tau)r}(a_{(1+10\tau)r})\subset B_{R}(a),

(38)

\displaystyle B_{r}(a_{\rho})\supset B_{(1-5\tau)r}(a_{(1-5\tau)r})\quad\text{ if }(1-5\tau)r\geq\rho,

(39)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad(1+10\tau)r\leq(1-\sigma)R.

Therefore, ensuring
\tau(\Lambda),\beta(\Lambda)
are sufficiently small, for every such
r
we can apply Lemma
5.4
in each annulus
A_{r,r/2}(a_{\rho})
to get (
33
), (
32
).

To get the upper density bound (
34
), we can use (
37
), (
39
) to get for
r
as above:

\displaystyle\theta_{V}(a_{\rho},r)\leq(1+10\tau)^{7}\theta_{V}(a_{(1+10\tau)r},(1+10\tau)r)\leq m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)+c(\Lambda,m)(\tau+\beta).

The lower bound is similar, using (
38
):

\displaystyle\theta_{V}(a_{\rho},r)

\displaystyle\geq(1-5\tau)^{7}\theta_{V}(a_{\max\{(1-5\tau)r,\rho\}},\max\{(1-5\tau)r,\rho\})

\displaystyle\geq m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)-c(\Lambda,m)(\beta+\tau).

This proves Item
3
.
∎

We now work towards proving Theorem
6.3
, which is a smallness estimate that implies cone regions are in fact strong-cone regions. We first require a helper lemma which implies that small graphicality “propogates” to nearby scales.

Lemma 6.2
.

Given
\epsilon,\Lambda>0
, there is a
\gamma(\Lambda)
so that the following holds. Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
g
be a
C^{2}
metric on
B_{1}
, and
V
an integral varifold in
(B_{1},g)
. Suppose
V\llcorner(A_{1,1/2},g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-cone region for
\beta\leq\gamma
, and

V\llcorner A_{1,1/2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{1,1/2}]_{g},\quad|u_{j}|_{C^{2}}\leq\epsilon\leq\gamma.

Then

V\llcorner A_{1,1/16}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{1,1/16}]_{g},\quad|u_{j}|_{C^{2}}\leq c(\Lambda)(\epsilon+\beta).

Proof.

Follows from the definition of cone region and Lemma
5.4
.
∎

Theorem 6.3
.

Given
\epsilon,m,\Lambda>0
, there are
\beta(\Lambda,m,\epsilon)
,
\delta(\Lambda,m,\epsilon)
so that the following holds. Let
g
be a
C^{3}
metric on
B_{1}
such that
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\beta
. Take
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho\in[0,1]
, and let
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{1},g)
be such that
V\llcorner(A_{1,\rho},g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-cone region. Suppose there are
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{j}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2}\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}\}_{j}
such that

(40)

V\llcorner A_{1,1/2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{1,1/2}]_{g},\quad|u_{j}|_{C^{2}(\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2})}\leq\delta.

Then the
u_{j}
can be extended to
C^{2}
functions on
\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,\rho/8}
, so that

(41)

V\llcorner A_{1,\rho/8}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{1,\rho/8}]_{g},\quad|x|^{-1}|u_{j}|+|\nabla u_{j}|+|x||\nabla^{2}u_{j}|\leq\epsilon.

In particular,
V\cap(A_{1,\rho},g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\epsilon)
-strong-cone region.

If
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=1
then we additionally have the curvature estimate

(42)

\int_{A_{1,\rho/8}}|A_{V,g}|^{7}d\mu_{V}(x)\leq\epsilon

where
|A_{V,g}|
is the length of the second fundamental form of
\mathrm{reg}V\subset(B_{1},g)
.

Remark 6.4
.

If
V
is stable in
(B_{1},g)
, then in place of assuming that
V\llcorner(A_{1,\rho},g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-cone region, one can instead assume that

(43)

\theta_{V}(0,1)\leq m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)+\beta,\quad\theta_{V}(0,\rho/16)\geq m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)-\beta.

Compare to Theorem
13.1
, Lemma
13.2
.

Remark 6.5
.

In place of assuming (
34
) in Theorem
6.3
one could instead assume directly a bound of the form

(44)

\int_{A_{1,\rho/8}}|x|^{-9}|\pi_{V}^{\perp}(x)|^{2}d\mu_{V}(x)\leq\beta.

In this case it would suffice to know
V
is only defined and stationary in the annulus
A_{1,\rho/8}
.

Remark 6.6
.

By Remark
11.2
(and considering the change of variables
t=\log r
) the same conclusion in Theorem
6.3
holds if instead of (
40
) we assume there is a
\rho^{\prime}\in[\rho/4,1]
so that

V\llcorner A_{\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}/2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}/2}]_{g}

for some
C^{2}
functions
u_{j}
satisfying
\rho^{\prime-1}|u_{j}|+|\nabla u_{j}|+\rho^{\prime}|\nabla^{2}u_{j}|\leq\delta
.

Remark 6.7
.

If
g(0)=g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
and
Dg|_{0}=0
then we additionally get the Dini-type estimate

(45)

\int_{A_{1,\rho/8}}|x|^{-8}|\pi_{V}^{\perp}(x)|d\mu_{V}(x)\leq\epsilon,

which can be viewed as a quantitative version of the statement that tangent cones are unique. Here (like in (
17
))
\pi^{\perp}_{V}|_{x}
is the Euclidean orthogonal projection onto the Euclidean normal space
T_{x}^{\perp}V
.

One can recast (
45
) as a
C^{0}
-Dini-type estimate in the following fashion. In analogue to the planar Jones’
\beta
-numbers, let us define the “conical
\beta
-numbers”
\beta_{\mathbf{C}}(V,a,r)
by

\beta_{\mathbf{C}}(V,a,r)=\inf_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})}r^{-1}d_{H}(\mathrm{spt}V\cap A_{r,r/8}(a),(a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap A_{r,r/8}(a)).

With
\beta
,
V
as in Theorem
6.3
, there is a
\gamma(\mathbf{C})\geq 2
so that if
V\llcorner(A_{1,\rho},g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},1,\beta)
-strong-cone region, then
V
satisfies the estimate

(46)

\int_{\rho}^{1}\beta_{\mathbf{C}}(V,0,r)^{\gamma}\frac{dr}{r}\leq\epsilon.

Here
\gamma
is the
C^{0}
-Lojacsiewicz exponent (
[
Sim83a
, (2.1)]
), and if
\mathbf{C}
is integrable then one can take
\gamma=2
. In particular, if
\mathbf{C}
is planar then

(47)

\int_{A_{1,\rho/8}}|x|^{-5}|A_{V,g}|^{2}d\mu_{V}(x)\leq\epsilon,

which is a “deeper” reason for estimate (
42
). If
m\geq 2
, then by the maximum principle
V\llcorner A_{1,\rho/8}
splits into
m
varifolds
V_{j}
, each of which satisfies (
46
).

Remark 6.8
.

By using sharper linear estimates, it probably suffices to assume
g
is only
C^{2}
close to Euclidean (see
[
Sim84
]
).

Proof.

I claim that it suffices to prove Theorem
6.3
with
\delta
depending on
(\mathbf{C},m,\epsilon)
. Let us assume we can do this, and show how to get
\delta
to depend only on
(\Lambda,m,\epsilon)
. Let
c_{2}(\Lambda)
,
\gamma_{2}(\Lambda)
be the constants from Lemma
5.4
, and
\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda)
the constant from Theorem
5.1
. For each
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, apply (by assumption) Theorem
6.3
with
\epsilon/(10c_{2})
in place of
\epsilon
, to obtain a
\delta_{\mathbf{C}}(\mathbf{C},m,\epsilon)
and
\beta_{\mathbf{C}}(\mathbf{C},m,\epsilon)
. WLOG of course we can assume
\delta_{\mathbf{C}}\leq\min(\epsilon,\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{0})
. Now we can apply Lemma
5.2
to obtain a finite collection
\mathbf{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{C}_{\Lambda}
, so that given any
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, we can find an
i
so that

(48)

d_{H}(\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1},\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap\partial B_{1})<\delta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}/(10c_{2})

Set
\delta=\min_{i=1,\ldots,N}\delta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}/(10c_{2})
,
\beta=\min_{i=1,\ldots,N}\beta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}
.

Now take a general
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, and suppose (
40
) holds with this
\mathbf{C}
and
\beta,\delta
as chosen above. Pick
\mathbf{C}_{i}
so that (
48
) holds, and then by Lemma
5.4
we can write

V\llcorner A_{1,1/2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\tilde{u}_{j})\cap A_{1,1/2}]_{g},\quad|\tilde{u}_{j}|_{C^{2}}\leq 2c_{2}\delta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}/(10c_{2})\leq\delta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}.

Moreover, by Theorem
5.1
we get that
V\llcorner(A_{1,\rho},g)
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{i},m,\beta_{\mathbf{C}})
-cone region. By our choice of constants we can apply Theorem
6.3
to deduce

V\llcorner A_{1,\rho/8}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(\tilde{u}_{j})\cap A_{1,\rho/8}]_{g},\quad|x|^{-1}|\tilde{u}_{j}|+|\nabla\tilde{u}_{j}|+|x||\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}_{j}|\leq\epsilon/(10c_{2}),

and then finally apply Lemma
5.4
at each scale
r\in[\rho/4,1]
to get (
41
). This proves
\delta
can be made independent of
\mathbf{C}
.

I next claim that it suffices to prove Theorem
6.3
under the assumption that
g(0)=g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
,
Dg|_{0}=0
. We prove this. Let
V
,
\mathbf{C}
,
g
satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
6.3
. By taking
\phi^{-1}
to be a quadratic perturbation of a linear map, we can find a smooth diffeomorphism
\phi:B_{1/2}\to B_{1}
satisfying

(49)

|\phi(x)-x|\leq c\beta|x|,\quad|D\phi-Id|+|D^{2}\phi|\leq c\beta,

so that the pullback metric
g^{\prime}=\phi^{*}g
satisfies

g^{\prime}(0)=g_{\mathrm{eucl}},\quad Dg^{\prime}|_{0}=0,\quad|g^{\prime}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1/2})}\leq c\beta,

where
c
is an absolute constant.

From Theorem
5.1
, (
49
), and the inverse function theorem, ensuring
\beta(\Lambda)
is sufficiently small, if
V^{\prime}=(\phi^{-1})_{\sharp}V\llcorner B_{1/2}
then
V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{1/2},g^{\prime})
,
V^{\prime}\llcorner(A_{1/2,2\rho},g^{\prime})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,c(\Lambda)\beta)
-cone region, and (using Lemma
6.2
) we can find
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{j}^{\prime}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1/2,1/4}\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}\}_{j=1}^{m}
so that

V^{\prime}\llcorner A_{1/2,1/4}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j}^{\prime})\cap A_{1/2,1/4}]_{g^{\prime}},\quad|u_{j}^{\prime}|_{C^{2}(\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1/2,1/4})}\leq c(\Lambda)(\delta+\beta).

For any
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, ensuring
\beta(\Lambda,m,\epsilon^{\prime})
,
\delta(\Lambda,m,\epsilon^{\prime})
are sufficiently small, we can apply Theorem
6.3
to the rescaled varifold/metric
(\eta_{0,1/2})_{\sharp}V^{\prime}
,
g^{\prime}\circ\eta_{0,1/2}^{-1}
to deduce

V^{\prime}\llcorner A_{1/2,\rho/4}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j}^{\prime})\cap A_{1/2,\rho/4}]_{g^{\prime}},\quad|x|^{-1}|u_{j}^{\prime}|+|\nabla u_{j}^{\prime}|+|x||\nabla^{2}u_{j}^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

We can again apply the inverse function theorem and Lemma
6.2
to deduce (
41
) with
c(\Lambda)(\epsilon^{\prime}+\beta)
in place of
\epsilon
. Provided
\epsilon^{\prime}(\Lambda,\epsilon)
,
\beta(\Lambda,\epsilon)
are sufficiently small, this proves (
41
).

If
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=1
, then since
\phi
is an isometry we obtain from the previous paragraph

\int_{A_{1/4,\rho/2}}|A_{V,g}|^{7}d\mu_{V}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

(
42
) then follows by (
32
), provided
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon/2
and
\beta(m,\epsilon)
is sufficiently small. This proves my second reduction.

We now prove Theorem
6.3
with
\mathbf{C}
fixed, and
\delta
,
\beta\leq\delta
depending on
(\mathbf{C},m,\epsilon)
, and under the assumption that
g(0)=g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
,
Dg|_{0}=0
. Note this implies that

(50)

|g|_{x}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|\leq\beta|x|^{2},\quad|Dg|_{x}|\leq\beta|x|.

We note also that there is no loss in assuming
\epsilon(\mathbf{C})
is as small as we like. Let
\rho_{*}
be the least radius for which we have

(51)

V\llcorner A_{1,\rho_{*}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{1,\rho_{*}}]_{g},\quad|x|^{-1}|u_{j}|+|\nabla u_{j}|+|x||\nabla^{2}u_{j}|\leq\epsilon.

By Lemma
6.2
, ensuring
\delta(\Lambda,\epsilon,m)
,
\beta(\Lambda,\epsilon,m)
are sufficiently small, we can assume
\rho_{*}\leq\max(e^{-6},\rho/8)
. Note also that Lemma
6.2
and our restriction
\beta\leq\delta
imply we can assume

(52)

|u_{j}|_{C^{2}(\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,e^{-2}})}\leq c(\Lambda)\delta.

We will suppose
\rho_{*}>\rho/8
, and derive a contradiction. By the maximum principle and our singular set bound we can assume the graphs
G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{1,\rho_{*}}
are disjoint, and in particular each graph
G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{1,\rho_{*}}
is stationary in
(A_{1,\rho_{*}},g)
. By this and by Lemma
6.2
, it will suffice to show that for any
0<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon
of our choosing, we can pick any one
u=u_{j}
and get the bound

(53)

|x|^{-1}|u|+|\nabla u|+|x||\nabla^{2}u|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\quad x\in\mathbf{C}\cap A_{e^{-1},e^{3}\rho_{*}},

provided
\delta(\mathbf{C},m,\epsilon^{\prime})
,
\beta(\mathbf{C},m,\epsilon^{\prime})
are sufficiently small. Our strategy is to use the decay-growth Theorem
11.1
, to show small bounds on
u
must persist as long as the density bound (
40
) holds. We will use notation from
[
Sim83a
]
; see also Appendix
11
.

Let
\Sigma=\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1}
, and write
\hat{\nabla}
for the connection on
T^{\perp}\Sigma\subset TS^{7}
. Write
r=|x|
, and define the change of variables
t=-\log r
,
v(t,\theta)=r^{-1}u(r\theta)
, so that
v
is a
C^{2}
function on
\Sigma\times(0,T_{*})
for
T_{*}=-\log\rho_{*}
. Write
\dot{v}\equiv\partial_{t}v
. Recall the norms
|v|_{k}^{*}(t)
(defined in Section
11
), and
||v(t)||=||v(t,\cdot)||_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
. We have

(54)

\frac{1}{c}|v|_{2}^{*}(t)\leq\sup_{x\in e^{-t}\Sigma}|x|^{-1}|u|+|\nabla u|+|x||\nabla^{2}u|\leq c|v|_{2}^{*}(t)

for every
t
, and some absolute constant
c
. Define the function
G:\Sigma\times(\rho_{*},1)\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
by setting

G(\theta,r)=\frac{r\theta+u(r\theta)}{\sqrt{1+r^{-1}|u(r\theta)|^{2}}}\equiv\frac{r\theta+rv}{\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}}

Provided
\epsilon(\Sigma)
is sufficiently small, then for any
0\leq a<b\leq T_{*}
we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{a,b}(v)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g}(G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap A_{e^{-b},e^{-a}})

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\int_{\Sigma}e^{-7t}(F(\theta,v,\hat{\nabla}v,\dot{v})+H(\theta,t,v,\hat{\nabla}v,\dot{v}))d\theta dt,

where the integrand is really just the Jacobian of the map
(\theta,t)\mapsto G(\theta,e^{-t})
w.r.t. to the metric
g
, and
e^{-7t}F
is the Jacobian w.r.t.
g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
. By embedding
T^{\perp}\Sigma,T\Sigma\subset T\mathbb{R}^{8}
, one can think of
F(\theta,z,p,q)
, resp.
H(\theta,t,z,p,q)
, as being defined on
\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}^{8}\times\mathbb{R}^{64}\times\mathbb{R}^{8}
, resp.
\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{8}\times\mathbb{R}^{64}\times\mathbb{R}^{8}
(c.f.
[
Sim84
]
).

F
,
H
satisfy the following properties:
F(\theta,z,p,q)
is smooth (and analytic in
z,p,q
) and independent of
g
, and takes the form

F(\theta,z,p,q)^{2}=(1+|q|^{2}+q^{2}\cdot z^{2}\cdot F_{1}(z))E(\theta,z,p)^{2}+q^{2}\cdot p^{2}\cdot F_{2}(\theta,z,p),

for
F_{1},F_{2}
smooth, and
E(\theta,z,p)\equiv F(\theta,z,p,0)
; the functional

\mathcal{E}(v(t)):=\int_{\Sigma}E(\theta,v,\hat{\nabla}v)\equiv e^{6t}\mathcal{H}^{6}(G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap\partial B_{e^{-t}})

satisfies the convexity and analyticity hypotheses of
[
Sim83a
, (1.2), (1.3)]
, and has the form

E(\theta,z,p)^{2}=1+z^{2}\cdot E_{1}(\theta,z,p)+p^{2}\cdot E_{2}(\theta,z,p)

for smooth functions
E_{1},E_{2}
;
H(\theta,t,z,p,q)
is
C^{3}
in the
(\theta,t,z)
variables, smooth in the
(p,q)
variables, and from (
50
)
H
satisfies the bounds

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq 3\\
0\leq i+j\leq 1\end{subarray}}|D^{k}D_{p}^{i}D_{q}^{j}H|\leq c(\Sigma)\beta e^{-2t},

here
D
being the full derivative on
\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{8}\times\mathbb{R}^{64}\times\mathbb{R}^{8}
. In the above we are abusing notation slightly: when we write
F_{2}
(e.g.) we really mean a collection of functions
F_{2ijkl}^{\alpha\beta}(\theta,z,p)
defined on
\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}^{8}\times\mathbb{R}^{64}
so that

q^{2}\cdot p^{2}\cdot F_{2}(\theta,z,p)\equiv\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j,k,l=1,\ldots,8\\
\alpha,\beta=1,\ldots,8\end{subarray}}q^{i}q^{j}p^{k}_{\alpha}p^{l}_{\beta}F_{2ijkl}^{\alpha\beta}(\theta,z,p)

A straightforward computation then shows that stationarity of
v
for the area functional
\mathcal{F}_{a,b}
implies that
v
solves a PDE of the form

(55)

\displaystyle-\ddot{v}+7\dot{v}-\mathcal{M}(v)-\mathcal{R}_{1}(v)-\mathcal{R}_{2}(v)=f

where, as in Section
11
,
\mathcal{M}=-\mathrm{grad}\mathcal{E}
in the
L^{2}(\Sigma)
sense;
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is independent of
g
and has the form of Section
11
:Item
2
; and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
,
f
are as in Section
11
:Items
1
,
3
, except with
c(\Sigma)\beta e^{-2t}
in place of
\delta e^{-\epsilon t}
.

As exploited in
[
Sim83a
]
, for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
provided
\beta(\Sigma,\delta^{\prime})
and
\epsilon(\Sigma,\delta^{\prime})
are sufficiently small then both
v
and
w=\dot{v}
solves an PDE of the form

(56)

\displaystyle-\ddot{w}+7\dot{w}-Lw+e_{1}\cdot\hat{\nabla}^{2}w+e_{2}\cdot\hat{\nabla}\dot{w}+e_{3}\ddot{w}+e_{4}\cdot\hat{\nabla}w+e_{5}\dot{w}+e_{6}w=f^{\prime}

for
e_{i}(x,t)
,
f^{\prime}
being
C^{1}
functions (different for
v
and
w
) having the estimates
|e_{i}|_{1}^{*}(t)\leq\delta^{\prime}
,
|f^{\prime}|_{1}^{*}(t)\leq c(\Sigma)\beta e^{-2t}
, and
L
being the (elliptic) linearization of
\mathcal{M}
at
0
. In fact, for us
L=\hat{\Delta}_{\Sigma}+|A_{\Sigma}|^{2}+6
where
A_{\Sigma}
is the second fundamental form of
\Sigma\subset S^{7}
, and
\hat{\Delta}_{\Sigma}
the connection Laplacian on
T^{\perp}\Sigma
. Ensuring
\delta^{\prime}(\Sigma)
is small, we can apply standard elliptic theory to get

(57)

\displaystyle|v|_{2}(t)^{*}\leq c(\Sigma)(||v||_{L^{1}(\Sigma\times(t-1,t+1))}+\beta e^{-2t})\quad t\in[1,T_{*}-2],

and the same for
\dot{v}
.

The key that allows us to apply Theorem
11.1
to
v
is the “Hardt-Simon” type inequality (c.f.
[
Sim93
]
): provided
\epsilon(\Sigma)
is sufficiently small, then we have

(58)

|\dot{v}(t,\theta)|^{2}\leq 2e^{2t}|\pi^{\perp}_{V}(G(\theta,e^{-t}))|^{2}\leq 4|\dot{v}(t,\theta)|^{2},\quad\forall t\in(0,T_{*}),\theta\in\Sigma,

where
\pi^{\perp}_{V}
is the Euclidean projection onto the Euclidean normal direction
T^{\perp}_{G(\theta,r)}V
. This follows by first observing that
r\mapsto G(r,\theta)
is a curve in
\mathrm{reg}V
, and hence

-\frac{\pi^{\perp}_{V}(r\partial_{r}v)}{\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}}=(1-(r\partial_{r}v)\cdot(G/r))\pi^{\perp}_{V}(G/r),

and then second observing that we have the bound
|\pi^{\perp}V-\pi^{\perp}_{T_{r\theta}\mathbf{C}}|\leq c(\Sigma)\epsilon
.

Using the monotonicity (
17
), (
50
), our hypothesis on
g
, and observing that the Jacobian of
(r,\theta)\mapsto G(\theta,r)
is
\geq(1-c(\Sigma)(\epsilon+\beta))r^{6}
, we can use (
58
) to estimate:

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{s}||\dot{v}(t)||^{2}dt

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{e^{-s}}^{e^{-t}}\int_{\Sigma}r^{-3}|\pi^{\perp}_{V}(G(r,\theta))|^{2}d\theta dr

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{A_{e^{-s},e^{-t}}\cap V}\frac{|\pi^{\perp}_{V}(x)|^{2}}{|x|^{7+2}}d\mu_{V}(x)

\displaystyle\leq c(\Lambda)\beta,

provided
\epsilon(\Sigma),\beta(\Sigma)
are sufficiently small, and
0<s<t\leq T_{*}-3

We deduce that, for
s,t
as above, we have

(59)

||v(t)-v(s)||\leq\int_{s}^{t}||\dot{v}(t)||dt\leq c(\Lambda)\beta^{1/2}|t-s|^{1/2}.

Therefore, combining (
59
) with estimates (
57
), we obtain for any
s,t\in[1,T_{*}-3]
the bounds

\displaystyle|v|_{2}^{*}(t)

\displaystyle\leq c(\Sigma)(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in(t-1,t+1)}||v(t)||+\beta)

(60)

\displaystyle\leq c(\Sigma)|v|_{2}^{*}(s)+c(\Sigma)\beta^{1/2}\max\{1,|t-s|\},

and hence
v
has
(c(\Sigma),c(\Sigma)\beta^{1/2})
-linear growth on
\Sigma\times[1,T_{*}-3]
.

On the other hand, we can use (
55
), the
C^{1}
Lojasiewicz-Simon inequality for
\mathcal{E}

[
Sim83a
, (2.2)]
, and (
57
) to get

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}(v(t))-\mathcal{E}(0)|^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\leq||\mathcal{M}(v(t))||

\displaystyle\leq c(\Sigma)|\dot{v}|_{1}^{*}(t)+c(\Sigma)\beta e^{-2t}

\displaystyle\leq c(\Sigma)||\dot{v}||_{L^{2}(\Sigma\times(t-1,t+1))}+c(\Sigma)\beta e^{-2t}

(61)

\displaystyle\leq c(\Sigma)\beta^{1/2},

for any
t\in[1,T_{*}-3]
. Here
\theta(\Sigma)\in(0,1/2)
is the Lojasiewicz exponent as in
[
Sim83a
, Theorem 3]
.

Our hypothesis (
52
), and (
60
), (
61
), imply that provided
\delta(\Sigma)
,
\beta(\delta,\Sigma)
are sufficiently small, we can apply Theorem
11.1
to deduce that

(62)

|v|_{2}^{*}(t)\leq c(\Sigma)\delta^{\alpha}\quad\forall t\in[1,T_{*}-3],

for some
\alpha(\Sigma)\in(0,1/2)
. In particular, by (
54
), ensuring
\delta(\Sigma,\epsilon^{\prime})
is small, we deduce (
53
). From Theorem
11.1
we also have the bound

(63)

\int_{1}^{T_{*}-3}||\dot{v}(t)||dt\leq c(\Sigma)\delta^{\alpha/2}

which together with (
62
), (
58
) implies (
45
).

Using a similar computation to (
61
), the
C^{0}
Lojasiewicz-Simon inequality
[
Sim83a
, (2.1)]
implies there is a
\gamma(\Sigma)\geq 2
, so that for every
t\in[1,T_{*}-3]
we can find a
\zeta_{t}\in C^{2}(\Sigma,\Sigma^{\perp})
solving
\mathcal{M}(\zeta_{t})=0
so that

\displaystyle||v(t)-\zeta_{t}||^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq 2||\mathcal{M}(v(t))||

\displaystyle\leq c(\Sigma)||\dot{v}||_{L^{1}(\Sigma\times(t-1,t+1))}+c(\Sigma)\beta e^{-2t}

\displaystyle\leq c(\Sigma)\int_{t-1}^{t+1}||\dot{v}(t)||dt+c(\Sigma)\beta e^{-2t}.

Here
\gamma
is the Lojasiewicz exponent, and we note that if
\mathbf{C}
is “‘integrable” then one can take
\gamma=2
(see e.g.
[
Sim96
, Page 80]
).

If
2\leq i\leq T_{*}-7
, then by (
59
), the above, and (
62
) (since
\gamma\geq 2
), we get

\sup_{t\in[i,i+3]}||v(t)-\zeta_{i}||^{\gamma}\leq c(\Sigma)\int_{i-1}^{i+4}||\dot{v}(t)||dt+c(\Sigma)\beta e^{-2t}.

Setting
\zeta_{i}=0
if
i\leq 2
or
i>T_{*}-7
, we deduce there are numbers
\{\epsilon_{i}\}_{i}
so that every integer
i\in(0,T_{*})
and for every
t\in[0,T_{*})\cap[i-3,i]
, we have

(64)

\displaystyle||v(t)-\zeta_{i}||^{\gamma}\leq\epsilon_{i},\quad\sum_{i}\epsilon_{i}\leq c(\Sigma)\delta^{\alpha/2}.

When
\mathbf{C}
is planar, this in particular implies there are planes
\mathbf{C}_{i}
so that

\left(e^{i}d_{H}(G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap A_{e^{-i},e^{-i-3}},\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap A_{e^{-i},e^{-i-3}})\right)^{2}\leq\epsilon_{i},

for
i,\epsilon_{i}
as in (
64
). Hence, ensuring
\beta
is sufficiently small, by (
14
) we deduce that for every
r\in[2\rho,1/2]
we have the bound

\displaystyle\sup_{G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap A_{r,r/8}}r^{2}|A|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|^{2}_{C^{0}(B_{2r})}+cr^{2}|Dg|^{2}_{C^{1}(B_{2r})}+c\epsilon_{i}

\displaystyle\leq c\beta r^{2}+c\epsilon_{i},

for
c
an absolute constant, and
A
be the second fundamental form of
G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\subset(B_{1},g)
. Together with (
64
), (
19
), this implies

\int_{A_{1/2,\rho/4}}|x|^{-5}|A|^{2}d\mu_{V}\leq c(m)(\beta+\delta^{\alpha/2}),

which combined with (
32
) and Theorem
5.1
gives (
47
), (
42
) provided
\delta(m,\epsilon),\beta(m,\epsilon)
are chosen sufficiently small.
∎

7.
Decomposition

Here is the core of our argument. We show that a minimal surface lying sufficiently close to a stable minimal cone with multiplicity must break up into a “cone decomposition,” consisting of a controlled number of “smooth regions” (multi-graphs over a smooth model surface) and a controlled number of “strong cone regions” (multi-graphs over cones). We already defined cone regions in the previous section. We first define our smooth models/regions and cone decompositions. In this section we work in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
.

A smooth model is an entire minimal hypersurface-with-multiplicity
S
in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
along with a choice of disjoint balls, so that
S
is entirely smooth outside of these balls. The multiplicities of
S
specify the number of graphs we expect to see over each component. Each hole more or less corresponds to a would-be singularity or point of index concentration.

Definition 7.0.1

(Smooth model)
.

Given
\Lambda,\gamma\geq 0
,
\sigma\in(0,1/3)
, we say that
(S,\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}))\}_{\alpha})
is a
(\Lambda,\sigma,\gamma)
-smooth model if
S\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
and satisifes
\theta_{S}(0,\infty)\leq\Lambda
,
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
\{B_{2r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha}
is a finite collection of disjoint balls in
B_{1-3\sigma}
, and
\{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}
is a corresponding finite collection of cones in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, such that the following is satisfied:

(1)

There is a decomposition

(65)

S=m_{1}[S_{1}]+\ldots+m_{k}[S_{k}],

where
m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
S_{i}
are smooth, disjoint, closed (as sets), embedded minimal hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}y_{\alpha}
.

(2)

There is an
m\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\Lambda
so that
S\llcorner(A_{\infty,1},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\gamma)
-strong-cone region.

(3)

For each
\alpha
, there is a
j
so that
\mathrm{spt}S\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},0}(y_{\alpha})=S_{j}\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},0}(y_{\alpha})
and
S_{j}\cap(A_{2r_{\alpha},0}(y_{\alpha}),g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},1,\gamma)
-strong-cone region.

Often we will refer to the smooth model as simply
S
, where the existence of the associated
\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}))\}_{\alpha}
is implicitly understood.

Up to scaling, any
S\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
with
\theta_{S}(0,\infty)\leq\Lambda
and
\mathrm{index}(S,\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})<\infty
is a smooth model for some choice of balls
\{B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha}
. In some sense the main content of this definition is the choice of balls, as pinning down a scale by which to measure
S
, which is made precise in the following definition.

Definition 7.0.2
.

Given a smooth
(\Lambda,\sigma,\gamma)
-model
S
, we let
\epsilon_{S}
be the largest number
\leq\min\{1,\min_{\alpha}\{r_{\alpha}\}\}
for which the map
G_{S}:T^{\perp}(\cup_{j}S_{j})\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
defined by
G_{S}(x,v)=x+v
is a diffeomorphism from
\{(x,v)\in T^{\perp}(\cup_{j}S_{j}):x\in B_{2}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}/8}(y_{\alpha}),|v|<2\epsilon_{S}\}
onto its image, and satisfies
|DG_{S}|_{(x,t)}-Id|\leq\epsilon_{S}^{-1}|t|
. Note that
\epsilon_{S}
is always positive.

A smooth region is a multi-graph over
S
, with
C^{2}
norm small at the scale of
\epsilon_{S}
.

Definition 7.0.3

(Smooth region)
.

Given a smooth model
S
,
g
a
C^{2}
metric on
B_{R}(a)\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
, and
V
an integral varifold in
(B_{R}(a),g)
, we say
V\llcorner(B_{R}(a),g)
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region if for each
i=1,\ldots,k
there are
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{ij}:S_{i}\to S_{i}^{\perp}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}
so that

\left((\eta_{a,R})_{\sharp}V\right)\llcorner B_{1}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}/4}(y_{\alpha})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}[\mathrm{graph}_{S_{i}}(u_{ij})\cap B_{1}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}/4}(y_{\alpha})]_{g\circ\eta_{a,R}^{-1}},

and

|u_{ij}|_{C^{2}(S_{i})}\leq\beta\epsilon_{S},\quad\forall i,j.

The “global” structure of one of our minimal surfaces/stationary varifolds is captured by a strong-cone decomposition, which is essentially a finite collection of strong-cone regions and smooth regions which fit together to cover all of
B_{R}(x)
. The total number of regions is controlled, and the smooth models can only come from some (finite) predefined pool of models
\mathcal{S}
. Similar structures have appeared in many other contexts, sometimes called bubble trees (e.g. for harmonic maps
[
Par96
]
) or neck decompositions (e.g. for limits of spaces with bounded Ricci
[
JN21
]
).

Definition 7.0.4

(Cone decomposition)
.

Take
\theta,\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma\in(0,1/3)
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
g
be a
C^{2}
metric on
B_{R}(x)\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
,
V
be an integral varifold in
(B_{R}(x),g)
, and let
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{s}\}_{s}
be a finite collection of
(\theta,\sigma,\gamma)
-smooth models. A
(\theta,\beta,\mathcal{S},N)
-cone decomposition of
V\llcorner(B_{R}(x),g)
consists of integers
N_{S},N_{C}
, satisfying
N_{C}+N_{S}\leq N
, points
\{x_{a}\}_{a},\{x_{b}\}_{b}\subset B_{R}(x)
, radii
\{R_{a}\geq 2\rho_{a}\}_{a}
,
\{R_{b}\}_{b}
, indices
\{s_{b}\}_{b}
, integers
\{1\leq m_{a}\leq[\theta]\}_{a}
, and cones
\{\mathbf{C}_{a}\}_{a}\subset\mathcal{C}
, where
a=1,\ldots,N_{C}
,
b=1,\ldots,N_{S}
, such that:

(1)

Every
V\llcorner(B_{R_{b}}(x_{b}),g)
is a
(S_{s_{b}},\beta)
-smooth region, and every
V\llcorner(A_{R_{a},\rho_{a}}(x_{a}),g)
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{a},m_{a},\beta)
-strong-cone region.

(2)

There is either a smooth region
B_{R_{b}}(x_{b})
with
R_{b}=R
,
x_{b}=x
, or a strong-cone region
A_{R_{a},\rho_{a}}(x_{a})
with
R_{a}=R
,
x_{a}=x
.

(3)

If
B_{R_{b}}(x_{b})
is a smooth region, with associated smooth model
(S,\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}))\}_{\alpha})\in\mathcal{S}
, then for each
\alpha
there we can find a radius
R_{b,\alpha}
and point
x_{b,\alpha}
satisfying

|x_{b,\alpha}-y_{\alpha}|\leq\beta R_{b}r_{\alpha},\quad\frac{1}{2}\leq\frac{R_{b,\alpha}}{R_{b}r_{\alpha}}\leq 1+\beta,

so that there is either a strong-cone region
A_{R_{a},\rho_{a}}(x_{a})
with
R_{a}=R_{b,\alpha}
,
x_{a}=x_{b,\alpha}
, or another smooth region
B_{R_{b^{\prime}}}(x_{b^{\prime}})
with
R_{b^{\prime}}=R_{b,\alpha}
,
x_{b^{\prime}}=x_{b,\alpha}
.

(4)

If
A_{R_{a},\rho_{a}}(x_{a})
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{a},m_{a},\beta)
-strong-cone region, and
\rho_{a}>0
, then there is either a smooth region
B_{R_{b}}(x_{b})
with
R_{b}=\rho_{a}
,
x_{b}=x_{a}
, or another cone region
A_{R_{a^{\prime}},\rho_{a^{\prime}}}(x_{a^{\prime}})
with
R_{a^{\prime}}=\rho_{a}
,
x_{a^{\prime}}=x_{a}
. If
\rho_{a}=0
, then
\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{a}}(0)>1
.

Later on we will be primarily interested in cone decompositions for hypersurfaces. We define them in terms of the associated (multiplicity-one) varifold.

Definition 7.0.5

(Smooth region/Cone decomposition for
M
)
.

Given a
C^{2}
hypersurface
M\subset B_{R}(a)
, and
g
a
C^{2}
metric on
B_{R}(a)
, we say
M\cap(B_{R}(x),g)
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region or admits a
(\theta,\beta,\mathcal{S},N)
-cone decomposition if
[M]_{g}\llcorner(B_{R}(a),g)
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region or admits a
(\theta,\beta,\mathcal{S},N)
-cone decomposition (respectively).

Recall that Theorem
5.1
gives an enumeration of
\{\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0):\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}\}=\{\theta_{i}\}_{i}
. Let us enumerate the set of densities-with-multiplicity as
\{m\theta_{i}:m\in\mathbb{N},i=0,1,\ldots\}=\{\tilde{\theta}_{i}\}_{i}
, for
1=\tilde{\theta}_{0}<\tilde{\theta}_{1}<\ldots
. We prove our decomposition theorem
(\dagger_{l,I})
by induction on both the density-with-multplicity
\tilde{\theta}_{l}
and index
I
.

Theorem 7.1

(
\dagger_{l,I}
)
.

Given
l,I\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<\beta\leq\gamma\leq 1
,
\sigma\in(0,\frac{1}{100(I+1)}]
, there are constants
\delta_{l,I}
,
N
, and a finite collection of
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\sigma,\beta)
-smooth models
\{S_{s}\}_{s}=\mathcal{S}
, all depending (only) on
(l,I,\gamma,\beta,\sigma)
, so that the following holds.

Let
g
be a
C^{3}
metric on
B_{1}
satisfying
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\delta_{l,I}
. Take
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{1},g)
with
\mathrm{index}(V,B_{1},g)\leq I
,
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\tilde{\theta}_{l}
. Suppose that

(66)

d_{H}(\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1})\leq\delta_{l,I},

and

(67)

(m-1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\theta_{V}(0,1/2),\quad\theta_{V}(0,1)\leq(m+1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0).

Then there is a radius
r\in(1-20(I+1)\sigma,1)
, so that
V\llcorner(B_{r},g)
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\mathcal{S},N)
-strong-cone decomposition. If
I=0
then one can take
r=1-5\sigma
.

Additionally, each
S_{s}
satisfies
\mathrm{index}(S_{s})\leq I
. If
V
is area-minimizing,
2
2

2

In the sense that
V
is the varifold associated to some mass-minimizing current. This statement is a direct consequence of the nature of the proof, and the compactness for mass-minimizing currents
[
Sim83b
, Chapter 7, Theorem 2.4]
.
then one can assume all the
S_{s}
appearing in its cone decomposition are area-minimizing also.

Remark 7.2
.

We emphasize that
\delta_{l,I}
depends also on
\gamma,\beta,\sigma
. We write only the indices
l,I
as these are the integers we induct over.

Remark 7.3
.

By the curvature estimate (
42
), if all the cones appearing in the cone decomposition of
V
are planar, then
\mathrm{sing}V=\emptyset
and we have the bound

\int_{B_{r}}|A_{V,g}|^{7}d\mu_{V}(x)\leq c(l,\mathcal{S},N).

Remark 7.4
.

We will often use Theorem
7.1
at some different scale
B_{r}(x)
. Let us mention explicitly that if
g
is a
C^{3}
metric on
B_{r}(x)\subset\mathbb{R}^{8}
such that
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{r}(x))}\leq\delta
, then
g\circ\eta_{x,r}^{-1}\equiv g(x+r\cdot)
is a
C^{3}
metric on
B_{1}
satisfying
|g\circ\eta_{x,r}^{-1}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\max\{1,r\}\delta
.

Remark 7.5
.

The cone decomposition constructed by Theorem
7.1
has the additional properties: the outermost region is always a smooth region, and no two cone regions are “stacked” directly inside each other (so you cannot have large annulus consisting of numerous cone regions where
V
looks like a rotating cone).

Proof.

Fix
l,I
. We shall prove
(\dagger_{l,I})
by induction and by contradiction. We shall choose our constants as we go along, but it may help the reader to keep in mind:

\beta^{\prime\prime}<<\tau<<\beta^{\prime}<<\beta\leq\gamma<<\sigma<1.

Note there is no loss in assuming
\beta(l,\sigma)
is as small as we like.

Suppose
(\dagger_{l,I})
fails. Then there are sequences
\delta_{i}\to 0
,
C^{3}
metrics
g_{i}
,
V_{i}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{1},g_{i})
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
,
m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, so that
g_{i}
,
V_{i}
satisfies the hypotheses of
(\dagger_{l,I})
with
\delta_{i}
,
g_{i}
,
m_{i}
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}
in place of
\delta,g,m,\mathbf{C}
, but with the property that for any finite collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\sigma,\beta)
-smooth models, and any
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an
i_{0}
such that
V\llcorner(B_{r},g_{i})
does not admit a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\mathcal{S}^{\prime},N^{\prime})
-strong-cone decomposition for all
i>i_{0}
,
r\in(1-20(I+1)\sigma,1)
.

Passing to a subsequence, by Theorem
5.1
we can assume that
\mathbf{C}_{i}\to\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
smoothly with multiplicity-one away from
0
, and
\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
for all
i
. By Lemmas
4.4
,
4.2
, and our hypotheses, passing to a further subsequence, there are at most
I
points
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1}
so that
V_{i}\to m[\mathbf{C}]
as varifolds in
B_{1}
, in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathrm{sing}\mathbf{C}
. Note that since
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\tilde{\theta}_{l}
, we have
\max\{m,\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\}\leq\tilde{\theta}_{l}
.

We first note that if
l=0
, corresponding to
m[\mathbf{C}]=[\mathbf{C}]
being a multiplicity-one plane, and
I
is arbitrary, then provided
i>>1

[
All72
]
proves implies each
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{1-5\sigma},g_{i})
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region, for
S
being the smooth model
([\mathbf{C}],\mathbf{C},\emptyset)
. Similarly, if
\mathbf{C}
is planar,
I=0
, and
l=m
is arbitrary, then since
\mathrm{reg}V
is stable in
(B_{1},g_{i})

[
SS81
]
implies
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{1-5\sigma},g_{i})
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region for
i>>1
, where
S
is the smooth model
(m[\mathbf{C}],\mathbf{C},\emptyset)
. In either case we get that
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{1-5\sigma},g_{i})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\{S\},1)
-cone decomposition, which is a contradiction.

By our inductive hypothesis we can therefore assume that
(\dagger_{l^{\prime},I})
holds for every
l^{\prime}<l
,
I
arbitrary, and also that
(\dagger_{l,I^{\prime}})
holds for any
I^{\prime}<I
.

We break into three cases.

Case 1:
\mathcal{I}\subset\{0\}
.
For each
i
, let
\rho_{i}
be the smallest number such that
V_{i}\llcorner(A_{1,\rho_{i}}(0),g_{i})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta^{\prime\prime},\tau,\sigma)
-weak-cone region, for
\beta^{\prime\prime},\tau
to be chosen later depending only on
(l,\beta,\gamma)
. Let
a_{i}=a_{\rho_{i}}(V_{i})
be the annulus center at radius
\rho_{i}
(as in Definition
6.0.1
). By our varifold convergence
V_{i}\to m[\mathbf{C}]
, and
C^{2}
convergence on compact subsets of
B_{1}\setminus\{0\}
, we have
a_{i}\to 0
and
\rho_{i}\to 0
.

Ensuring
\beta^{\prime\prime}(l,\beta^{\prime})
,
\tau(l,\beta^{\prime},\sigma)
are sufficiently small, Lemma
6.1
implies each
V_{i}\llcorner(A_{1-3\sigma,\rho_{i}}(a_{i}),g_{i})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta^{\prime})
-cone region. Ensuring
\beta^{\prime}(l,\beta)
is sufficiently small, and
i>>1
, we can apply Theorem
6.3
to deduce that each
V_{i}\llcorner(A_{1-3\sigma,\rho_{i}}(a_{i}),g_{i})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-strong-cone region.

First suppose
\rho_{i}=0
for infinitely-many
i
. For each such
i>>1
, the maximum principle of
[
Ilm96
]
implies
V_{i}\llcorner B_{1-4\sigma}=m[M_{i}]_{g_{i}}
for some
M_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1-4\sigma},g_{i})
. If
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=1
, corresponding to
m[\mathbf{C}]
being a multiplicity-
m
plane, then
[M_{i}]_{g_{i}}\to[\mathbf{C}]
in
B_{1-4\sigma}
and so by
[
All72
]
we deduce
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{1-5\sigma},g_{i})
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region for
S
the smooth model
(m[\mathbf{C}],\mathbf{C},\emptyset)
. So infinitely-many
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{1-5\sigma},g_{i})
admit a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\{S\},1)
-cone decomposition, which is a contradiction.

If
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)>1
, define the smooth model
(S=[\mathbf{C}],\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C},B_{1/10})\})
, and let
r=1-5\sigma
. By
C^{2}
convergence,
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r},g_{i})
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region for
i>>1
. Since
|a_{i}|\leq\beta r/10
and
V_{i}\llcorner(A_{r/10,\rho_{i}}(a_{i}),g_{i})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-strong-cone region for every
i>>1
, we deduce that infinitely-many
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r},g_{i})
admit a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\{S\},2)
-strong-cone decomposition. This is a contradiction.

So we must have
\rho_{i}>0
for all
i>>1
. Define the rescaled varifolds
V_{i}^{\prime}=(\eta_{a_{i},\rho_{i}})_{\sharp}V_{i}
, and rescaled metrics
g_{i}^{\prime}=g_{i}\circ\eta_{a_{i},\rho_{i}}^{-1}
. Then for any
R>2
and
i>>1
,
V_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{R}(0),g_{i}^{\prime})
, and by Lemma
6.1
(
2
) and scale-invariance (Remark
4.3
), we have that

(68)

1=\inf\{\rho:V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(A_{R,\rho}(0),g_{i}^{\prime})\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},m,\beta^{\prime\prime},\tau,\sigma)$-weak-cone region}\}.

Of course we also have that
a_{1}(V_{i}^{\prime})=0
, this being the center of the annular region of
V_{i}^{\prime}
at radius
1
. Moreover, for any
R>2
and
i>>1
, each
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(A_{R,1},g_{i}^{\prime})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-strong-cone region. Finally, note that we continue to have
C^{3}
convergence
g_{i}^{\prime}\to g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
.

Monotonicity (
18
) and the fact
a_{i}\to 0
imply that
\theta_{V_{i}^{\prime}}(0,R)\leq(m+o(1))\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
for any particular
R>0
, where here
o(1)\to 0
as
i\to\infty
. Trivially we also have
\mathrm{index}(V_{i}^{\prime},B_{R},g_{i}^{\prime})\leq I
for every
R>0
. Therefore, by Lemma
4.4
, we can find an
V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
with
\theta_{V^{\prime}}(0,\infty)\leq m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, and at most
I
points
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime}
, so that
V_{i}^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
as varifolds in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
and in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus(\mathrm{sing}V^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
. Provided
\beta(l)
is sufficiently small, Arzela-Ascoli, Theorem
5.1
, and (
14
) imply that
V^{\prime}\llcorner(A_{\infty,1},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-strong-cone region, and
V_{i}^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus\overline{B_{1/8}}
. In particular
\mathrm{sing}V^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subset\overline{B_{1/8}}
is a finite set.

I claim that any tangent cone to
V^{\prime}
at infinity takes the form
m[\mathbf{C}^{\prime}]
for some
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
. To see this, take any
R_{i}\to\infty
, and suppose
(\eta_{0,R_{i}})_{\sharp}V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(\mathbb{R}^{8})
as varifolds. Since (by Lemma
4.4
)
\mathrm{index}(V^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})<\infty
and
V^{\prime\prime}
is a cone,
V^{\prime\prime}
is stable and hence
V^{\prime\prime}=m^{\prime\prime}[\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}]
for some
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
and
m^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
m^{\prime\prime}\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}}(0)\leq m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\tilde{\theta}_{l}
. We can assume that
m^{\prime\prime}[\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}]\llcorner(A_{\infty,0},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-strong-cone region, and hence

d_{H}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1})\leq\beta,\quad m^{\prime\prime}\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}}(0)\geq m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)-\beta.

Ensuring
\beta(l)
is sufficiently small, Theorem
5.1
implies
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
, and hence
\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
and
m^{\prime\prime}=m
. This proves my claim.

We break into two subcases.

Subcase 1A:
\theta_{V^{\prime}}(a)\geq\tilde{\theta}_{l}
for some
a\in\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime}
.
In this case, by the monotonicity formula we must have
V^{\prime}=m[a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}]
for some
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
. If
\mathbf{C}
is planar, there is no loss in assuming
a\in\mathbf{C}^{\perp}
.

Since each
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(A_{4,1}(0),g_{i}^{\prime})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta^{\prime\prime},\tau,\sigma)
-weak-cone region, for every
r\in[1,4]
there are
a_{r,i}\equiv a_{r}(V_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{8}
,
\mathbf{C}_{r,i}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
, and
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{r,i,j}:(a_{r,i}+\mathbf{C}_{r,i})\cap A_{r,r/8}(a_{r,i})\to\mathbf{C}_{r,i}^{\perp}\}_{j=1}^{m}
such that

(69)

V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner A_{r,r/8}(a_{r,i})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{a_{r,i}+\mathbf{C}_{r,i}}(u_{r,i})\cap A_{r,r/8}(a_{r,i})]_{g_{i}^{\prime}},

and

(70)

r^{-1}|u_{r,i,j}|+|\nabla u_{r,i,j}|+r|\nabla^{2}u_{r,i,j}|\leq\beta^{\prime\prime}\quad\forall i,j,r.

Moreover, by construction we have
a_{1,i}=0
.

It follows by (
69
), (
70
), Ahlfors regularity (
19
), and varifold convergence
V_{i}^{\prime}\to[a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}]
, that for every
r\in[1,4]
and
i>>1
, we have

d_{H}((a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap A_{r/2,r/4}(a_{r,i}),(a_{r,i}+\mathbf{C}_{r,i})\cap A_{r/2,r/4}(a_{r,i}))\leq 8\beta^{\prime\prime}.

(Note the
\beta^{\prime\prime}
instead of
\beta^{\prime}
!) By Lemma
5.3
(applied in the ball
B_{r/2}(a_{r,i})
), ensuring
\beta^{\prime\prime}(l,\tau)
is sufficiently small, we deduce that

(71)

|a|\leq 2^{-10}\tau\leq 2^{-4},\quad|a-a_{r,i}|\leq 2^{-10}\tau r,\quad d_{H}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}_{r,i})\leq 2^{-10}\tau,

for each
r\in[1,4]
, and all
i>>1
.

I claim that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\subset\{a\}
if
\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)>1
, and
\#\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\geq 2
if
\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)=1
. Let us prove this. If
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)>1
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subset\{a\}
then set
a^{\prime}=a
and
r^{\prime}=1/2
. If, on the other hand,
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=1
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\{y^{\prime}\}\subset\overline{B_{1/8}}\cap(a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
, then set
a^{\prime}=a+2^{-4}\tau(y^{\prime}-a)
,
r^{\prime}=1-2^{-5}\tau<1
. In this case we get

(72)

|a^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|<r^{\prime}/8,\quad|a^{\prime}-a|\leq 2^{-6}\tau,\quad|a^{\prime}-a_{r,i}|\leq 2^{-5}\tau.

Either way
a^{\prime}+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
.

In either case, by assumption and our choice of
a^{\prime},r^{\prime}
, we can find for all
i>>1

C^{2}
functions
\{u_{i,j}^{\prime}:(a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap A_{1,r^{\prime}/8}(a^{\prime})\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\perp}\}_{j=1}^{m}
with the properties that

(73)

V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner A_{1,r^{\prime}/8}(a^{\prime})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{a^{\prime}+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(u_{i,j}^{\prime})\cap A_{1,r^{\prime}/8}(a^{\prime})]_{g_{i}^{\prime}},

and

(74)

|u_{i,j}^{\prime}|+|\nabla u_{i,j}^{\prime}|+|\nabla^{2}u_{i,j}^{\prime}|\leq\beta^{\prime\prime}/2.

On the other hand, since
V_{i}^{\prime}\to[a^{\prime}+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}]
as varifolds and
\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, we have
|\theta_{V_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},r)-m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)|\leq\beta^{\prime\prime}
for all
i>>1
and
r\in[1/2,4]
. This, together with (
69
), (
70
), (
72
), (
73
), (
74
), implies that
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(A_{4,r^{\prime}}(0),g_{i}^{\prime})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta^{\prime\prime},\tau,\sigma)
-weak-cone region, contradicting (
68
). This proves my claim.

If
\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)>1
, we have
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\subset\{a\}
, and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subset B_{1/4}(a)
, and so we can apply Case 2 of
(\dagger_{l,I})
to the translated/dilated sequences
\eta_{a,1/4}(V_{i}^{\prime})
,
g_{i}^{\prime}\circ\eta_{a,1/4}^{-1}
. We obtain a finite collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\sigma,\beta)
-models, an integer
N^{\prime}
, and a radii
r^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in(\frac{4}{5}\cdot\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{4})
(recall our assumption on
\sigma
), so that every
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(B_{r^{\prime\prime}_{i}}(a),g_{i}^{\prime})
(for
i>>1
) admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\mathcal{S}^{\prime},N^{\prime})
-strong-cone decomposition.

If
\theta_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(0)=1
, we have
\#\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\geq 2
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subset B_{1/4}(a)
, and so we can apply Case 3 of
(\dagger_{l,I})
as in the previous paragraph to deduce the same conclusion.

By Lemma
5.4
and (
71
), for
\tau(\gamma,l)
sufficiently small, we can write

(a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap A_{\infty,1/2}=G_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(u^{\prime})\cap A_{\infty,1/2}

for
u^{\prime}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap A_{\infty,1/2}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\perp}
a
C^{2}
function satisfying
|x|^{-1}|u^{\prime}|+|\nabla u^{\prime}|+|x||\nabla^{2}u^{\prime}|\leq\gamma
. Letting
S^{\prime}=m[a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}]
, then this and our requirement
|a|\leq 1/16
imply
(S^{\prime},\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C},B_{1/4}(a))\})
is a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\sigma,\gamma)
-smooth model.
C^{2}
convergence to
m[a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime}]
means that for
i>>1
, every
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(B_{1},g_{i}^{\prime})
is a
(S^{\prime},\beta)
-smooth region.

Combining the above two paragraphs, we get that every
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(B_{1},g_{i}^{\prime})
, and hence
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{\rho_{i}}(a_{i}),g_{i})
, admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\{S^{\prime}\}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime},1+N^{\prime})
-strong-cone decomposition. If we let
S
be the smooth model as constructed in the
\rho_{i}=0
case, then like we argued before we deduce that
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{1-5\sigma}(0),g_{i})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\{S,S^{\prime}\}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime},3+N^{\prime})
-strong-cone decomposition. This is a contradiction, and completes the proof of Case 1A.

Subcase 1B:
\theta_{V^{\prime}}(x)\leq\tilde{\theta}_{l-1}
for all
x\in\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime}.
Write
\mathrm{sing}V^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\{y_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,y_{d^{\prime}}^{\prime}\}
. By Lemma
4.1
and our subcase hypothesis, for each
\alpha=1,\ldots,d^{\prime}
there is a tangent cone to
V^{\prime}
at
y_{\alpha}^{\prime}
of the form
m_{\alpha}[\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\prime}]
, where
\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
and
m_{\alpha}\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\prime}}(0)\leq\tilde{\theta}_{l-1}
. By Lemmas
4.1
,
4.4
, Theorem
6.3
, Remark
6.4
, and the maximum principle of
[
Ilm96
]
, we can choose radii
r_{\alpha}
so that the following holds:

(1)

the collection
\{B_{2r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}^{\prime})\}_{\alpha}\subset B_{1/2}(0)
are disjiont;

(2)

for each
\alpha
,
\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime}\cap(A_{2r_{\alpha},0}(y_{\alpha}),g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\prime},1,\gamma)
-strong-cone region;

(3)

for each
\alpha
, we have

d_{H}(\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime}\cap B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}^{\prime}),(y_{\alpha}^{\prime}+\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\prime})\cap B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{l-1,I}/2,

and

(m_{\alpha}-1/4)\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\prime}}(0)\leq\theta_{V^{\prime}}(y_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/2),\quad\theta_{V^{\prime}}(y_{\alpha},r_{\alpha})\leq(m+1/4)\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\prime}}(0)

Here
\delta_{l-1,I}=\delta_{l-1,I}(l-1,I,\beta,\sigma)
is the constant from
(\dagger_{l-1,I})
.

For each
\alpha
, we can therefore apply
(\dagger_{l-1,I})
to the sequences
(\eta_{y_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}})_{\sharp}V_{i}^{\prime}
,
g_{i}^{\prime}\circ\eta_{y_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}}^{-1}
. We obtain a finite collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\sigma,\beta)
-smooth models, an integer
N^{\prime}
, and radii
r_{\alpha,i}^{\prime}\in(\frac{4}{5}r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha})
, so that each
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(B_{r_{\alpha,i}^{\prime}}(y_{\alpha}^{\prime}),g_{i}^{\prime})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\mathcal{S}^{\prime},N^{\prime})
-strong-cone decomposition, for
i>>1
.

Recall that
V^{\prime}\llcorner(A_{\infty,1},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-strong-cone region, and
\beta\leq\gamma
. By our choice of
\alpha
, we get that
(S=V^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\{(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\prime},B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}^{\prime}))\}_{\alpha})
is a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\sigma,\gamma)
-smooth model.
C^{2}
convergence implies
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(B_{1},g_{i}^{\prime})
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region provided
i>>1
.

It follows that each
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner(B_{1},g_{i}^{\prime})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\{S\}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime},1+N^{\prime})
-strong-cone decomposition. The proof of Case 1B now continues as in Case 1A.

Case 2:
\mathcal{I}\not\subset\{0\}
and
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)>1
.
Let
\mathcal{I}\setminus\{0\}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{d^{\prime}}\}
, which is non-empty by assumption. We can choose a radius
r\in(1-20\sigma(I+1),1-5\sigma)
with the property that
\max\{||y_{\alpha}|-r||:y_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}\}\geq 5\sigma
.

Set
r_{0}=\min\{|y_{\alpha}|:y_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}\}/4
, and
y_{0}=0
, and then for each
\alpha=1,\ldots,d^{\prime}
with
|y_{\alpha}|<r
, choose a radius
r_{\alpha}
so that:

(1)

the collection
\{B_{2r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha}\subset A_{r-4\sigma,2r_{0}}(0)
are disjoint;

(2)

for each
\alpha
, if we set
\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}=T_{y_{\alpha}}\mathbf{C}
to be the tangent plane of
\mathbf{C}
, then
\mathbf{C}\cap(A_{2r_{\alpha},0}(y_{\alpha}),g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},1,\gamma)
-strong-cone region;

(3)

for each
\alpha
, we have

d_{H}(\mathbf{C}\cap B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}),(y_{\alpha}+\mathbf{C}_{\alpha})\cap B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}))\leq\delta_{l-1,I}/2,

and

m-1/4\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(y_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/2),\quad\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(y_{\alpha},r_{\alpha})\leq m+1/4.

Note that every
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(0)=m<m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, and so in fact
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(0)\leq\tilde{\theta}_{l-1}
.

For
i>>1
we can apply
(\dagger_{l-1,I})
to find a finite collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\sigma,\beta)
-smooth models, an integer
N^{\prime}
, and radii
r_{\alpha,i}^{\prime}\in(\frac{4}{5}r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha})
, so that every
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r^{\prime}_{\alpha,i}}(y_{\alpha}),g_{i})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\mathcal{S}^{\prime},N^{\prime})
-strong-cone decomposition.

On the other hand, since
\mathrm{index}(V_{i},B_{r_{0}}(0),g_{i})\leq I-1
for all
i>>1
, we can apply
(\dagger_{l,I-1})
to find another finite collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
, an integer
N^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, and radii
r^{\prime}_{0,i}\in(\frac{4}{5}r_{0},r_{0})
, so that each
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r^{\prime}_{0,i}}(0),g_{i})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
-strong-cone decomposition.

Now observe that
(m[\mathbf{C}],\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},B_{\frac{r_{\alpha}}{r}}(\frac{y_{\alpha}}{r}))\}_{\alpha=0}^{d^{\prime}})
is a
(m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0),\sigma,\gamma)
-smooth model, and each
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r},g_{i})
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region for
i>>1
.

Combining all of the above, if we take
\mathcal{S}=\{S\}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
,
N=IN^{\prime}+N^{\prime\prime}+1
, then every
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r},g_{i})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\mathcal{S},N)
-strong-cone decomposition. This is a contradiction, and finishes the proof of Case 2.

Case 3:
\mathcal{I}\not\subset\{0\}
and
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=1
.
In this case, since
\mathrm{sing}\mathbf{C}=\emptyset
,
V_{i}\to m[\mathbf{C}]
in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
B_{1}\setminus\mathcal{I}
. Pick a radius
r\in(1-20\sigma(I+1),1-5\sigma)
with the property that
\max\{||y_{\alpha}|-r||:y_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}\}\geq 5\sigma
. Let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}\cap B_{r}=\{1,\ldots,d^{\prime}\}
.

For each
y_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, choose a radius
r_{\alpha}
so that all the
\{B_{2r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha}\subset B_{r-4\sigma}
are disjoint. Define the rescaled varifolds
V^{\alpha}_{i}=(\eta_{y_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}})_{\sharp}V_{i}
and metrics
g_{i}^{\alpha}=g_{i}\circ\eta_{y_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}}^{-1}
. Then for each
\alpha,i
we have
\mathrm{index}(V^{\alpha}_{i},B_{1},g_{i}^{\alpha})\leq I
, and
V^{\alpha}_{i}
converge to
m[\mathbf{C}]
as varifolds in
B_{1}
, and converge to
\mathbf{C}
in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
B_{1}\setminus\{0\}
. We can therefore apply Case 1 of
(\dagger_{l,I})
to each sequence to deduce the existence of a finite collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\sigma,\beta)
-smooth models, and an integer
N^{\prime}
, and radii
r^{\prime}_{\alpha,i}\in(\frac{4}{5}r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha})
, so that every
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r^{\prime}_{\alpha,i}}(y_{\alpha}),g_{i})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\mathcal{S}^{\prime},N^{\prime})
-strong-decomposition whenever
i>>1
.

An important note: if
\#\mathcal{I}\geq 2
, then by Lemma
4.4
and our choice of balls
B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})
, we have
\mathrm{index}(V^{\alpha}_{i},B_{1},g_{i}^{\alpha})\leq I-1
for each
\alpha=1,\ldots,d^{\prime}
and
i>>1
. We can therefore apply Case 1 of
(\dagger_{I-1,l})
to the sequence
V^{\alpha}_{i}
rather than Case 1 of
(\dagger_{l,I})
, to deduce the same result.

Now
(S=m[\mathbf{C}],\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{C},B_{\frac{r_{\alpha}}{r}}(\frac{y_{\alpha}}{r}))\}_{\alpha=1}^{d^{\prime}})
is a
(m,\sigma,0)
-smooth model. By
C^{2}
convergence of
V_{i}
to
m[\mathbf{C}]
away from
\mathcal{I}
, and our choice of
r
, the
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r},g_{i})
are
(S,\beta)
-smooth regions for
i>>1
.

Therefore, we deduce that
V_{i}\llcorner(B_{r}(0),g_{i})
admits a
(\tilde{\theta}_{l},\beta,\{S\}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime},IN^{\prime}+1)
-strong-cone decomposition for all
i>>1
. This is a contradiction, and completes the proof of Case 3. ∎

8.
Parameterization

In this section we construct “parameterizations” for our cone regions. We show that every
M
admitting a cone-decomposition can be realized as the image under some Lipschitz map of one of finitely-many
\{M_{v}\}_{v}
, depending only on the parameters of the decomposition. In this section we work in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
.

This section is annoying technical, due almost entirely to multiplicity. If all our
M
s were single-sheeted (e.g. as when
M
is an area-minimizing boundary), then all our parameterizing maps would be
C^{1}
perturbations of the identity, and this section would be more or less trivial. Unfortunately, maps
\phi
taking multi-sheeted
M_{1}
to
M_{2}
are only
C^{0}
perturbations of the identity, with the
C^{1}
norm depending on the relative gaps between in
M_{1}
versus
M_{2}
. So to glue various parameterizations together we cannot use “soft” methods, but must rely much more explicitly on the exact structure of these maps.

We cannot get a priori control on
D\phi^{-1}
in Theorem
8.1
for two reasons. Intuitively: the first is that within a “class” of
M
having the same kind of cone decomposition, the inner radius of cone regions can be made arbitrarily small, but the outer radius has an upper bound; the second is that sheets can be made arbitrarily close together, but not arbitrarily far apart. For an example of the first behavior, consider scalings of a leaf of the Hardt-Simon foliation associated to an area-minimizing hypercone. For an example of the second, simply consider two planes converging to each other.

Getting global regularity of
\phi
better than Lipschitz seems to be more or less equivalent to knowing that all the cones you see are integrable through rotations. Even knowing this, getting better a priori bounds on
\phi
requires sharper decay estimates in Theorem
6.3
. In certain circumstances this is known. See Remark
8.2
.

It will be convenient to introduce some further notation. For each
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
fix a choice of unit normal
\nu_{\mathbf{C}}(x)
, and write
B^{*}_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{8}:\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathbf{C})<\epsilon|x|\}
for the conical
\epsilon
-neighborhood around
\mathbf{C}
. Let us abuse notation slightly, and define the function
G_{\mathbf{C}}:\mathbf{C}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
by

G_{\mathbf{C}}(x,t)=\frac{x+t\nu_{\mathbf{C}}(x)}{|x+t\nu_{\mathbf{C}}(x)|}|x|.

By Theorem
5.1
, after shrinking
\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda)
as necessary, if
U_{\mathbf{C}}=\{(x,t)\in\mathbf{C}\times\mathbb{R}:|t|<2\epsilon_{0}|x|\}
, then
G_{\mathbf{C}}
restricts to a smooth diffeomorphism from
U_{\mathbf{C}}
onto its image, and we have
|G_{\mathbf{C}}(x,t)|=|x|
, and

(75)

\displaystyle|G_{\mathbf{C}}(x,t)-(x+t\nu_{\mathbf{C}}(x))|\leq|t|^{2},\quad|DG_{\mathbf{C}}|_{(x,t)}-Id|\leq|t|/\epsilon_{0},

(76)

\displaystyle(1-|x|^{-2}t^{2})|t|\leq d(G_{\mathbf{C}}(x,t),\mathbf{C})\leq|t|,

for all
(x,t)\in U_{\mathbf{C}}
. In the second inequality we identify
T_{x}\mathbf{C}\times\mathbb{R}
with
\mathbb{R}^{8}
via
(v,t)\leftrightarrow v+t\nu_{\mathbf{C}}(x)
.

Let
X_{\mathbf{C}}
be the vector field in
G(U_{\mathbf{C}})\supset B^{*}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\mathbf{C})
defined by
X_{\mathbf{C}}=DG_{\mathbf{C}}(\partial_{t})
, so that
y(t)=G_{\mathbf{C}}(x,t)
is a solution to the ODE:
\dot{y}(t)=X_{\mathbf{C}}(y(t))
,
y(0)=x
. After shrinking
\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda)
possibly further we can assume that for
x\in B_{\epsilon_{0}}^{*}(\mathbf{C})
, we have

(77)

\displaystyle X_{\mathbf{C}}(x)\perp x,\quad 0.99\leq|X_{\mathbf{C}}|\leq 1,\quad|x||DX_{\mathbf{C}}|+|x|^{2}|D^{2}X_{\mathbf{C}}|\leq 1/\epsilon_{0}.

Let us henceforth fix
\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda)
so that the previous discussion holds, for any
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
.

Given a smooth vector field
X
defined on some open set
U
, we let
F_{X}(x,t)
be the flow of
X
, i.e. the map defined by
\partial_{t}F_{X}(x,t)=X(F_{X}(x,t))
,
F_{X}(x,0)=x
. We say a mapping
\phi:U\to U
preserves the flowlines of
X
if
\phi(x)=F_{X}(x,t_{\phi}(x))
for some real-valued function
t_{\phi}
.

Given
R>\rho>0
,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{8}
,
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
, we say
\phi|_{A_{R,\rho}(a)}
is a
(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
-map if
\phi
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism
A_{R,\rho}(a)\to A_{R,\rho}(a)
,
\phi=id
outside
a+B^{*}_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})
, and
\phi
preserves the flowlines of
X_{\mathbf{C}}(\cdot-a)
.

Below is our main theorem of this section. We remind the reader that all distances, derivatives, inner products, etc. are taken with respect to
g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
unless explicitly stated otherwise.

Theorem 8.1

(Parameterizing cone decompositions)
.

Given
\Lambda,\epsilon>0,\sigma\in(0,10^{-2}]
, there are constants
\beta(\Lambda,\sigma,\epsilon)
,
\gamma(\Lambda,\sigma,\epsilon)
so that the following holds. Take
\mathcal{S}
be any finite collection of
(\Lambda,\sigma,\gamma)
-smooth models,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathcal{G}
any set of
C^{3}
metrics on
B_{1}
. Then we can find finite collections
\{g_{v}\}_{v}\subset\mathcal{G}
,
\{M_{v}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1},g_{v})\}_{v}
,
\{\mathbf{C}_{v}\}_{v}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, and an increasing function
C_{\mathcal{S},N,\mathcal{G}}:[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
, with the properties

\mathrm{sing}M_{v}\subset B_{1-\sigma},\quad d_{H}(M_{v}\cap A_{1,1-\sigma},\mathbf{C}_{v}\cap A_{1,1-\sigma})\leq\epsilon,

and so that: given any
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1},g)
with
M\cap B_{1}
admitting a
(\Lambda,\beta,\mathcal{S},N)
-strong-cone decomposition, then we can find a
v
and a local bi-Lipschitz map
\phi:B_{1}\to B_{1}
such that the following holds true:

(1)

\phi(\overline{M_{v}}\cap B_{1})=\overline{M}\cap B_{1}
,
\phi(\mathrm{sing}M_{v}\cap B_{1})=\mathrm{sing}M\cap B_{1}
;

(2)

\phi|_{B_{1}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{v}}
is a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism onto its image;

(3)

\mathrm{Lip}(\phi|_{B_{r}})\leq C_{\mathcal{S},N,\mathcal{G}}(r)
for any
r<1
;

(4)

\phi|_{A_{1,1-\sigma}}
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{v},\epsilon)
-map;

(5)

\mathrm{sing}M\subset B_{1-\sigma}
and
d_{H}(M\cap A_{1,1-\sigma},\mathbf{C}_{v}\cap A_{1,1-\sigma})\leq\epsilon
.

If
M
is area-minimizing, then one can assume that the
M_{v}
is area-minimizing also.

Remark 8.2
.

If all the singularities of
M
are modelled on cones of a certain type, for example quadratic cones or other special cones being “integrable through rotations,” then one can arrange
\phi
in Theorem
8.1
to be globally
C^{1,\alpha}
, though without any a priori
C^{1,\alpha}
estimates. When
\mathbf{C}
is planar (as in
[
Whi18
]
), or when
\mathbf{C}
is a minimizing quadratic cone in Euclidean space (as in
[
ES19
]
), then I would expect one could get
C^{1,\alpha}
estimates on the
\phi
. See Remark
8.8
.

Remark 8.3
.

The restriction
r<1
in Item
3
is purely technical. The reason is that the Lipschitz norm of
\phi
depends on distance between sheets in each
M_{v}
. In a cone decomposition this distance is bounded away from zero inside
B_{1}
, but could tend to
0
as you approach
\partial B_{1}
.

Before proving Theorem
8.1
we require several technical Lemmas. The first two construct parameterizations for a single smooth or cone region. The third is a gluing Lemma, that allows us to glue parameterizations between two regions.

Lemma 8.4

(Smooth region parameterization)
.

Given
\Lambda,\epsilon>0
,
\sigma\in(0,10^{-2}]
, there are
\gamma(\Lambda,\sigma,\epsilon)
,
\beta(\Lambda,\epsilon)
so that the following holds. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
(S,\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}))\}_{\alpha})
be a
(\Lambda,\sigma,\gamma)
-smooth model. Let
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1},g)
for any
C^{2}
metric
g
, and suppose that each
M_{l}\cap(B_{1},g)
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region,
l=1,2
.

Write
O=B_{1}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}/4}(y_{\alpha})
. Then we can find a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
\phi:O\to O
such that

(78)

\displaystyle\phi(M_{1})=M_{2},\quad\phi|_{A_{1,1-\sigma}}\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)$-map, }

(79)

\displaystyle\phi|_{A_{r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/4}(y_{\alpha})}\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},\epsilon)$-map, for each $\alpha$,}

and

(80)

\displaystyle|D\phi|_{C^{0}(O\cap B_{r})}\leq c(S,M_{1},r)<\infty\quad\forall r<1.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\min\{\epsilon_{S},\epsilon/32\}
for some constant
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}(\Lambda)\leq\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda)/32
to be determined later, and ensure
\gamma\leq\min\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon\}/32
. Let
O^{\prime}=B_{1}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}/5}(y_{\alpha})
.

Write
S\llcorner B_{2}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}/8}(y_{\alpha})=m_{1}[S_{1}]+\ldots+m_{k}[S_{k}]
as in the Definition
7.0.1
of smooth model. Each
S_{i}
is orientable, being a smooth, closed (as sets) codimension-1 submanifold of the simply-connected region
B_{2}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}/8}(y_{\alpha})
. We can therefore choose a normal vector
\nu_{i}
on
S_{i}
so that
|\nu_{i}-X_{\mathbf{C}}|\leq c(\Lambda)\gamma
on
S_{i}\cap A_{1+\sigma,1-3\sigma}
. After replacing
X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}
with
-X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}
if necessary, we have also
|\nu_{i}-X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(\cdot-y_{\alpha})|\leq c(\Lambda)\gamma
on
S_{i}\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/8}(y_{\alpha})
, for each
\alpha
.

Consider the map
G_{i}:S_{i}\cap B_{1+\sigma}\times(-2\epsilon_{S},2\epsilon_{S})\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
by
G_{i}(x^{\prime},t)=x^{\prime}+t\nu_{i}(x^{\prime})
. By definition of the
\epsilon_{S}
,
G_{i}
is a smooth diffeomorphism onto its image
O_{i}
, and the images
O_{i}
are disjoint. Provided
\gamma(\Lambda,\sigma)
is sufficiently small, we have
O_{i}\supset B_{2\epsilon_{S}}(S_{i})\cap O^{\prime}
.

Define the smooth vector field
X_{i}=DG_{i}(\partial_{t})
on
O_{i}
, so that
t\mapsto G_{i}(x^{\prime},t)
is the flow of
X_{i}
. Let
\xi(t)
be a smooth, increasing function satisfying

\xi|_{(-\infty,1-2\sigma]}\equiv 0,\quad\xi|_{[1-\sigma,\infty)}\equiv 1,\quad|\xi^{\prime}|\leq 10/\sigma,

and then define
Y_{i}
on
B_{2\epsilon^{\prime}}(S_{i})\cap O^{\prime}
by

Y_{i}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}(1-\xi(|x|))X_{i}(x)+\xi(|x|)X_{\mathbf{C}}(x)&x\in A_{1,1-3\sigma}\\
\xi(\frac{|x-y_{\alpha}|}{2r_{\alpha}})X_{i}(x)+(1-\xi(\frac{|x-y_{\alpha}|}{2r_{\alpha}}))X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(x-y_{\alpha})&x\in A_{2r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/5}(y_{\alpha})\\
X_{i}(x)&otherwise\end{array}\right.

For
Z_{i}=X_{i},Y_{i}
, we have

(81)

\sup_{B_{2\epsilon^{\prime}}(S_{i})\cap A_{1,1-3\sigma}}|Z_{i}-X_{\mathbf{C}}|+|DZ_{i}-DX_{\mathbf{C}}|\leq c\gamma,

and for each
\alpha
,

(82)

\sup_{B_{2\epsilon^{\prime}}(S_{i})\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/5}(y_{\alpha})}|Z_{i}-X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(\cdot-y_{\alpha})|+r_{\alpha}|DZ_{i}-DX_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(\cdot-y_{\alpha})|\leq c\gamma,

where
c=c(\Lambda,\sigma)
. And of course
X_{i}=Y_{i}
in
B_{1-3\sigma}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})
. From the above and (
77
), provided
\gamma(\Lambda,\sigma)
is sufficiently small, the flow
F_{X_{i}}(x^{\prime},t)
is defined for all
|t|<\epsilon^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime}\in S_{i}\cap O^{\prime}
.

Let
H_{i}:S_{i}\cap O^{\prime}\times(-\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
be the smooth map given by
H_{i}(x^{\prime},t)=F_{X_{i}}(x^{\prime},t)
. Recalling our restriction
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon_{0}\min_{\alpha}r_{\alpha}/16
, (
81
), (
82
), (
77
) imply

(83)

\displaystyle|H_{i}-G_{i}|\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma)\gamma|t|,\quad|DH_{i}-DG_{i}|\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma)\gamma

(84)

\displaystyle 0.98|t|\leq d(G_{i}(x^{\prime},t),S_{i})\leq|t|

(85)

\displaystyle B_{\epsilon^{\prime}/2}(S_{i})\cap O^{\prime}\subset H_{i}(S_{i}\cap O\times(-\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}))\subset B_{2\epsilon^{\prime}}(S_{i})\cap O^{\prime}.

In particular, after identitying
S_{i}\cap O^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}
with
T^{\perp}S_{i}
via
\nu_{i}
, we have
|DH_{i}-Id|\leq\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+c(\Lambda,\sigma)\gamma
. Ensuring
\gamma(\Lambda,\sigma)
and
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}(\Lambda)
are sufficiently small, we get that
H_{i}
is a smooth diffeomorphism onto its image.

By considering the map
H_{i}^{-1}\circ G_{i}
, ensuring
\beta(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})
,
\gamma(\Lambda,\sigma)
are sufficiently small, and recalling the definition of smooth region, for each
p=1,2,i=1,\ldots,k,j=1,\ldots,m_{k}
, we can find
C^{2}
functions
u_{pij}:S_{i}\to(-\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})
satisfying

(86)

\displaystyle M_{p}\cap O^{\prime}=\cup_{i=1}^{k}\cup_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\{H_{i}(x^{\prime},u_{pij}(x^{\prime})):x^{\prime}\in S_{i}\cap O^{\prime}\},

(87)

\displaystyle|u_{pij}|\leq(1+c\gamma)\beta\epsilon_{S}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}/10,\quad|u_{pij}|_{C^{1}}\leq c(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}+\gamma+\beta\epsilon_{S})\leq 1,

for
c=c(\Lambda,\sigma)
. Since each
M_{p}
is multiplicity-one, by the maximum principle we can assume that

(88)

\displaystyle u_{pi1}<u_{pi2}<\ldots<u_{pim_{k}}\quad\forall p=1,2;i=1,\ldots,k.

For each
i=1,\ldots,k
, define the
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
g_{i}:S_{i}\cap O\times(-\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})\to S_{i}\cap O\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
by

g_{i}(x^{\prime},t)=g_{i}(x^{\prime},f_{\{u_{1ij}(x^{\prime})\}_{j},\{u_{2ij}(x^{\prime})\}_{j}}(t)),

where
f_{\{a_{j}\}_{j},\{b_{j}\}_{j}}(t)
is the function from Lemma
12.1
with
m_{i}
in place of
k
, and
\epsilon^{\prime}/10
in place of
\epsilon
. Then
g_{i}
is the identity for
|t|>\epsilon/5
and
g_{i}(x^{\prime},u_{1ij}(x^{\prime}))=(x^{\prime},u_{2ij}(x^{\prime}))
for every
j=1,\ldots,m_{i}
. If we let

D_{r}=\min\{u_{1ij}(x^{\prime})-u_{1ij^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}):j<j^{\prime},x^{\prime}\in S_{i}\cap B_{r}\cap O\},

then
D_{r}>0
for all
r<1
(by (
88
) and our definition of
O^{\prime}
), and it follows by Lemma
12.1
and (
87
) that
|Dg_{i}|\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma,D_{r})<\infty
on
B_{r}
.

Define
\phi_{i}:O\to O
by setting

\phi_{i}(x)=H_{i}\circ g\circ H_{i}^{-1}\text{ if }x\in B_{\epsilon^{\prime}/2}(S_{i})\cap O,\quad\phi_{i}(x)=x\text{ otherwise.}

By (
84
), (
85
) and our construction of
g_{i}
,
\phi_{i}
is a well-defined
C^{2}
diffeomorphism, satisfying

\phi_{i}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(S_{i})\cap O)=\overline{M_{2}}\cap B_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(S_{i})\cap O,\quad|D\phi_{i}|\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma,M_{1}).

Now set
\phi=\phi_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{k}
. We have
\phi(M_{1}\cap O)=M_{2}\cap O
by disjointness of the
O_{i}
, and
\phi|_{A_{1,1-\sigma}}
,
\phi|_{A_{r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/4}(y_{\alpha})}
are
(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
-,
(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},\epsilon)
-maps by our construction and our choice of
\gamma,\epsilon^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 8.5

(Cone region parameterization)
.

Take
\Lambda,\epsilon>0
,
\beta\geq 0
. Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
\rho\in[0,1)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
. For
i=1,2
,
j=1,\ldots,m
let
u_{ij}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,\rho}\to\mathbb{R}
be
C^{1}
functions satisfying

|x|^{-1}|u_{ij}|+|\nabla u_{ij}|\leq\beta\leq\epsilon/4\leq\epsilon_{0}/8,\quad u_{i1}<u_{i2}<\ldots<u_{im}\text{ for }i=1,2.

Write
M_{i}=\cup_{j=1}^{m}G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{ij}\nu_{\mathbf{C}})\cap A_{1,\rho}
.

Then we can find a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism
\phi:A_{1,\rho}\to A_{1,\rho}
satisfying

(89)

\displaystyle\phi(M_{1})=M_{2},\quad|\phi(x)|=|x|,\quad|D\phi|_{C^{0}(A_{r,\rho/r})}\leq c(\Lambda,M_{1},r)\text{ for every $r<1$}

(90)

\phi|_{A_{1,\rho}}
is a
(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
-map.

If
m=1
, then in fact we have
|D\phi-Id|\leq c(\Lambda)\frac{\beta}{\epsilon}
. If the
u_{ij}
are
C^{k}
, then
\phi
is
C^{k}
also.

Remark 8.6
.

If
\rho=0
, the maximum principle
[
Ilm96
]
implies
m=1
, so provided
\beta/\epsilon
is sufficiently small (depending only on
\Lambda
),
\phi
extends to a bi-Lipschitz map
B_{1}\to B_{1}
, satisfying

(1-c(\Lambda)\beta/\epsilon)|x-y|\leq|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|\leq(1+c(\Lambda)\beta/\epsilon)|x-y|\quad\forall x,y\in B_{1}.

Remark 8.7
.

It’s tempting to think the
\phi
in Lemma
8.5
should also satisfy an estimate like
|x||D^{2}\phi|\leq c(\Lambda,M_{1})
, provided the
u_{ij}
satisfy a similar estimate
|x||\nabla^{2}u_{ij}|\leq\beta
. This is certainly true if
m=1
, but when
m\geq 2
this seems to be false. The issue is that second derivatives of the function
f_{\{a_{j}\}_{j},\{b_{j}\}_{j}}
from Lemma
12.1
depend on the spacing of
both
the
\{a_{j}\}_{j}
and the
\{b_{j}\}_{j}
(as opposed to the first derivative which depends only on the spacing of the
\{a_{j}\}_{j}
).

Proof.

Let
f_{\{a_{j}\}_{j},\{b_{j}\}_{j}}(t)
be the function from Lemma
12.1
, with
m
in place of
k
,
\epsilon/4
in place of
\epsilon
, and let
U=\{(x,t)\in\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,\rho}\times\mathbb{R}:|t|<2\epsilon_{0}|x|\}
. If
m\geq 2
, or
\beta\geq 10^{-3}\epsilon
, define
g:U\to U
by

g(x,t)=(x,|x|f_{\{|x|^{-1}u_{1j}(x)\}_{j},\{|x|^{-1}u_{2j}(x)\}_{j}}(|x|^{-1}t)).

If
m=1
and
\beta\leq 10^{-3}\epsilon
, then take
\eta(t)
to be the function as in the proof of Lemma
12.1
, and then instead set

g(x,t)=(x,t+(u_{21}(x)-u_{11}(x))\eta(4\epsilon^{-1}|x|^{-1}(t-u_{11}(x)))).

Then by Lemma
12.1

g
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism
U\to U
, satisfying

\displaystyle g(x,t)=(x,t)\text{ for }|t|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}|x|,\quad|Dg|_{C^{0}(U\cap A_{r,\rho/r})}\leq c(\Lambda,M_{1},r)\text{ for all $r<1$},

\displaystyle g(x,u_{1j}(x))=(x,u_{2j}(x))\text{ for each }j=1,\ldots,m.

If
m=1
, then
|Dg-Id|\leq c(\Lambda)\beta/\epsilon
on all of
U
.

Now define
\phi
by

\phi(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}G_{\mathbf{C}}\circ g\circ G_{\mathbf{C}}^{-1}&x\in A_{1,\rho}\cap G_{\mathbf{C}}(U)\\
x&x\in A_{1,\rho}\setminus G_{\mathbf{C}}(U)\end{array}\right..\qed

Remark 8.8
.

If
\rho=0
and
\mathbf{C}
is “integrable through rotations” (e.g. if
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
consists of rotations of
\mathbf{C}
), then provided
\beta(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
,
\epsilon(\Lambda)
are sufficiently small, instead of (
89
), (
90
) we can arrange
\phi
to instead satisfy:

(91)

\displaystyle\text{$\phi$ is a $C^{2}$ diffeomorphism $A_{1,0}\to A_{1,0}$},\quad\phi(M_{1})=M_{2},\quad|\phi(x)|=|x|,

(92)

\displaystyle\phi\text{ extends in a $C^{1,\alpha}$ fashion to $B_{1}$, for some $\alpha(\mathbf{C})>0$},

(93)

\displaystyle\text{$\phi|_{A_{1,1/8}}$ is a $(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)$-map}.

By using this
\phi
in the proof of Theorem
8.1
instead of the
\phi
generated by Lemma
8.5
, one can get the map in Theorem
8.1
to be globally
C^{1,\alpha}
.

We outline how to do this.
[
AA81
]
(see also
[
Sim93
, Lemma 1]
) implies that for such
\mathbf{C}
there are constants
\alpha(\mathbf{C}),\delta(\mathbf{C})\in(0,1)
so that if
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1},g)
,
M\cap(A_{1,0},g)
is a
(\mathbf{C},1,\delta)
-strong-cone region, and
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\delta
, then there is a
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C})
being a rotation of
\mathbf{C}
such that

M\cap A_{1,0}=G_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(u)\cap A_{1,0},\quad|x|^{-1}|u|+|\nabla u|+|x||\nabla^{2}u|\leq c(\mathbf{C})\delta|x|^{\alpha},

for some
C^{2}
function
u:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap A_{1,0}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\perp}
.

Now take
M_{1},M_{2}
satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma
8.5
with this
\mathbf{C}
, and
\beta(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
,
\epsilon(\Lambda)
to be chosen. Combining the previous paragraph with Lemma
8.5
, provided
\beta(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
is small we deduce there are
\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2}
rotations of
\mathbf{C}
and
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}:A_{1,0}\to A_{1,0}
, so that
\phi_{i}(\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap A_{1,0})=\mathrm{spt}M_{i}\cap A_{1,0}
,
|D\phi_{i}|_{x}-Id|\leq c(\mathbf{C})\beta|x|^{\alpha}
, and
\phi_{i}|_{A_{1,0}}
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{i},\epsilon)
-map. In particular each
\phi_{i}
extends to a
C^{1,\alpha}
map on
B_{1}
with
D\phi_{i}|_{0}=Id
. Let
P
be any rotation taking
\mathbf{C}_{1}
to
\mathbf{C}_{2}
, with
|P-Id|\leq c(\mathbf{C})\beta
.

On the other hand, we can also apply Lemma
8.5
directly to obtain a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
\phi
satisfying (
89
), (
90
). Since
d_{H}(\mathbf{C}_{i}\cap\partial B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap\partial B_{1})\leq c(\mathbf{C})\beta
, using Theorem
5.1
and Lemma
8.9
, ensuring
\epsilon(\Lambda)
and
\beta(\Lambda,\epsilon)
are sufficiently small, we can obtain a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
\psi:A_{1,0}\to A_{1,0}
satisfying

\psi|_{A_{1,1/8}}=\phi,\quad\psi_{A_{1/16,0}}=\phi_{2}\circ P\circ\phi_{1}^{-1},\quad|\psi(x)|=|x|,\quad|D\psi|\leq c(\mathbf{C}).

This
\psi
will satisfy (
91
), (
92
), (
93
).

Unfortunately, integrability is in general an open condition, so it’s not clear if one can take
\beta
to depend only on
\Lambda
, rather than the specific integrable cone
\mathbf{C}
. Of course, the only
known
stable
7
-dimensional hypercones are the quadratic cones, which are all integrable through rotations, and one could obviously arrange
\beta
so that the previous argument works with these cones. In fact I believe there are no known minimal hypercones which are not integrable through rotations. At any rate it would be interesting to understand this better.

Lemma 8.9

(Gluing Lemma)
.

Given
\Lambda,\epsilon>0
,
\sigma\in(0,10^{-2}]
, there are constants
\delta_{1}(\Lambda,\sigma)
,
\delta^{\prime}_{1}(\Lambda,\epsilon,\sigma)
,
\epsilon_{1}(\Lambda)\leq\epsilon_{0}/4
