so that provided
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{1}
the following holds. Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
. For
i=1,2,j=1,\ldots,m
, take
C^{1}
functions
u_{ij}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1+\sigma,1-4\sigma}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

|u_{ij}|_{C^{1}}\leq\epsilon/10,\quad u_{i1}<u_{i2}<\ldots<u_{im},

and then set
M_{i}=\cup_{j=1}^{m}G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{ij}\nu_{\mathbf{C}})\cap A_{1+\sigma,1-4\sigma}
.

Let
O_{1},O_{2}\supset A_{1+\sigma,1-4\sigma}
be open sets in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
. For
l=1,2
, let
T_{l}:O_{l}\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
,
\phi_{l}:O_{l}\to O_{l}
be
C^{1}
diffeomorphisms such that

\displaystyle|T_{l}-id|_{C^{1}(O_{l})}\leq\delta^{\prime}_{1},\quad T_{l}(O_{l})\supset A_{1+\sigma,1-4\sigma},

\displaystyle T_{l}(\phi_{l}(M_{1}\cap O_{l}))=M_{2}\cap T_{l}(O_{l}),\quad\phi_{l}=id\text{ outside }B_{\epsilon/10}(\mathbf{C}).

Assume additionally there are smooth vector fields
X_{l}
on
O_{l}\cap B_{2\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})
satisfying

|X_{l}-X_{\mathbf{C}}|_{C^{1}(B_{2\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\cap O_{l})}\leq\delta_{1},

so that each
\phi_{l}
preserves the flowlines of
X_{l}
.

Define

\displaystyle O=(O_{1}\setminus B_{1-2\sigma})\cup(O_{2}\cap B_{1-\sigma}),

\displaystyle O^{\prime}=(T_{1}(O_{1})\setminus B_{1-2\sigma})\cup(T_{2}(O_{2})\cap B_{1-\sigma}).

Then we can find a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism
h:O\to O^{\prime}
with the properties that:

\displaystyle h|_{O\setminus B_{1-0.5\sigma}}=T_{1}\phi_{1},\quad h|_{O\cap B_{1-2.5\sigma}}=T_{2}\phi_{2},

\displaystyle h(M_{1}\cap O)=M_{2}\cap O^{\prime},\quad|Dh|\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma,M_{1},|D\phi_{1}|_{C^{1}(O_{1})},|D\phi_{2}|_{C^{1}(O_{2})}).

If the
u_{ij}
,
T_{l}
,
\phi_{l}
are
C^{k}
, then
h
is
C^{k}
also.

Remark 8.10
.

Of course the same Lemma holds true if
X_{\mathbf{C}}
is replaced with
-X_{\mathbf{C}}
.

Proof.

Take
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}/4
. For ease of notation write
U=(\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1-3\sigma})\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
. Fix
\xi(t)
to be a smooth, increasing function satisfying

\xi|_{(-\infty,1-2\sigma]}=0,\quad\xi|_{[1-\sigma,\infty)}=1,\quad\xi^{\prime}\leq 10/\sigma.

Define
T:A_{1+\sigma,1-4\sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
by
T(x)=\xi(|x|)T_{1}(x)+(1-\xi(|x|))T_{2}(x)
. Then for
\delta^{\prime}(\sigma)
sufficiently small,
T
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism onto its image satisfying

|T-id|\leq\delta^{\prime},\quad|DT-Id|\leq c(\sigma)\delta^{\prime}.

Observe that from our hypotheses on
\phi_{l}
and our construction of
T
, we have

(94)

\displaystyle T_{1}\phi_{1}=T\text{ on }A_{1,1-\sigma}\setminus B_{\epsilon/10}(\mathbf{C}),\quad T_{2}\phi_{2}=T\text{ on }A_{1-2\sigma,1-3\sigma}\setminus B_{\epsilon/10}(\mathbf{C}).

Define on
B_{2\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\cap A_{1+\sigma,1-4\sigma}
the smooth vector field

X(x)=\xi(|x|)X_{1}(x)+(1-\xi(|x|))X_{2}(x).

Then we have

|X-X_{\mathbf{C}}|_{C^{1}(B_{2\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\cap A_{1+\sigma,1-4\sigma})}\leq c(\sigma)\delta.

By the above and (
77
), provided
\delta(\sigma)
is sufficiently small, the flow
F_{X}(x^{\prime},t)
of
X
exists for all
|t|<\epsilon
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1-3\sigma}
.

Define
G:U\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
by
G(x^{\prime},t)=F_{X}(x^{\prime},t)
. Then, ensuring
\epsilon(\Lambda)
and
\delta(\Lambda,\sigma)
are small,
G
is a smooth diffeomorphism onto its image satisfying:

(95)

\displaystyle|G-G_{\mathbf{C}}|\leq c\delta|t|,\quad|DG-DG_{\mathbf{C}}|+|D(G^{-1}\circ G_{\mathbf{C}})-Id|\leq c\delta,

(96)

\displaystyle(1-c\delta)|x^{\prime}|\leq|G(x^{\prime},t)|\leq(1+c\delta)|x^{\prime}|

(97)

\displaystyle 0.98|t|\leq d(G(x^{\prime},t),\mathbf{C})\leq 1.02|t|,

for
c=c(\Lambda,\sigma)
, and

(98)

\displaystyle B_{0.9\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\cap A_{1-0.1\sigma,1-2.9\sigma}\subset G(U)\subset B_{1.1\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\cap A_{1+0.1\sigma,1-3.1\sigma},

Similarly, ensuring
\delta^{\prime}(\epsilon,\sigma)
is small, then the diffeomorphism
TG\equiv T\circ G
satisfies (with
c=c(\Lambda,\sigma)
)

(99)

\displaystyle|TG-G_{\mathbf{C}}|\leq c(\delta|t|+\delta^{\prime}),\quad|D(TG)-G_{\mathbf{C}}|+|D(G^{-1}T^{-1}G_{\mathbf{C}})-Id|\leq c(\delta+\delta^{\prime})

(100)

\displaystyle B_{0.8\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\cap A_{1-0.2\sigma,1-2.8\sigma}\subset(TG)(U)\subset B_{1.2\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\cap A_{1+0.2\sigma,1-3.2\sigma}.

By considering the maps
G^{-1}G_{\mathbf{C}}
and
G^{-1}T^{-1}G_{\mathbf{C}}
, ensuring
\delta^{\prime}(\epsilon,\Lambda,\sigma)
,
\delta(\Lambda,\sigma)
are small, then (
95
)-(
100
) imply we can find
C^{1}
functions
u^{G}_{j},u^{T}_{j}:U\to\mathbb{R}
(
j=1,\ldots,m
) satisfying

\displaystyle M_{1}\cap G(U)=\cup_{j=1}^{m}\{G(x^{\prime},u^{G}_{j}(x^{\prime})):x^{\prime}\in U\},

(101)

\displaystyle|u^{G}_{j}|\leq(1+c\delta)\epsilon/10\leq\epsilon/9,\quad|u^{G}_{j}|_{C^{1}(U)}\leq c(\beta+\delta)\leq 1,

\displaystyle u^{G}_{1}<u^{G}_{2}<\ldots<u^{G}_{m},

and

\displaystyle M_{2}\cap TG(U)=\cup_{j=1}^{m}\{TG(x^{\prime},u^{T}_{j}(x^{\prime})):x^{\prime}\in U\},

(102)

\displaystyle|u^{T}_{j}|\leq(1+c\delta)\epsilon/10+c\delta^{\prime}\leq\epsilon/9,\quad|u^{T}_{j}|_{C^{1}(U)}\leq c(\beta+\delta+\delta^{\prime})\leq 1,

\displaystyle u^{T}_{1}<u^{T}_{2}<\ldots<u^{T}_{m},

where
c=c(\Lambda,\sigma)
.

By (
94
), (
96
) (taking
\delta(\Lambda,\sigma)
sufficiently small), (
97
), and our hypotheses on
T_{l}
,
\phi_{l}
, we can define
C^{1}
functions
t_{1}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1-0.9\sigma}\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\to\mathbb{R}
,
t_{2}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1-2.1\sigma,1-3\sigma}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(G^{-1}\circ T^{-1}\circ T_{l}\circ\phi_{l}\circ G)(x^{\prime},t)=(x^{\prime},t_{l}(x^{\prime},t)).

The
t_{l}
have the properties that: for each
x^{\prime}
,
t_{l}(x^{\prime},\cdot)
is a diffeomorphism of
(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
coinciding with
id
for
|t|>\epsilon/9
;
t_{l}(x^{\prime},u_{j}^{G}(x^{\prime}))=u_{j}^{T}(x^{\prime})
; and

(103)

\displaystyle|\nabla t_{l}|+|\partial_{t}t_{l}|\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma,|\phi_{l}|_{C^{1}(O_{l})}).

Let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth functions satisfying

\displaystyle\eta_{1}|_{(-\infty,1-2.3\sigma]}\equiv 1,\quad\eta_{1}|_{[1-2.2\sigma,\infty)}\equiv 0,\quad 0\leq-\eta_{1}^{\prime}\leq 100/\sigma

\displaystyle\eta_{2}|_{(-\infty,1-0.8\sigma]}\equiv 1,\quad\eta_{2}|_{[1-0.7\sigma,\infty)}\equiv 0,\quad 0\leq-\eta_{2}^{\prime}\leq 100/\sigma.

Define
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
by

f(x^{\prime},t)=f_{\{u_{j}^{G}(x^{\prime})\}_{j},\{u_{j}^{T}(x^{\prime})\}_{j}}(t)

where
f_{\{a_{j}\}_{j},\{b_{j}\}_{j}}(t)
is the function from Lemma
12.1
with
\epsilon/9
in place of
\epsilon
, and
m
in place of
k
. Define
s:U\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle s(x^{\prime},t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\eta_{1}(|x^{\prime}|)t_{2}(x^{\prime},t)+(1-\eta_{1}(|x^{\prime}|))f(x^{\prime},t)&1-3\sigma<|x^{\prime}|<1-2.2\sigma\\
f(x^{\prime},t)&1-2.2\sigma\leq|x^{\prime}|<1-0.8\sigma\\
\eta_{2}(|x^{\prime}|)f(x^{\prime},t)+(1-\eta_{2}(|x^{\prime}|))t_{1}(x^{\prime},t)&1-0.8\sigma\leq|x^{\prime}|<1\end{array}\right..

Then
s
is
C^{1}
(or
C^{k}
if the
\phi_{l}
,
T_{l}
,
u_{ij}
are
C^{k}
), and
s(x^{\prime},\cdot)
is a diffeomorphism of
(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
for every
x^{\prime}
, and
s(x^{\prime},t)
enjoys the properties:

\displaystyle s=t_{1}\text{ if }|x^{\prime}|>1-0.7\sigma,\quad s=t_{2}\text{ if }|x^{\prime}|<1-2.3\sigma,

\displaystyle s(x^{\prime},t)=t\text{ if }|t|\geq\epsilon/4

\displaystyle s(x^{\prime},u_{j}^{G}(x^{\prime}))=u_{j}^{T}(x^{\prime})\text{ for every }j=1,\ldots,m.

If we let

D=\inf\{u_{j}^{G}(x^{\prime})-u_{j^{\prime}}^{G}(x^{\prime}):j<j^{\prime},x^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1-0.5\sigma,1-2.5\sigma}\}>0,

then by (
101
), (
102
), (
103
) and Lemma
12.1
we get

|\nabla s|+|\partial_{t}s|\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma,D,|\phi_{1}|_{C^{1}(O_{1})},|\phi_{2}|_{C^{1}(O_{2})})<\infty.

Let us define
g:U\to U
by
g(x^{\prime},t)=(x^{\prime},s(x^{\prime},t))
. Trivially
g
is a
C^{1}
(or
C^{k}
) diffeomorphism of
U
. We then define
h:O\to O^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle h(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}(T\circ G\circ g\circ G^{-1})(x)&x\in B_{0.9\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\cap A_{1-0.1\sigma,1-2.9\sigma}\\
T(x)&x\in A_{1-0.1\sigma,1-2.9\sigma}\setminus B_{0.9\epsilon}(\mathbf{C})\\
T_{1}\phi_{1}(x)&x\in O\setminus B_{1-0.2\sigma}\\
T_{2}\phi_{2}(x)&x\in O\cap B_{1-2.8\sigma}\end{array}\right..

It follows by (
95
)-(
98
) and our definition of
g
that
\phi
is well-defined, and satisfies the requirements of the Lemma.
∎

We are now set up to prove Theorem
8.1
.

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

We prove Theorem
8.1
by induction on
N
and by contradiction. Suppose either
N=1
, or by inductive hypothesis the Lemma holds with
N-1
in place of
N
. Suppose, towards a contradiction, the Lemma fails, for
\beta,\gamma,\epsilon
for the moment arbitrarily fixed, but to be chosen later (we note that there is no loss in assuming
\epsilon(\Lambda,\sigma)
is as small as we like). Then there is a finite collection
\mathcal{S}
of
(\Lambda,\sigma,\gamma)
-smooth models, a set
\mathcal{G}
of
C^{3}
metrics on
B_{1}
, sequences
g_{i}\in\mathcal{G}
,
M_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1},g_{i})
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, and an
r<1
, so that each
M_{i}\cap B_{1}
admits a
(\Lambda,\beta,\mathcal{S},N)
-strong-cone decomposition, but for every
j>i
there is no local bi-Lipschitz map
\phi_{ij}:B_{1}\to B_{1}
satisfying
Lip(\phi_{ij}|_{B_{r}})\leq j
, and making Theorem
8.1
(
1
),(
2
),(
4
),(
5
) true with
\phi_{ij}
,
M_{i}
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}
,
M_{j}
in place of
\phi
,
M_{v}
,
\mathbf{C}_{v}
,
M
(respectively). Note that if we replace
i
with any subsequence of
i
, then this new sequence satisfies the same contradiction hypotheses.

By hypothesis, for every
i
there is a collection of at most
N
smooth regions and strong-cone regions which fit together as per Definition
7.0.4
. After passing to a subsequence (and apply Theorem
5.1
), we can assume that for all
i
we have either:
M_{i}\cap(B_{1},g_{i})
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region for some fixed smooth model
(S,\mathbf{C},\{(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}))\}_{\alpha})\in\mathcal{S}
; or
M_{i}\cap(A_{1,\rho_{i}},g_{i})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,2\beta)
-strong-cone region for some
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho_{i}\leq 1/2
.

We treat each case separately, but observe first that by definition of cone-/smooth-region, we have for each
i
:

\displaystyle d_{H}(M_{i}\cap A_{1,1-\sigma},\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1-\sigma})\leq c(\Lambda)(\beta+\gamma)\leq\epsilon,

\displaystyle\mathrm{sing}M_{i}\subset B_{1-\sigma},

provided we take
\beta(\Lambda,\sigma,\epsilon),\gamma(\Lambda,\sigma,\epsilon)
sufficiently small. So Theorem
8.1
(
5
) holds for
M_{i}
,
\mathbf{C}
in place of
M
,
\mathbf{C}_{v}
(resp.) for all
i
.

Case 1:
Every
M_{i}\cap(B_{1},g_{i})
is a
(S,\beta)
-smooth region. Let
O=B_{1}\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}/4}(y_{\alpha})
. Taking
\gamma(\Lambda,\sigma,\epsilon)
,
\beta(\Lambda,\epsilon)
sufficiently small, we can apply Lemma
8.4
to obtain a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
\psi_{i}:O\to O
satisfying

\displaystyle\psi_{i}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap O)=\overline{M_{i}}\cap O,\quad|D\psi_{i}|_{C^{0}(O\cap B_{r})}\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma,M_{1},r)\text{ for any }r<1

\displaystyle\psi_{i}|_{A_{1,1-\sigma}}\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)$-map},

\displaystyle\psi_{i}|_{A_{r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/4}(y_{\alpha})}\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},\epsilon)$-map, for each $\alpha$.}

If
\{\alpha\}=\emptyset
, then
O=B_{1}
, and we deduce a contradiction. This proves Case 1 when
N=1
.

By our inductive hypothesis, passing to a further subsequence as necessary, we can find for every
\alpha
a
g_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{G}
,
\hat{M}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(B_{1},g_{\alpha})
,
\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
x_{\alpha i}\in B_{\beta r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})
,
r_{\alpha i}\in(\frac{1}{2}r_{\alpha},(1+\beta)r_{\alpha})
, and local bi-Lipschitz maps
\hat{\phi}_{\alpha i}:B_{1}\to B_{1}
, so that Theorem
8.1
(
1
)-(
5
) hold with
\hat{\phi}_{\alpha i}
,
\hat{M}_{\alpha}
,
\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}
,
\eta_{x_{\alpha i},r_{\alpha i}}(M_{i})
,
C_{\mathcal{S},N-1,\mathcal{G}}
in place of
\phi
,
M_{v}
,
\mathbf{C}_{v}
,
M
,
C_{\mathcal{S},N,\mathcal{G}}
(respectively). We can moreover assume that the
\{r_{\alpha i}\}_{i}
converge, and hence assume
1-\beta\leq\frac{r_{\alpha i}}{r_{\alpha 1}}\leq 1+\beta
for for all
i
.

By virtue of
M_{i}\cap(B_{1},g_{i})
being a smooth region, if we ensure
\gamma(\Lambda)
,
\beta(\Lambda)
are sufficiently small and recall that
\epsilon_{S}\leq\min_{\alpha}r_{\alpha}
, then we can write

(104)

M_{i}\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/4}(y_{\alpha})=\cup_{j=1}^{m}G_{y_{\alpha}+\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(u_{\alpha ij})\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/8}(y_{\alpha})

for
C^{2}
functions
u_{\alpha ij}:(y_{\alpha}+\mathbf{C}_{\alpha})\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/8}(y_{\alpha})\to\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}^{\perp}
satisfying

(105)

r_{\alpha}^{-1}|u_{\alpha ij}|+|\nabla u_{\alpha ij}|+r_{\alpha}|\nabla^{2}u_{\alpha ij}|\leq c(\Lambda)(\gamma+\beta)\leq\epsilon_{1}(\Lambda)/100

where
\epsilon_{1}
as in Lemma
8.9
.

Ensure
\beta\leq 10^{-3}\sigma
, and let
\sigma^{\prime}=10^{-2}\sigma
,
r_{\alpha}^{\prime}=\frac{1-2\sigma^{\prime}}{1+5\sigma^{\prime}}\min\{r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha 1}\}
. Then we have

A_{\alpha}:=A_{(1+\sigma^{\prime})r_{\alpha}^{\prime},(1-4\sigma^{\prime})r_{\alpha}^{\prime}}(y_{\alpha})\subset A_{r,(1-\sigma)r}(z),

for any

and any

. Note in particular this holds for

and

.

From (

),(

), Theorem

(

), and the bounds

,

, we get

d_{H}(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}\cap B_{1},\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}\cap B_{1})\leq c(\Lambda)(\gamma+\beta+\epsilon).

Recalling that
\epsilon_{1}\leq\epsilon_{0}/4
(for

\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda)
as in the earlier discussion in this Section), and ensuring

\displaystyle\quad+r_{\alpha}^{\prime}|DX_{\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}}(x-x_{\alpha 1})-DX_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(x-y_{\alpha})|\leq\delta_{1}(\Lambda,\sigma^{\prime}).

Here

. Finally, if we set

r_{\alpha}^{\prime-1}|T_{\alpha i}-id|+|DT_{\alpha i}-Id|\leq\delta_{1}^{\prime}(\Lambda,\epsilon_{1}(\Lambda),\sigma^{\prime})\text{ on }B_{2r^{\prime}_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}),

where

as in Lemma

.

Now define the local bi-Lipschitz maps

by

The

. Moreover, from Theorem

(

\displaystyle T_{\alpha i}^{-1}\phi_{\alpha i}|_{A_{\alpha}}\text{ preserves the flow lines of $X_{\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}}(\cdot-x_{\alpha 1})$},

\displaystyle T_{\alpha i}^{-1}\phi_{\alpha i}|_{A_{\alpha}}=id\text{ outside }

\displaystyle B_{\epsilon r_{\alpha}}(x_{\alpha 1}+\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\subset B_{c(\Lambda)(\gamma+\beta+\epsilon)r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}+\mathbf{C}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\subset B_{\epsilon_{1}(\Lambda)r_{\alpha}^{\prime}/10}(y_{\alpha}+\mathbf{C}_{\alpha})

provided

are sufficiently small (depending only on

).

On the other hand, since

, by construction

The above discussion implies we can apply Lemma

to the maps

,
\phi_{\alpha i}
at scale

(with

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{1})=\overline{M_{i}}\cap B_{1},\quad\phi_{i}(\mathrm{sing}M_{1})=\mathrm{sing}M_{i}

\displaystyle\phi_{i}|_{B_{1}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{1}}\text{ is a $C^{2}$ diffeomorphism}

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{Lip}(\phi_{i}|_{B_{r}})\leq c(\Lambda,\sigma,M_{1},C_{\mathcal{S},N-1,\mathcal{G}},r)\text{ for }r<1.

This is a contradiction, and therefore completes the proof of Case 1.

Case 2:
Every

for all

\displaystyle M_{i}\cap A_{1,\rho_{i}/8}=\cup_{j=1}^{m}G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{ij}\nu_{\mathbf{C}})\cap A_{1,\rho_{i}}

for

\displaystyle|x|^{-1}|u_{ij}|+|\nabla u_{ij}|+|x||\nabla^{2}u_{ij}|\leq 2\beta,\quad u_{i1}<u_{i2}<\ldots<u_{im}.

Let us ensure that

for

any fixed constant sufficiently large so that Remark

Suppose first that

\displaystyle\psi_{i}|_{A_{1,1/8}}\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)$-map.}

This is a contradiction, and proves Case 2 when

.

Let us assume now
\rho_{i}>0
for all

. Passing to a further subsequence, by our inductive hypothesis we can find a

,

,

\displaystyle\phi_{i}|_{B_{\rho_{1}}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{1}}\text{ is a $C^{2}$ diffeomorphism,}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\phi_{i}|_{B_{r\rho_{1}}})\leq\rho_{1}^{-1}C_{\mathcal{S},N-1,\mathcal{G}}(r)\text{ for every $r<1$, independent of $i$},

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{1}}{\rho_{i}}\phi_{i}|_{A_{\rho_{1},(1-\sigma)\rho_{1}}}\text{ is a $(\hat{\mathbf{C}},\epsilon)$-map.}

Define

by

. From Lemma

, we can find

Using the

Let

and

(110)

(111)

9.
Global finiteness

In this section we prove Theorem
2.4
. Here we shall work in
(N^{8},g)
, for
N^{8}
a closed Riemannian
8
-manifold with
C^{3}
metric
g
. We need to extend some of our definitions to be adapted to global geometry. Recall that if
M
is an embedded
C^{2}
hypersurface of
(N,g)
, then
T^{\perp_{g}}M
denotes the normal bundle of
M
in
(N,g)
, and
\nabla^{g}
is the connection induced by
g
on
T^{\perp_{g}}M
. We identity each
T_{x}^{\perp_{g}}M
with a subspace of
T_{x}N
in the obvious fashion. We write
C^{k}(M,M^{\perp})
for the space of
C^{k}
sections of this bundle, and define
|u|_{C^{k}(N,g)}
in terms of
\nabla^{g}
. Given
u\in C^{k}(M\cap U,M^{\perp_{g}})
, write
\mathrm{graph}_{M,g}(u)=\{\exp_{N}(u(x)):x\in U\cap M\}
. We define convergence of hypersurfaces
M_{i}\to M
in the same way as in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, except with the notion of
\mathrm{graph}_{M,g}
defined here.

In direct analogy with the set up of Section
4
, a
7
-varifold
V
in
N
is now a measure on the Grassmann bundle
G_{7}(TN)
. We define
\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(N,g)
to be the set of stationary integral
7
-varifolds
V
in
(N,g)
with
\mathrm{sing}V
consisting of a discrete collection of points. The various special cases and constructions (e.g.
[M]_{g}
,
f_{\sharp}V
,
\delta_{g}V
, etc.) extend in an obvious fashion to general
(N,g)
. Lemmas
4.1
,
4.2
,
4.4
continue to hold for
(N,g)
in place of
(U,g)
, but if one prefers one could break
N
into finitely-many coordinate neighborhoods, and apply the Lemmas in each one. Let us write
\theta_{V,g}(x,r)=\omega_{7}^{-1}r^{-7}\mu_{V}(B_{r}^{g}(x))
for the density ratio of
V
w.r.t.
g
-geodesic balls, and if
M
is a
C^{1}
embedded hypersurface,
\theta_{M,g}(x,r)=\theta_{[M]_{g},g}(x,r)
.

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

One could prove this Theorem by first constructing a closed analogue of a cone decomposition, as we did with Theorem
2.1
, but since we are only interested in the parameterization it will be less cumbersome to prove it directly by contradiction.

We first make a general note about minimal surfaces in
(N,g)
. Since
N
is closed, we can find numbers
R(N,g),\eta(N,g)\in(0,1]
so that if
g^{\prime}
is any
C^{2}
metric on
N
satisfying
|g-g^{\prime}|_{C^{2}(N,g)}\leq\eta
, then every ball
(B_{R}(x),g^{\prime})
is isometric to some
(B_{R}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{8},g^{\prime}_{x})
, for
g^{\prime}_{x}
a
C^{2}
metric on
B_{R}(0)
satisfying
|g^{\prime}_{x}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{2}(B_{R}(0))}\leq\min\{c_{0}^{-1},10^{-2}\}
,
c_{0}
as in (
18
). In particular, for
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(N,g^{\prime})
we have the bounds

\theta_{V,g^{\prime}}(x,r)\leq 2\theta_{V,g^{\prime}}(x,R)\leq c_{2}(N,g)\mu_{V}(N)\quad\forall x\in N,r\in(0,R).

So by (
19
),

(112)

V\neq 0\iff\mu_{V}(N)\geq\frac{1}{2c_{2}}\iff\mathrm{spt}V\neq\emptyset,

and

(113)

\mu_{V}(N)\leq\Lambda\implies\theta_{V,g^{\prime}}(x,r)\leq\Lambda^{\prime}:=c_{2}\Lambda\quad\forall x\in N,\forall r<R.

Let
\sigma=\frac{1}{100(1+I)}
, and ensure
\epsilon\leq\min\{\epsilon_{1}(\Lambda^{\prime}),10^{-2}\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda^{\prime})\}
, for
\epsilon_{1}
as in Lemma
8.9
and
\epsilon_{0}
as in the discussion of Section
8
. We will choose constants
\delta^{\prime},\beta,\epsilon>0
as we progress, along with several choices, which a posteriori can all be fixed, but to guide the reader our dependencies look like the following (
\to
meaning “depends on”):

i_{0}\to\epsilon_{M}\to\{B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha}\to M,\delta^{\prime},\beta,\epsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}\to\beta\to\epsilon\to\sigma,\Lambda^{\prime},I.

Suppose the theorem fails. Then for every
i
large there is a set of
C^{3}
metric
\mathcal{G}_{i}
on
N
satisfying
|g^{\prime}-g|_{C^{3}(N,g)}\leq 1/i
for all
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{i}
, such that we can find sequences
\{g_{ij}\}_{j}\subset\mathcal{G}_{i}
,
\{M_{ij}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(N,g_{ij})\}_{j}
, satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g_{ij}}(M_{ij})\leq\Lambda
,
\mathrm{index}(M_{ij},N,g_{ij})\leq I
, with the property that for every
j^{\prime}>j
there does not exist a bi-Lipschitz mapping
\phi:(N,g)\to(N,g)
satisfying Theorem
2.4
(
1
)-(
2
)-(
3
) with
M_{ij}
,
M_{ij^{\prime}}
,
j^{\prime}
in place of
M_{v}
,
M
,
C
. As before, this contradiction hypothesis is preserved if we replace
j
with any subsequence of
j
.

If
M_{ij}=M_{ij^{\prime}}=\emptyset
then trivially such a
\phi
exists, so by (
112
) after discarding finitely-many terms in each sequence we must have
\mathcal{H}^{7}_{g_{i}}(M_{ij})\geq\frac{1}{2c_{2}}
for all
i
,
j
. After passing to subsequences in
j
, we can assume there are
C^{2}
metrics
g_{i}
(satisfying
|g_{i}-g|_{C^{2}(N,g)}\leq 1/i
) so that
g_{ij}\to g_{i}
in
C^{2}(N,g)
, and (by Lemma
4.4
) there are non-zero varifolds
V_{i}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(N,g_{i})
, and discrete sets
\mathcal{I}_{i}
consisting of at most
I
points, so that
[M_{ij}]_{g_{ij}}\to V_{i}
as varifolds in
(N,g)
, and in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
N\setminus(\mathrm{sing}V_{i}\cup\mathcal{I}_{i})
. Moreover, we have
\mu_{V_{i}}(N)\leq\Lambda
, and
\mathrm{index}(V_{i},N,g_{i})\leq I
.

Trivially
g_{i}\to g
in
C^{2}(N,g)
, so passing to a subsequence in
i
we find a non-zero
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(N,g)
, and at most
I
points
\mathcal{I}
, so that
V_{i}\to V
as varifolds in
(N,g)
, and in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
N\setminus(\mathrm{sing}V\cup\mathcal{I})
. Moreover, since we cannot have points
x_{i}\in\mathrm{sing}V_{i}
converging to a point in
\mathrm{reg}V\setminus\mathcal{I}
, and
N
is compact, after enlarging
\mathcal{I}
to consist of possibly
2I
points, we can assume that for every
\tau>0
there is an
i_{1}
so that
\mathrm{sing}V_{i}\cup\mathcal{I}_{i}\subset B_{\tau}^{g}(\mathrm{sing}V\cup\mathcal{I})
for all
i>i_{1}
.
V
satisfies
\mu_{V}(N)\leq\Lambda
,
\mathrm{index}(V,N,g)\leq I
.

Write
\mathrm{sing}V\cup\mathcal{I}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{d}\}\subset\mathrm{spt}V
, and take
\delta^{\prime},\epsilon,\beta>0
(for the moment) arbitrary. By working in normal coordinates centered at each
y_{\alpha}
, by Lemmas
4.1
,
4.4
, (
18
), (
113
), Theorem
6.3
, Remark
6.4
, and the maximum principle
[
Ilm96
]
, we can choose radii
r_{\alpha}\in(0,1/8)
, integers
m_{\alpha}
, and cones
\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}
, satisfying
m_{\alpha}\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(0)\leq\Lambda^{\prime}
, so that:

A.1

the balls
\{B^{g}_{8r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha}
are disjoint, and each
(B^{g}_{8r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha}),g)
is isometric via normal coordinates to (and will henceforth be identified with)
(B_{8r_{\alpha}}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\alpha})
for some
C^{3}
metric
g_{\alpha}
satisfying
|g_{\alpha}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{8r_{\alpha}}(0),g_{\mathrm{eucl}})}\leq\delta^{\prime}/2
;

A.2

\mathrm{spt}V\cap(A_{8r_{\alpha},0}(0),g_{\alpha})
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},1,\beta/2)
-strong-cone region;

A.3

for each
\alpha
, we have

(114)

\displaystyle d_{H}(\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{r_{\alpha}},\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}\cap B_{r_{\alpha}})\leq(\delta^{\prime}/2)r_{\alpha}

(115)

\displaystyle\theta_{V}(0,r_{\alpha}/2)\geq(m_{\alpha}-1/4)\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(0),\quad\theta_{V}(0,r_{\alpha})\leq(m_{\alpha}+1/4)\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}.

Let
O=N\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{2r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})
,
O^{\prime}=N\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})
, and then we can write
V\llcorner O^{\prime}=n_{1}[S_{1}]+\ldots+n_{p}[S_{p}]
for
n_{s}\in\mathbb{N}
, and the
S_{s}
being a
C^{2}
, disjoint, closed (as sets), embedded minimal hypersurface in
(O^{\prime},g)
. For each
S_{s}
, define the map
G_{s}:T^{\perp_{g}}S_{s}\to N
by
G_{s}(x,v)=\exp_{N}|_{x}(v)
. By
A.2
, we can choose an
0<\epsilon_{M}\leq\epsilon\min\{1,\min_{\alpha}r_{\alpha}\}
so that each
G_{s}
is a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism from
\{(x,v)\in T^{\perp_{g}}S_{s}:|v|<4\epsilon_{M}\}
onto its image
O_{s}
, and
O_{s}\supset B^{g}_{4\epsilon_{M}}(\mathrm{spt}V)\cap O
, and
G_{s}
admits the bound
|DG_{s}|\leq 2
, and all the images
O_{s}
are disjoint.

Let us ensure
\delta^{\prime}(I,\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,\sigma)
is smaller than the constant
\delta_{l,I}(l,I,\gamma=\beta,\sigma)
from Theorem
7.1
, for
l
chosen so that
\tilde{\theta}_{l}\geq\Lambda^{\prime}
. We can then fix an
i_{0}
, depending on
\delta^{\prime},\epsilon_{M},\beta,V,\{B_{r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha}
, and pass to a subsequence in
j
, so that for each
j
we have:

B.1

M_{i_{0}j}\subset B^{g}_{\epsilon_{M}}(\mathrm{spt}V)
;

B.2

in each
(B_{8r_{\alpha}}(0),g_{\alpha})
, the metric
g_{i_{0}j}
satisfies
|g_{i_{0}j}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{8r_{\alpha}}(0),g_{\mathrm{eucl}})}\leq\delta^{\prime}
;

B.3

for every
\alpha
,
M_{i_{0}j}\cap(A_{8r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}/8},g_{\alpha})
is a
(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},m_{\alpha},\beta)
-strong-cone region;

B.4

we have for every
\alpha
:

(116)

\displaystyle d_{H}(M_{i_{0}j}\cap B_{r_{\alpha}},\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}\cap B_{r_{\alpha}})\leq\delta^{\prime}r_{\alpha}

(117)

\displaystyle\theta_{M_{i_{0}j},g_{i_{0}j}}(0,r_{\alpha}/2)\geq(m_{\alpha}-1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}(0),\quad\theta_{M_{i_{0}j},g_{i_{0}j}}(0,r_{\alpha})\leq(m_{\alpha}+1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}};

B.5

for each
s=1,\ldots,p
, there are
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{jsk}:S_{s}\to S_{s}^{\perp_{g}}\}_{k=1}^{n_{s}}
so that

M_{i_{0}j}\cap O_{s}=\cup_{k=1}^{n_{s}}\{G_{s}(x,u_{jsk}(x)):x\in S_{s}\},\quad|u_{jsk}|_{C^{2}(S_{s})}\leq\epsilon_{M}.

Having fixed this
i_{0}
, let us for simplicity drop the
i
notation, think only of the sequence
M_{j}\equiv M_{i_{0}j}
and
g_{j}\equiv g_{i_{0}j}
. We can forget all the other sequences. We shall build bi-Lipschitz maps
\phi_{j}:(N,g)\to(N,g)
with uniform Lipschitz constant taking
\overline{M_{1}}\to\overline{M_{j}}
, which will contradict our choice of
M_{j}
.

By
B.2
,
B.4
, and since every
r_{\alpha}\leq 1
, we can apply Theorem
7.1
to each
[M_{j}]_{g_{j}}
in each ball
(B_{r_{\alpha}},g_{j})
, to deduce there is a finite collection of
(\Lambda^{\prime},\sigma,\beta)
-smooth regions
\mathcal{S}
and an integer
K
so that for every
\alpha
and
j
, there is a radius
r_{\alpha j}\in(\frac{4}{5}r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha})
so that
M_{j}\cap(B_{r_{\alpha j}},g_{j})
admits a
(\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,\mathcal{S},K)
-strong-cone decomposition. From
B.3
, we deduce that each
M_{j}\cap(B_{2r_{\alpha}},g_{j})
admits a
(\Lambda^{\prime},\beta,\mathcal{S},K+1)
-strong-cone decomposition.

Assuming
\beta(\Lambda^{\prime},\epsilon,\sigma)
is small, we can apply Theorem
8.1
, pass to a subsequence in
j
, and obtain bi-Lipschitz maps
\phi_{\alpha j}:B_{2r_{\alpha}}\to B_{2r_{\alpha}}
, cones
\{\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}^{\prime}
, and an increasing function
C:[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
(independent of
\alpha,j
) satisfying the properties:

C.1

\phi_{\alpha j}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{2r_{\alpha}})=\overline{M_{j}}\cap B_{2r_{\alpha}}
, and
\phi_{\alpha j}(\mathrm{sing}M_{1}\cap B_{2r_{\alpha}})=\mathrm{sing}M_{j}\cap B_{2r_{\alpha}}
;

C.2

\phi_{\alpha j}|_{B_{2r_{\alpha}}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{1}}
is a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism;

C.3

\mathrm{Lip}_{g_{\alpha}}(\phi_{\alpha j}|_{B_{2r_{\alpha}\rho}})\leq C(\rho)
for every
\rho<1
;

C.4

\phi_{\alpha j}|_{A_{2r_{\alpha},2r_{\alpha}(1-\sigma)}}
is a
(\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha},\epsilon)
-map;

C.5

d_{H}(\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},2r_{\alpha}(1-\sigma)},M_{j}\cap A_{2r_{\alpha},2r_{\alpha}(1-\sigma)})\leq 2r_{\alpha}\epsilon
and
\mathrm{sing}M_{j}\cap B_{2r_{\alpha}}\subset B_{2r_{\alpha}(1-\sigma)}
.

We now work towards gluing these
\phi_{\alpha j}
together.

We first build our large-scale diffeomorphisms. Define
g_{sj}:T^{\perp_{g}}S_{s}\to T^{\perp_{g}}S_{s}
as follows. Given any
x\in S_{s}
, there is a neighborhood
U\subset S_{s}
containing
x
, in which the normal bundle
T^{\perp_{g}}(U\cap S_{s})
is isometric to a trivialization
(U\cap S_{s})\times\mathbb{R}\ni(x,t)
. Under this identification, we can think of the
u_{jsk}
as taking values in
(-4\epsilon_{M},4\epsilon_{M})
, and so by the maximum principle and the smallness of the singular set of
M_{j}
we can assume

u_{js1}<\ldots<u_{jsm_{s}}\quad\forall j=1,2,\ldots,\forall s=1,\ldots,p.

Let
f_{\{a_{j}\}_{j},\{b_{j}\}_{j}}(t)
be the function from Lemma
12.1
with
m_{s}
in place of
k
and
\epsilon_{M}
in place of
\epsilon
. For
(x,t)\in(U\cap S_{j})\times\mathbb{R}\cong T^{\perp_{g}}(U\cap S_{j})
, we define

g_{sj}(x,t)=(x,f_{\{u_{1sk}(x)\}_{k},\{u_{jsk}(x)\}_{k}}(t)),

so that
g_{sj}(x,u_{1sk}(x))=(x,u_{jsk}(x))
for every
k=1,2,\ldots,m_{j}
. Trivially,
g_{sj}
is a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
(U\cap S_{s})\times(-4\epsilon_{M},4\epsilon_{M})
which coincides with the identity for
|t|\geq 2\epsilon_{M}
. By Remark
12.1
, this definition is independent of our choice of trivialization, and so gives rise a diffeomorphism from
T^{\perp_{g}}S_{s}
to itself. By Item
A.3
, the number

D=\inf\{|u_{1sk}(x)-u_{1sk^{\prime}}(x)|:x\in S_{s},k\neq k^{\prime},s=1,\ldots,p\}>0,

and hence by Lemma
12.1
we have the uniform
C^{1}
bound
|Dg_{sj}|\leq c(N,V,M_{1})
.

Now define
\psi_{j}:(O,g)\to(N,g)
by setting

\psi_{j}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}G_{s}^{-1}\circ g_{sj}\circ G_{s}&x\in O_{s}\\
x&x\not\in O_{s}\end{array}\right..

By our construction and by
B.1
,
\psi_{j}
is a well-defined
C^{2}
diffeomorphism onto its image, satisfying

\psi_{j}(M_{1}\cap O)=M_{j}\cap\psi_{j}(O),\quad|\psi_{j}(x)-x|\leq 10\epsilon_{M},\quad|D\psi_{j}|\leq c(N,V,M_{1}).

For each
\alpha
, set
S_{\alpha}^{\prime}=\mathrm{spt}V\cap A_{8r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}}
. Let
\nu_{\alpha}
be a choice of unit normal for
S_{\alpha}^{\prime}
. By
A.2
, Theorem
5.1
and our choice of
\epsilon_{M}
, provided
\delta^{\prime}(\Lambda^{\prime}),\beta(\Lambda^{\prime})
are sufficiently small, the function
G_{\alpha}:S_{\alpha}^{\prime}\times(-4\epsilon_{M},4\epsilon_{M})\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
given by
G_{\alpha}(x,v)=\exp_{g_{\alpha}}|_{x}(t\nu_{\alpha}(x))
is a well-defined
C^{2}
diffeomorphism onto its image. If we let
X_{\alpha}:=DG_{\alpha}(\partial_{t})
, then after shrinking
\delta^{\prime}(\Lambda^{\prime}),\beta(\Lambda^{\prime})
and flipping
\nu_{\alpha}
as necessary,
X_{\alpha}
is a
C^{2}
vector field on
B_{3\epsilon_{M}}(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha})\cap A_{4r_{\alpha},2r_{\alpha}}
satisfying

|X_{\alpha}-X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}|+r_{\alpha}|DX_{\alpha}-X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}|\leq c(\Lambda^{\prime})(\beta+\delta^{\prime}).

Ensuring
\beta(\Lambda^{\prime},\epsilon)
,
\delta^{\prime}(\Lambda^{\prime},\epsilon)
are small, and recalling our restriction
\epsilon_{M}\leq\epsilon r_{\alpha}
, we can argue as in Lemma
8.4
and obtain
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms
\psi_{\alpha j}:(A_{4r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}},g_{\alpha})\to(B_{8r_{\alpha}},g_{\alpha})
such that

(118)

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha j}(M_{1}\cap A_{4r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}})=M_{j}\cap\psi_{\alpha j}(A_{4r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}}),\quad|D\psi_{\alpha j}|\leq c(N,V,M_{1}),

(119)

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha j}|_{A_{4r_{\alpha},3r_{\alpha}}}=\psi_{j},\quad\psi_{\alpha j}|_{A_{2r_{\alpha},r_{\alpha}}}\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha},\epsilon)$-map}.

We use the
\psi_{\alpha j}
to transition from our large-scale
\psi_{j}
, and the small-scale maps
\phi_{\alpha j}
arising from the cone decompositions of
M_{i}
in each ball
B_{2r_{\alpha}}
.

Let
\sigma^{\prime}=10^{-2}\sigma
, and
r_{\alpha}^{\prime}=(1+5\sigma^{\prime})^{-1}r_{\alpha}
. Then we have

A_{\alpha}:=A_{2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(1+\sigma^{\prime}),2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(1-4\sigma^{\prime})}\subset A_{2r_{\alpha},2r_{\alpha}(1-\sigma)}.

A.3
,
C.4
,
C.5
imply that
d_{H}(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}\cap B_{1},\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}\cap B_{1})\leq c(\Lambda^{\prime})(\epsilon+\beta)
, and so by ensuring
\beta(\Lambda^{\prime},\sigma),\epsilon(\Lambda^{\prime},\sigma)
are sufficiently small Theorem
5.1
implies that both
X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}
and
X_{\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}}
are defined on
B_{2\epsilon_{1}r_{\alpha}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}_{\alpha})\cap A_{\alpha}
and satisfy

|X_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}-X_{\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}}|+r_{\alpha}^{\prime}|DX_{\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}}-X_{\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}}|\leq\delta_{1}(\Lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}).

Here
\epsilon_{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})
,
\delta_{1}(\Lambda^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
are the constants from Lemma
8.9
.

Combined with (
118
), (
119
),
C.3
,
C.4
,
B.3
, and our choice of
A_{\alpha}
, we can therefore apply Lemma
8.9
(at scale
B_{2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}}
and with
\sigma^{\prime},\epsilon_{1}
in place of
\sigma,\epsilon
) to obtain
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms
\hat{\phi}_{\alpha j}:(A_{\alpha},g_{\alpha})\to(A_{\alpha},g_{\alpha})
satisfying

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}_{\alpha j}(M_{1}\cap A_{\alpha})=M_{j}\cap A_{\alpha},\quad|D\hat{\phi}_{\alpha j}|\leq c(N,V,M_{1}),

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}_{\alpha j}|_{A_{2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(1+\sigma^{\prime}),2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}}}=\psi_{\alpha j},\quad\hat{\phi}_{\alpha j}|_{A_{2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(1-3\sigma^{\prime}),2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(1-4\sigma^{\prime})}}=\phi_{\alpha j}.

We now define
\phi_{j}
by setting

\phi_{j}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\psi_{j}(x)&x\in N\setminus\cup_{\alpha}B_{3r_{\alpha}}(y_{\alpha})\\
\psi_{\alpha j}(x)&x\in A_{4r_{\alpha},2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}}\\
\hat{\phi}_{\alpha j}(x)&x\in A_{\alpha}\\
\phi_{\alpha j}(x)&x\in B_{2r_{\alpha}^{\prime}(1-3\sigma^{\prime})}\end{array}\right.

By
B.1
and our construction, the
\phi_{j}
are bi-Lipschitz maps
(N,g)\to(N,g)
satisfying

\displaystyle\phi_{j}(\overline{M_{1}})=\overline{M_{j}},\quad\phi_{j}(\mathrm{sing}M_{1})=\mathrm{sing}M_{j},

\displaystyle\phi_{j}|_{N\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{1}}\text{ is a $C^{2}$ diffeomorphism},\quad\mathrm{Lip}_{g}(\phi_{j})\leq c(N,V,M_{1}).

This is a contradiction, and finishes the proof of Theorem
2.4
.
∎

10.
Other corollaries

Here we prove Theorems
2.1
and
2.6
. Theorem
2.1
is a trivial consequence of Theorems
7.1
,
8.1
, but we include a proof for the sake of precision. We work in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

We first note that the existence of
V
,
\mathcal{I}
, and convergence
M_{i}\to V
, follows directly from Lemma
4.4
.

A straightforward contradiction argument, using Theorem
5.1
, Lemma
4.4
, and the constancy theorem implies that provided
\delta(\Lambda,I,\sigma)
is sufficiently small, we can find integers
m_{i}\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
so that
m_{i}\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\Lambda
, and

\theta_{[M_{i}]_{g_{i}}}(0,1-\sigma)\leq(m_{i}+1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0),\quad\theta_{[M_{i}]_{g_{i}}}(0,(1-\sigma)/2)\geq(m_{i}-1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0).

Passing to a subsequence, there no loss in assuming
m_{i}\equiv m
. If
m=0
, then provided we take
\delta^{\prime}(\Lambda,\sigma)
small (
19
) implies
M_{i}\cap B_{1-2\sigma}=\emptyset
for all
i
(and hence
\mu_{V}(B_{1-2\sigma})=\emptyset
), so we can just take
r=1-2\sigma
,
\phi_{i}\equiv id
.

By Theorem
7.1
, choosing
\delta^{\prime}(\Lambda,I,\beta,\sigma)
small, we can find a finite collection
\mathcal{S}
of
(\Lambda,\beta,5\sigma)
-smooth models, and a constant
K(\Lambda,I,\beta,\sigma)
, so that for all
i>>1
there are radii
r_{i}\in(1-200\sigma(I+1),1)
so that
M_{i}\cap(B_{r_{i}}(0),g_{i})
admits a
(\Lambda,\beta,\mathcal{S},K)
-cone decomposition. Passing to a subsequence, we can assume
r_{i}\to(1+\sigma)r
.

Trivially
\mathcal{S}
are also
(\Lambda,\beta,3\sigma)
-smooth models, and so for every
i>>1
and
r^{\prime}\in((1-\sigma)r,(1+\sigma)r)
, each
M_{i}\cap(B_{r^{\prime}}(0),g_{i})
admits a
(\Lambda,\beta,\mathcal{S},K)
-cone decomposition (though not explicitly stated, the outer-most region in the decomposition of Theorem
7.1
is always a smooth region). This proves the first assertion.

To prove existence of the
\phi_{i}
, we apply Theorem
8.1
at scale
B_{(1+\sigma)r}(0)
(with
3\sigma
in place of
\sigma
,
\epsilon>0
arbitrary), and deduce that after passing to a further subsequence, we can find a constant
C
, a cone
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
, and local bi-Lipschitz maps
\phi_{i}:B_{(1+\sigma)r}\to B_{(1+\sigma)r}
satisfying

(120)

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{(1+\sigma)r})=\overline{M_{i}},\quad\phi_{i}(\mathrm{sing}M_{1}\cap B_{(1+\sigma)r})=\mathrm{sing}M_{i},

(121)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\phi_{i}|_{B_{r}})\leq C,

(122)

\displaystyle\phi_{i}|_{A_{(1+\sigma)r,(1-2\sigma)r}}\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)$-map},\quad\mathrm{sing}M_{i}\subset B_{(1-2\sigma)r}.

(
122
) implies
|\phi_{i}(x)|=|x|
for
x\in A_{(1+\sigma)r,(1-2\sigma)r}
, and so we can restrict
\phi_{i}|_{B_{r}}
to obtain our required maps.

Passing to one last subsequence, we can assume there is a Lipschitz
\phi_{\infty}:B_{r}\to B_{r}
so that
\phi_{i}\to\phi_{\infty}
in
C^{\alpha}(B_{r})
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Since
|\phi_{i}(x)|=|x|
for
|x|\in((1-2\sigma)r,r)
, we have for all
i
:

(123)

\max\{|\phi_{i}^{-1}(x)|,|\phi_{i}(x)|\}\leq\max\{(1-2\sigma)r,|x|\},\quad|\phi_{\infty}(x)|\leq\max\{(1-2\sigma)r,|x|\}.

Varifold convergence
[M_{i}]_{g_{i}}\to V
in
B_{1}
, (
19
), and (
123
) imply
\phi_{\infty}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{r})\subset\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{r}
. On the other hand, again from (
19
) and varifold convergence, if
x\in\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{r}
, then there is a sequence
x_{i}\in\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{r}
so that
\phi_{i}(x_{i})\to x
. By (
123
) and since
\overline{M_{1}}
is closed, there is no loss in assuming that
x_{i}\to x^{\prime}\in\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{r}
. The uniform Lipschitz bound (
121
) then implies
\phi_{\infty}(x^{\prime})=x
. This proves
\phi_{\infty}(\overline{M_{1}}\cap B_{r})=\mathrm{spt}V\cap B_{r}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

I first claim that given
\beta>0
there is an
\eta(\Lambda,\beta)
so that if
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{10},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
satisfies

(124)

\theta_{V}(0,10)\leq\Lambda,\quad\theta_{V}(0,10)-\theta_{V}(0,\eta)\leq\eta,\quad\mathrm{index}(V,A_{10,\eta},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})=0,

then we can find
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\Lambda
, and
C^{2}
functions
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{8,1}\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}\}
, so that

(125)

V\llcorner A_{8,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}[G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{j})\cap A_{8,1}],\quad|u_{j}|_{C^{2}}\leq\beta.

Suppose otherwise: then there are sequences
\eta_{i}\to 0
, and
V_{i}\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{10},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
, satisfying (
124
), but failing (
125
) for any
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\Lambda
. By Lemma
4.4
, we can find a
V\in\mathcal{ISV}_{7}(B_{10},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
with
\theta_{V}(0,10)\leq\Lambda
,
\mathrm{index}(V,B_{10},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})=0
, so that
M_{i}\to M
as varifolds in
B_{10}
, and
V_{i}\to V
smoothly on compact subsets of
A_{10,0}
. By (
17
),
V
is dilation-invariant and stable, and hence
V=m[\mathbf{C}]\llcorner B_{10}
for some
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}
, and
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)=\theta_{V}(0,10)\leq\Lambda
. Smooth convergence then implies (
125
) holds for
i>>1
, which is a contradiction. This proves my first claim.

I next claim that given any
\beta,\delta>0
, and
M\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
satisfying (
3
), then we can find a positive
R_{M}\in\mathbb{R}
, a
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, and an
m\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\Lambda
, so that the following is true:

(126)

\displaystyle d_{H}(\mathrm{spt}M\cap B_{R_{M}/2},\mathbf{C}\cap B_{R_{M}/2})\leq\delta R_{M}/2

(127)

\displaystyle(m-1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\theta_{M}(0,R_{M}/4)\quad\theta_{M}(0,R_{M}/2)\leq(m+1/2)\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)

(128)

\displaystyle M\cap(A_{\infty,R_{M}},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)$-strong-cone region.}

By Lemma
4.4
and (
17
), we can take
m[\mathbf{C}]
to be any (the) tangent cone at infinity for
M
. More precisely, there are radii
R_{i}\to\infty
so that
[\eta_{0,R_{i},\sharp}(M)]\to m[\mathbf{C}]
as varifolds and in the local Hausdorff distance, and smoothly with multiplicity-
m
on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus\{0\}
. In particular, (
126
), (
127
) hold for
R=R_{i}
for any choice of
R_{i}
large.

Choose
\beta^{\prime}(\Lambda)\leq\beta(\Lambda)
sufficiently small so that if
\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
satisfy
d_{H}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}\cap B_{1})\leq 2\beta^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
(as guaranteed by Theorem
5.1
). Choose
\eta(\Lambda,\beta^{\prime})
as in Claim 1, and now taking
R
sufficiently large so that
\theta_{M}(0,\infty)-\theta_{M}(0,R)\leq\eta
,
\mathrm{index}(M,A_{\infty,R},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})=0
, it follows from Claim 1 that
M\cap(A_{\infty,8R/\eta},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta^{\prime})
-cone region. It then follows by Theorem
6.3
, provided
\beta^{\prime}(\Lambda,\beta)
is sufficiently small, that
M\cap(A_{\infty,R_{i}},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
is a
(\mathbf{C},m,\beta)
-strong-cone region for all
i>>1
. This proves my second claim.

Let
\sigma=\frac{1}{100(I+1)}
, and let
2\delta=\delta_{l,I}(l,I,\gamma=\beta,\sigma)
be the constant from Theorem
7.1
, with
l
chosen so that
\tilde{\theta}_{l}\geq\Lambda
. Let
R_{M}
be the radius from Claim 2 with this choice of
\delta
, and
\beta>0
to be determined later. It will suffice to prove Theorem
2.6
with
\lambda=R_{M}^{-1}
.

Suppose, towards a contradiction, Theorem
2.6
fails. Then there is a sequence of
M_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{7}(\mathbb{R}^{8},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
satisfying (
3
) and normalized so that
R_{M_{i}}=1
, and a radius
\rho
, with the property that for any
i^{\prime}>i
, there does not exist a local bi-Lipschitz
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{8}\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
satisfying

\displaystyle\phi(\overline{M_{i}})=\overline{M_{i^{\prime}}},\quad\phi(\mathrm{sing}M_{i})=\mathrm{sing}M_{i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\phi|_{\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{i}}\text{ is a $C^{2}$ diffeomorphism},\quad\mathrm{Lip}(\phi|_{B_{\rho}})\leq i^{\prime}.

By our normalization, we have for each
i
an
m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
satisfying
m_{i}\theta_{\mathbf{C}_{i}}(0)\leq\Lambda
, so that (
126
), (
127
), (
128
) hold with
M_{i}
,
1
,
m_{i}
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}
in place of
M
,
R_{M}
,
m
,
\mathbf{C}
. By Theorem
5.1
, after passing to a subsequence, we can assume there is a fixed
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
(with
m\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)\leq\Lambda
), so that (
126
), (
127
), (
128
) hold with
M_{i}
,
1
,
2\delta
,
2\beta
in place of
M
,
R_{m}
,
\delta
,
\beta
.

By our choice of
\delta
, we can therefore apply Theorem
7.1
to each
M_{i}
to deduce there is a finite set of
(\Lambda,\sigma,\beta)
-smooth models
\mathcal{S}
, and a constant
K
, and radii
r_{i}\in(4/10,1/2)
, so that each
M_{i}\cap(B_{r_{i}},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
admits a
(\Lambda,\beta,\mathcal{S},K)
-strong-cone decomposition. From (
128
), we get that each
M_{i}\cap(B_{1},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
admits a
(\Lambda,\beta,\mathcal{S},K+1)
-strong-cone decomposition. Ensuring
\beta(\Lambda,\sigma,\epsilon)
is sufficiently small and passing to a subsequence, from Theorem
8.1
we can find a cone
\hat{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
, and local bi-Lipschitz maps
\hat{\phi}_{i}:B_{1}\to B_{1}
, satisfying Theorem
8.1
(
1
)-(
5
) with
M_{1},M_{i},\hat{\mathbf{C}}
in place of
M_{v},M,\mathbf{C}_{v}
.

On the other hand, ensuring
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}(\Lambda)/8
, we can apply Lemma
8.5
to obtain
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms
\psi_{i}:A_{\infty,1/4}\to A_{\infty,1/4}
satisfying

\displaystyle\psi_{i}(M_{1}\cap A_{\infty,1/4})=M_{i}\cap A_{\infty,1/4},\quad\psi_{i}|_{A_{1,1/4}}\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)$-map },

\displaystyle|D\psi_{i}|_{C^{0}(A_{R,1/2})}\leq c(R,M_{1})\text{ for every $R\in(1/2,\infty)$}.

If
m=1
, then Lemma
8.5
implies
|D\psi_{i}|\leq C
on all of
A_{\infty,1/4}
.

Theorem
8.1
(
5
) and (
128
) imply that
d_{H}(\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1},\hat{\mathbf{C}}\cap B_{1})\leq c(\Lambda)(\beta+\epsilon)
. Therefore, arguing like we did in the proof of Theorem
2.4
, provided
\beta(\Lambda,\sigma),\epsilon(\Lambda,\sigma)
are sufficiently small, we can apply Lemma
8.9
to the maps
\hat{\phi}_{i}
,
\psi_{i}
, to obtain local bi-Lipschitz
\phi_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{8}\to\mathbb{R}^{8}
satisfying

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(\overline{M_{1}})=\overline{M_{i}},\quad\phi_{i}(\mathrm{sing}M_{1})=\mathrm{sing}M_{i},\quad\phi_{i}|_{\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus\mathrm{sing}M_{1}}\text{ is a $C^{2}$ diffeomorphism},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lip}(\phi_{i}|_{B_{R}})\leq C(R)\text{ for every $R\in\mathbb{R}$},

\displaystyle\phi_{i}|_{B_{1-10\sigma^{\prime}}}=\hat{\phi}_{i},\quad\phi_{i}|_{\mathbb{R}^{8}\setminus B_{1-\sigma^{\prime}}}=\psi_{i},

where
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma/100
, and
C:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is an increasing function. This is a contradiction.
∎

11.
Appendix 1: a smallness estimate

In this section we prove an estimate which is a slight variation on Simon’s asymptotic decay Theorem
[
Sim83a
]
. Following
[
Sim83a
]
, we take
\Sigma
a smooth closed
n
-dimensional surface, and
\mathcal{E}
a functional defined on
C^{1}
functions
u
given by

\mathcal{E}(u)=\int_{\Sigma}E(x,u,\nabla u),

where
E
satisfies the convexity and analyticity hypotheses of
[
Sim83a
, (1.2), (1.3)]
. We write
\mathcal{M}(u)
for the
-\mathrm{grad}\mathcal{E}
in the
L^{2}
sense (i.e. so that

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}(u+t\zeta)=-\int_{\Sigma}\mathcal{M}(u)\zeta\quad\forall\zeta\in C^{1}(\Sigma)

holds), and
L
for the linearization of
\mathcal{M}
at
u=0
. As in
[
Sim83a
]
, we allow
u
to take values in some vector bundle
V
over
\Sigma
.

Let us recall some notation from
[
Sim83a
]
. Let
u
be a
V
-valued function on
\Sigma\times[0,T)
. Write
\nabla
for the connection derivative on
V
, and
\dot{u}\equiv\partial_{t}u
. Write
|u(t)|_{0}=|u(t,\cdot)|_{C^{0}(\Sigma)}
,
||u(t)||=||u(t,\cdot)||_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
, and

\displaystyle|u|_{1}^{*}(t)

\displaystyle=|u(t)|_{0}+|\nabla u(t)|_{0}+|\dot{u}|_{0},

\displaystyle|u|_{2}^{*}(t)

\displaystyle=|u|_{1}^{*}(t)+|\nabla^{2}u(t)|_{0}+|\nabla\dot{u}(t)|_{0}+|\ddot{u}(t)|_{0}.

We consider here
C^{2}

V
-valued functions
u
on
\Sigma\times[0,T)
solving the PDE

(129)

-\ddot{u}+m\dot{u}-\mathcal{M}(u)-\mathcal{R}_{1}(u)-\mathcal{R}_{2}(u)=f(x,t),

where
m>0
, and:

(1)

f(x,t)
is a
C^{2}
function satisfying
|f|_{2}^{*}(t)\leq\delta e^{-\epsilon t}
;

(2)

\mathcal{R}_{1}
has the form

\mathcal{R}_{1}(u)=(a_{1}\cdot\nabla^{2}u+a_{2})\dot{u}+a_{3}\cdot\nabla\dot{u}+a_{4}\ddot{u}

where
a_{i}
are
C^{2}
functions of
(x,t,u,\nabla u,\dot{u})
such that
a_{i}(x,t,0,0,0)=0
for
i=2,3,4
;

(3)

\mathcal{R}_{2}
has the form

\mathcal{R}_{2}(u)=b_{1}\cdot\nabla^{2}u+b_{2}\cdot\nabla\dot{u}+b_{3}\ddot{u}+b_{4}\cdot\nabla u+b_{5}\dot{u}+b_{6}u,

where
b_{i}
are
C^{2}
functions of
(x,t)
such that
|b_{i}|_{2}^{*}(t)\leq\delta e^{-\epsilon t}
.

The main content of our Theorem
11.1
is that one can replace Simon’s bounded exponential growth condition
[
Sim83a
, Definition 2]
with a bounded linear growth condition (Definition
11.0.1
), at the expense of replacing a decay estimate with a smallness estimate. Though the name suggests otherwise, our linear growth condition is not a strictly weaker notion than Simon’s exponential growth condition – only when
|u|
is more-or-less larger than
\delta
are they comparable. Fortuitously, this is enough for our purposes.

The advantage is that the linear growth condition is effectively equivalent to smallness of density drop of a minimal surface
M
,
without
any assumptions about the actual value of the density. For example, the linear growth condition results directly from an assumption like
\sup_{r}|\theta_{M}(0,r)-\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)|\leq\delta^{2}
. In contrast, the exponential growth condition of
[
Sim83a
]
requires
additionally
the inequality
\sup_{r}\theta_{M}(0,r)\geq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
. This second condition is too strong for our purposes, because in our annular cone regions we only know
M
is varifold close to
\mathbf{C}
, and
\theta_{M}(0,r)
is close to
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
. In particular,
\theta_{M}(0,r)
may be
>\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
at some radii, but
<\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
at others.

On the other hand, the stronger assumption of Simon also gives a stronger estimate. The exponential growth condition allows
[
Sim83a
]
to rule out exponential growth of
u
at any time, to deduce that
u
exhibits only decay. This also illustrates the asymmetry of the lower bound on
\theta_{M}(0,r)
: if one instead assumed
\sup_{r}\theta_{M}(0,r)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, one could show
u
exhibits only growth (corresponding to uniqueness the tangent cone of
M
at infinity).

With our linear growth condition (
130
), we can only rule out exponential growth (or decay) when
|u|>>\delta
, and so our Theorem
11.1
can only conclude a smallness of
u
.
u
may still exhibit both exponential growth and decay. The linear growth condition is symmetric: smallness of
u
propogates both forward and backward in time. Of course, like
[
Sim83a
]
, we can also give a summability estimate (
134
) for
||\dot{u}(t)||
, as the decay/growth behavior of
u
is quantifiable.

Definition 11.0.1
.

Given
K\geq 1
,
\delta>0
, we say a solution
u
of (
129
) on
\Sigma\times[0,T)
has
(K,\delta)
-linear growth if

(130)

|u|^{*}_{2}(t)\leq\delta\max\{1,|t-s|\}+K|u|^{*}_{2}(s)

for every
t,s,\in[0,T)
.

Our modification of
[
Sim83a
, Theorem 1]
is the following:

Theorem 11.1
.

Take
T_{*}\geq T_{0}>0
,
K>0
. There are
\alpha(E),\epsilon(E)\in(0,1/2)
,
\delta_{0}(\epsilon,K,T_{0},E,\mathcal{R}_{1})>0
so that the following holds. Let
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
, and let
u
be a
C^{2}
solution to (
129
) on
\Sigma\times[0,T_{*})
with
(K,\delta)
-linear growth, such that

(131)

\displaystyle|u|_{2}^{*}(t)\leq\delta\text{ for }t\in[0,T_{0}],

(132)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u(t))\geq\mathcal{E}(0)-\delta\text{ for }t\in[0,T_{*}),

and Items 1-3 above hold for
f
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}
and all
(x,t)\in\Sigma\times[0,T_{*})
. Then

(133)

|u|_{2}^{*}(t)<\delta^{\alpha}\quad\forall t\in[0,T_{*}),

and

(134)

\int_{0}^{T_{*}}||\dot{u}(t)||dt\leq\delta^{\alpha/2}.

Remark 11.2
.

If
m<0
, then Theorem
11.1
continues to hold if instead of (
132
) we have
\mathcal{E}(u(t))\leq\mathcal{E}(0)+\delta
for all
t\in[0,T_{*})
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as Simon’s. First note that by changing variables
\tilde{t}=mt
and replacing
E
with
\frac{1}{m}E
, it suffices to consider the case when
m=1
. We shall highlight: first, where Simon’s growth condition is used, and how it can be replaced with our linear growth assumption; and second, where
\mathcal{R}_{2}
can be handled.

The strategy is to assume (after shrinking
T_{*}
if necessary), that

(135)

|u|_{2}^{*}(t)<\delta^{\alpha}\quad\forall t\in[0,T_{*}),\quad\limsup_{t\to T_{*}}|u|_{2}^{*}(t)=\delta^{\alpha}.

and then use this to show that in fact

(136)

\limsup_{t\to T_{*}}|u|_{2}^{*}(t)<\delta^{\alpha},

thereby establishing a contradiction. The choice of constants
\alpha
,
\epsilon
,
\epsilon_{1}
,
\epsilon_{2}
,
\eta
,
R
,
\delta
will satisfy the same restrictions as in Simon, except we may take
R(\eta,\epsilon,E,K)
larger than Simon, and
\delta(\epsilon,R,K,T_{0},E,\mathcal{R}_{1})
smaller.

As noted in
[
Sim83a
]
, if
\zeta
is a solution to
\mathcal{M}(\zeta)=0
with
|\zeta|_{C^{2}(\Sigma)}\leq\delta^{2\alpha}
, then by differentiating (
129
) the functions
u
,
w=\dot{u}
,
v=u-\zeta
all solve equations of the form

(137)

\displaystyle-\ddot{u}+\dot{u}-Lu=e_{1}\cdot\nabla^{2}u+e_{2}\cdot\nabla\dot{u}+e_{3}\ddot{u}+e_{4}\cdot\nabla u+e_{5}\dot{u}+e_{6}u+f^{\prime},

where
L
is the linearization of
\mathcal{M}
at
0
, and
e_{i}(t,x)
,
f^{\prime}(t,x)
are
C^{1}
functions (different for each choice of
u,v,w
) satisfying
|e_{j}|_{1}^{*}(t)\leq c(E,\mathcal{R}_{1})\delta^{\alpha}
,
|f^{\prime}|_{1}^{*}(t)\leq 100\delta e^{-\epsilon t}
. Therefore, ensuring
\delta(\epsilon,\eta,E,R,\mathcal{R}_{1})
is small, we can apply the growth theorem
[
Sim83a
, Theorem 4]
to each of
u
,
\dot{u}
,
u-\zeta
to obtain integers

1\leq j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq k-1,\quad 1\leq k_{1}\leq k_{2}\leq k-1,\quad 1\leq l_{1}\leq l_{2}\leq k-1,

so that
[
Sim83a
, (5.9), (5.10), (5.11)]
hold with
r_{1},r_{2}
replaced by
j_{1},j_{2}
(in the case of
u
),
k_{1},k_{2}
(in the case of
\dot{u}
), and
l_{1},l_{2}
(in the case of
u-\zeta
).

The first point where Simon uses his growth assumption is to show that
j_{2}=l_{2}=k-1
. To show this using our linear growth assumption we argue as follows. First note that provided
\delta(R,K)
is sufficiently small, (
135
) and (
130
) imply

(138)

|u|_{2}^{*}(t)\geq\delta^{\alpha}/(2K)\quad\text{ whenever }(k-3)R\leq t<T_{*}.

And then, if we had
j_{2}<k-1
, using (
138
),
[
Sim83a
, (5.4), (5.11)]
, (
135
), we would obtain the following contradiction for sufficiently large
R(E,K)
and small
\delta(E,K)
:

(139)

\displaystyle\delta^{\alpha}/(2K)

\displaystyle\leq|u|_{2}^{*}((k-3/2)R)

(140)

\displaystyle\leq c(E)(\sup_{[(k-2)R,(k-1)R]}||u(t)||+\delta)

(141)

\displaystyle\leq c(E)(e^{-(\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon)R}\sup_{[(k-1)R,kR]}||u(t)||+\delta)

(142)

\displaystyle\leq c(E)(e^{-(\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon)R}\delta^{\alpha}+\delta).

So
j_{2}=k-1
. A similar argument shows we must also have
l_{2}=k-1
, since
|\zeta|_{C^{2}(\Sigma)}\leq\delta^{2\alpha}<<\delta^{\alpha}
.

The second (and only other) way in which exponential growth is used is to show that bounds of
||u(t)||
or
|u|_{2}^{*}(t)
in terms of
\delta^{p}
, for any particular
p\leq 1
, persist for nearby
t
. This also follows from our linear growth assumption: if we have

|u|_{2}^{*}(t)\leq C\delta^{p}\quad\text{or}\quad\sup_{[jR,(j+1)R]}||u(t)||\leq C\delta^{p}

for some
C>0
,
p\leq 1
,
t\in[0,T_{*})
or
0\leq j<k-1
, then (
130
) and
[
Sim83a
, (5.4)]
imply

|u|_{2}^{*}(s)\leq c(C,K,R)\delta^{p},\quad\text{or}\quad\sup_{[j^{\prime}R,(j^{\prime}+1)R^{\prime}]}||u(t)||\leq c(C,K,R)\delta^{p}

(respectively) for some constant
c(C,K,R)
, provided
|s-t|\leq 10R
,
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 6
,
0\leq j^{\prime}\leq k-1
, and
\delta(R,K,p)
is sufficiently small.

The second remainder term
\mathcal{R}_{2}
can be dealt with in the same fashion as
f
. Specifically, we have by
[
Sim83a
, (6.26)]
and our bound
|u|_{2}^{*}\leq\delta^{\alpha}
that

||\mathcal{R}_{2}(t)||+||f(t)||\leq c(E)\delta^{1/2}||\dot{u}(t)||\quad\forall t\in(k_{1}R,(k_{2}-1)R),

which is used in applying
[
Sim83a
, Lemma 1]
to obtain
[
Sim83a
, (6.34)]
; and by
[
Sim83a
, (6.46)]
,

||\mathcal{R}_{2}((k_{2}+2)R)||+||f((k_{2}+2)R)||\leq c(E)\delta^{1/2}\sup_{[(k_{2}+1)R,(k_{2}+3)R]}||\dot{u}(t)||,

which is used in the estimate for
||v(t)||
on
[
Sim83a
, Page 557]
. The rest of the proof of estimate (
133
) proceeds as in
[
Sim83a
]
.

To prove (
134
), first note that we have by (
133
) the bound
||\dot{u}(t)||\leq c(\Sigma)\delta^{\alpha}
for all
t
. Now by
[
Sim83a
, (6.31), (6.34)]
we get

\int_{0}^{(k_{2}-1)R}||\dot{u}||dt\leq c(\epsilon)\delta^{1/2}+c(E)\delta^{\theta}+c(E)\delta^{\alpha}.

For
k_{2}-1\leq j\leq k-2
, note that from
[
Sim83a
, (6.41)]
we get
\sup_{[jR,(j+1)R]}||\dot{u}(t)||\leq c(\Sigma)e^{-(\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon)(j-k+1)R}\delta^{\alpha}
, and therefore

\int_{(k_{2}-1)R}^{T_{*}}||\dot{u}||dt\leq c(E,R)\delta^{\alpha}\sum_{j=0}^{k-k_{2}}e^{-(\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon)j}\leq c(E,R,\epsilon)\delta^{\alpha}.

Ensuring
\delta(E,R,\epsilon)
is sufficiently small, we obtain (
134
).
∎

12.
Appendix 2: a function

In constructing our parameterization we used the following
1
-dimensional Lemma.

Lemma 12.1
.

Given
\epsilon\in(0,1]
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,k}=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:-\epsilon<a_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots<a_{k}<\epsilon\}
. Then there is a smooth function
f\equiv f_{\{a_{i}\},\{b_{i}\}}(t):\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,k}\times\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,k}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying:

f(a_{i})=b_{i},\quad f|_{\{|t|\geq 2\epsilon\}}=t,\quad\frac{9}{10}\underline{M}\leq\partial_{t}f\leq 11\overline{M}

and

|\partial_{a_{j}}f|\leq c\overline{M},\quad|\partial_{b_{j}}f|\leq c,

where

\overline{M}=\max\left\{\max_{i}\frac{|b_{i+1}-b_{i}|}{|a_{i+1}-a_{i}|},3\right\},\quad\underline{M}=\min\left\{\min_{i}\frac{|b_{i+1}-b_{i}|}{|a_{i+1}-a_{i}|},\frac{1}{3}\right\},

and
c
is an absolute constant.

Remark 12.2
.

We highlight two basic facts about
f
. First, we have
f_{\{a_{i}\},\{a_{i}\}}(t)=t
. Second, if
\tilde{a}_{i}=-a_{k-i}
and
\tilde{b}_{i}=-b_{k-i}
, and
\tilde{f}=f_{\{\tilde{a}_{i}\},\{\tilde{b}_{i}\}}
, then we have
f_{\{a_{i}\},\{b_{i}\}}(t)=-\tilde{f}(-t)
.

Proof.

Fix
\underline{m},\overline{m}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
to be
1
-homogenous functions, smooth away from
0
, such that

(143)

\displaystyle\min\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\leq\underline{m}(s_{1},s_{2})\leq(101/100)\min\{s_{1},s_{2}\},\quad\underline{m}(s,s)=s,

(144)

\displaystyle\quad\overline{m}(s_{1},s_{2})\geq\max\{s_{1},s_{2}\}.

Fix
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
a smooth function satisfying

\eta|_{(-\infty,-1]\cup[1,\infty)}=0,\quad\eta|_{[-1/4,1/4]}=1,\quad t\eta^{\prime}(t)\leq 0,\quad|\eta^{\prime}|\leq 10.

Define
m:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
by setting

m(s_{1},s_{2},t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}s_{1}+\eta(t)(\underline{m}(s_{1},s_{2})-s_{1})&t<0\\
s_{2}+\eta(t)(\underline{m}(s_{1},s_{2})-s_{2})&t\geq 0\end{array}\right..

Trivially
m
is smooth on
\{s_{1},s_{2}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}
, and in this domain
m
satisfies

m+|\partial_{t}m|\leq 20\max\{s_{1},s_{2}\},\quad|\partial_{s_{i}}m|\leq c,

where
c=\max|D\underline{m}|
is an absolute constant.
m
is our transition gradient.

Define
\hat{f}(s_{1},s_{2},t)=m(s_{1},s_{2},t)t
. Trivially
\hat{f}
is smooth on
\{s_{1},s_{2}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}
, and
\hat{f}
satisfies

\hat{f}|_{(-\infty,-1]}=s_{1}t,\quad\hat{f}|_{[1,\infty)}=s_{2}t,\quad\hat{f}(s_{1},s_{2},0)=0.

A straightforward computation shows that

(9/10)\min\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\leq\partial_{t}\hat{f}\leq 11\max\{s_{1},s_{2}\},\quad|\partial_{s_{i}}\hat{f}|\leq c|t|,

with
c
an absolute constant.
\hat{f}
is our model transition function, between two linear functions of different positive gradients.

Take
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k},\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon,k}
. Let us define
a_{0}=b_{0}=-\frac{3}{2}\epsilon
,
a_{k+1}=b_{k+1}=\frac{3}{2}\epsilon
, and

(145)

\displaystyle r_{i}

\displaystyle=\overline{m}\left(\frac{2}{a_{i+1}-a_{i}},\frac{2}{a_{i}-a_{i-1}}\right)\quad i=1,\ldots,k,

(146)

\displaystyle m_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{i+1}-b_{i}}{a_{i+1}-a_{i}},\quad i=0,\ldots,k.

Now we let

f(\{a_{i}\},\{b_{i}\},t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\frac{\epsilon}{4}\hat{f}(1,m_{0},\frac{4}{\epsilon}(t+\frac{3}{2}\epsilon))-\frac{3}{2}\epsilon&t\leq a_{1}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}\\
\frac{1}{r_{i}}\hat{f}(m_{i-1},m_{i},r_{i}(t-a_{i})+b_{i}&a_{i}-\frac{1}{r_{i}}\leq t\leq a_{i}+\frac{1}{r_{i}}\\
m_{i}(t-a_{i})+b_{i}&a_{i}+\frac{1}{r_{i}}\leq t\leq a_{i+1}-\frac{1}{r_{i+1}}\\
\frac{\epsilon}{4}\hat{f}(m_{k},1,\frac{4}{\epsilon}(t-\frac{3}{2}\epsilon))+\frac{3}{2}\epsilon&t\geq a_{k}+\frac{1}{r_{k}}\end{array}\right..

Then one can readily check
f
is smooth, well-defined, and satisfies

(9/10)\min_{i}\{m_{i}\}\leq\partial_{t}f\leq 11\max_{i}\{m_{i}\},\quad|\partial_{a_{j}}f|\leq c\max_{i}\{m_{i}\},\quad|\partial_{b_{j}}f|\leq c,

for any
j=1,\ldots,k
, and for
c
being an absolute constant. This proves the Lemma. ∎

13.
Appendix 3: quadratic cones

In this section we prove Theorem
2.8
. We shall work in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, for
n\geq 7
. We will want to use a slight variant of Theorem
6.3
, the proof being virtually verbatim (in fact easier). Let us state that first.

Theorem 13.1
.

Let
\mathbf{C}^{n}
be a smooth (away from
0
), stationary cone in
\mathbb{R}^{n+k}
. Given any
\epsilon>0
, there are constants
\delta(\mathbf{C},k,\epsilon)
,
\beta(\mathbf{C},k,\epsilon)
so that the following occurs. Let
\rho\geq 0
,
g
be a
C^{3}
metric on
B_{1}
satisfying
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\beta
, and let
V
be a stationary integral
n
-varifold in
(B_{1},g)
. Suppose there is a
C^{2}
function
u:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,1/2}\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}
so that

(147)

\displaystyle\mathrm{spt}V\cap A_{1,1/2}=G_{\mathbf{C}}(u),\quad|u|_{C^{2}}\leq\delta

(148)

\displaystyle\theta_{V}(0,1)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)+\beta,\quad\theta_{V}(0,\rho/2)\geq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)-\beta.

Then
u
can be extended to a
C^{2}
function on
\mathbf{C}\cap A_{1,\rho}
, so that

\mathrm{spt}V\cap A_{1,\rho}=G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap A_{1,\rho},\quad|x|^{-1}|u|+|\nabla u|+|x||\nabla^{2}u|\leq\epsilon.

Proof.

Same as Theorem
6.3
, except we use Lemma
13.2
in place of Lemma
6.2
.
∎

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
\mathbf{C}^{n}
be a smooth (away from
0
), stationary cone in
\mathbb{R}^{n+k}
. Given
\epsilon>0
, there is a
\beta(\mathbf{C},k,\epsilon)
so that the following occurs. Let
g
be a
C^{3}
metric on
B_{1}
satisfying
|g-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{2}(B_{1})}\leq\beta
, and let
V
be a stationary integral
n
-varifold in
(B_{1},g)
satisfying

(149)

\displaystyle\mathrm{spt}V\cap A_{1,1/2}=G_{\mathbf{C}}(u),\quad|u|_{C^{2}}\leq\beta,

(150)

\displaystyle\theta_{V}(0,1)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)+\beta,\quad\theta_{V}(0,1/8)\geq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)-\beta.

Then

(151)

\mathrm{spt}V\cap A_{1,1/4}=G_{\mathbf{C}}(u)\cap A_{1,1/4},\quad|u|_{C^{2}}\leq\epsilon.

Proof.

Proof by contradiction: otherwise, there are
\beta_{i}\to 0
, metrics
g_{i}
such that
|g_{i}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\beta_{i}
, and
V_{i}
being stationary integral varifolds in
(B_{1},g_{i})
such that (
149
), (
150
) hold with
\beta_{i}
in place of
\beta
, but (
151
) fails. Passing to a subsequence, we can assume
V_{i}\to V
as varifolds for some stationary integral varifold
V
in
(B_{1},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
. Since
\theta_{V}(0,1)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
and
\lim_{r\downarrow 1/8}\theta_{V}(0,r)\geq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, monotonicity implies
V
is dilation-invariant in
A_{1,1/8}
. On the other hand, by (
149
) we can assume that
V_{i}\to[\mathbf{C}]
in
C^{2}
with multiplicity-one in
A_{1,1/2}
. So we must have
V\llcorner A_{1,1/8}=[\mathbf{C}]\llcorner A_{1,1/8}
. Allard’s theorem implies (
151
) holds for
i>>1
, which is a contradiction.
∎

We could use the cone regions from Section
6
, but to avoid clashes of notation and because everything in this proof is vastly easier, let us use instead the following simpler notion of cone regions.

Definition 13.2.1
.

Let
V
be a varifold in
B_{R}(a)
. We say
V\llcorner A_{R,\rho}(a)
is a
(\mathbf{C},\beta,\tau)
-weak-cone
*
region if for every
r\in(\rho/4,(1-10^{-2})R]
, there is an
a_{r}\in B_{R}(a)
with
B_{r}(a_{r})\subset B_{R}(a)
, so that

\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)-\beta\leq\theta_{V}(a_{r},r)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)+\beta,

and if
s\in(\rho/4,(1-10^{-2})R]\cap[r/2,2r]
then

|a_{r}-a_{s}|\leq\tau\max\{r,s\}.

We say
V\llcorner A_{R,\rho}(a)
is a
(\mathbf{C},\beta)
-cone
*
region if for every
r\in(\rho/4,R]
we have

\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)-\beta\leq\theta_{V}(a,r)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)+\beta.

If we think of
\sigma=10^{-2}
,
m=1
, and replace
c(\Lambda,m)
with
c(\mathbf{C})
, then Lemma
6.1
and its proof continue to hold for cone
*
regions. We are now prepared to prove Theorem
2.8
.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

We prove Theorem
2.8
by contradiction. We will choose our constants as we go along, and a posteriori they can all be fixed, but the reader should think of them like:

\beta^{\prime\prime}<<\tau<<\beta^{\prime}<<\epsilon^{\prime}<<\epsilon<1.

Suppose the theorem fails: then there is a sequence of numbers
\delta_{i}\to 0
, metrics
g_{i}
such that
|g_{i}-g_{\mathrm{eucl}}|_{C^{3}(B_{1})}\leq\delta_{i}
, and stationary integral varifolds
V_{i}
in
(B_{1},g_{i})
, so that (
7
) holds with
\delta_{i}
,
g_{i}
,
V_{i}
in place of
\delta
,
g
,
V
, but (
9
) fails for any
a,\lambda,q
satisfying (
8
). Passing to a subsequence, standard compactness for integral varifolds implies we can find a stationary integral varifold
V
in
(B_{1},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
, so that
V_{i}\to V
as varifolds. By our hypotheses and the constancy theorem we must have
V=[\mathbf{C}]
, and so by Allard’s theorem there is no loss in assuming
V_{i}\to[\mathbf{C}]
as varifolds in
B_{1}
and in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
B_{1}\setminus\{0\}
.

Let
\rho_{i}
be the least number such that
V_{i}\llcorner A_{1,\rho_{i}}(0)
is a
(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime\prime},\tau)
-weak-cone
*
region. Let
a_{i}=a_{\rho_{i}}(V_{i})
as in Definition
13.2.1
, and then by our convergence
V_{i}\to[\mathbf{C}]
we have
a_{i}\to 0
,
\rho_{i}\to 0
. By Lemma
6.1
, ensuring
\beta^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime})
,
\tau(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime})
are sufficiently small, each
V_{i}\llcorner A_{9/10,\rho_{i}}
is a
(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime})
-cone
*
region, and hence by our
C^{2}
convergence and by Theorem
13.1
, ensuring
\beta^{\prime}(\mathbf{C},\epsilon^{\prime})
is small and
i>>1
, we can find
C^{2}
functions
u_{i}:\mathbf{C}\cap A_{9/10,\rho_{i}/2}\to\mathbf{C}^{\perp}
so that

(152)

\mathrm{spt}V_{i}\cap A_{9/10,\rho_{i}/2}(a_{i})=a_{i}+G_{\mathbf{C}}(u_{i})\cap A_{9/10,\rho_{i}/2},\quad|x|^{-1}|u_{i}|+|\nabla u_{i}|+|x||\nabla^{2}u_{i}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon.

If
\rho_{i}=0
for infinitely-many
i
then by (
152
) we obtain a contradiction for
i
large. Suppose now every
\rho_{i}>0
. Define the rescaled varifolds
V_{i}^{\prime}=(\eta_{a_{i},\rho_{i}})_{\sharp}V_{i}
, and metrics
g_{i}^{\prime}=g_{i}\circ\eta_{a_{i},\rho_{i}}^{-1}
. Then for every
R>2
and
i>>1
, we have

(153)

1=\inf\{\rho:V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner A_{R,\rho}(0)\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime\prime},\tau)$-weak-cone$*$ region}\},

and
V_{i}^{\prime}
is stationary in
(B_{R},g_{i}^{\prime})
, and
g_{i}^{\prime}\to g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Monotonicity (
18
) implies that
\theta_{V_{i}^{\prime}}(0,R)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)+o(1)
, and so we can pass to a subsequence and obtain a stationary integral varifold
V^{\prime}
in
(\mathbb{R}^{n+1},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
satisying
\theta_{V^{\prime}}(0,\infty)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, so that
V_{i}^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
as varifolds. From (
152
), Arzela-Ascoli, and standard elliptic estimates, provided
\epsilon^{\prime}(\mathbf{C})
is small, we can assume that

(154)

\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime}\cap A_{\infty,1/2}=G_{\mathbf{C}}(u^{\prime})\cap A_{\infty,1/2},\quad|x|^{-1}|u^{\prime}|+|\nabla u^{\prime}|+|x||\nabla^{2}u^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime},

and
V_{i}^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
in
C^{2}
on compact subsets of
A_{\infty,1/2}
. We can also assume that
V^{\prime}\llcorner A_{\infty,1}
is a
(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime})
-cone
*
region.

Together the previous two sentences imply that if
V^{\prime\prime}
is a tangent cone of
V^{\prime}
at infinity, then
d_{H}(\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime\prime}\cap B_{1},\mathbf{C}\cap B_{1})\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
, and
|\theta_{V^{\prime\prime}}(0)-\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)|\leq\beta^{\prime}
. Ensuring
\epsilon^{\prime}(\mathbf{C})
,
\beta^{\prime}(\mathbf{C})
is small, we deduce by integrability of
\mathbf{C}
that
V^{\prime\prime}=[q(\mathbf{C})]
for some rotation
q\in SO(n+1)
satisfying
|q-Id|\leq c(\mathbf{C})\epsilon^{\prime}
.
[
SS86
]
or
[
ES19
, Corollary 3.7]
then implies that
V^{\prime}=[b+q(S_{\lambda})]
for some leaf
S_{\lambda}
of the Hardt-Simon foliation, where we allow
S_{0}=\mathbf{C}
. We aim to show
\lambda\neq 0
.

I claim there is an
\eta(\mathbf{C},\tau)
so that if
a\in B_{1}
,
r\in[1/2,4]
, and

|\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(a,r)-\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)|\leq\eta,

then
|a|\leq 2^{-10}\tau
. Suppose otherwise: there are sequences
\eta_{i}\to 0
,
a_{i}\in B_{2}
,
r_{i}\in[1/2,4]
so that
|\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(a_{i},r_{i})-\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)|\leq\eta_{i}
, but
|a_{i}|\geq 2^{-10}\tau
. Passing to a subsequence, we can assume
a_{i}\to a\in\overline{B_{1}}
,
r_{i}\to r\in[1/2,2]
, and so by monotonicity we get
\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(a,2r)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, and hence
\mathbf{C}\cap A_{\infty,2r}(a)=(a+\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap A_{\infty,2r}(a)
for some cone
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathbf{C}
has no symmetries, we must have
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}
and
a=0
. This is a contradiction.

Let us ensure
\beta^{\prime\prime}\leq\eta(\mathbf{C},\tau)
, and
\tau\leq 2^{-4}
. Assume that
V^{\prime}=[b+q(\mathbf{C})]
. By (
154
),
V^{\prime}
is regular outside
\overline{B_{1/2}}
, so we must have
b\in\overline{B_{1/2}}
. I next claim that if
a_{i,r}=a_{r}(V_{i}^{\prime})
, then for all
i>>1
and
r\in[1/2,2]
we must have

|a_{i,r}-b|\leq 2^{-9}\tau.

Note that by construction we have
a_{i,1}=0
, and (hence)
a_{i,r}\in B_{1/4}
. If my second claim failed, then we could find a sequence
r_{i}\in[1/2,2]
so that
|a_{i,r_{i}}|\geq 2^{-8}\tau
. Passing to a subsequence, we can assume
a_{i,r_{i}}\to a\in\overline{B_{1/4}}
and
r_{i}\to r\in[1/2,2]
, and hence
|\theta_{b+q(\mathbf{C})}(a,s)-\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)|\leq\beta^{\prime\prime}
for all
s>r
. From my first claim (applied in the ball
B_{1}(b)
), we deduce that
|a-b|\leq 2^{-10}\tau
. This proves my second claim.

Varifold convergence
V_{i}^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
implies
|\theta_{V_{i}^{\prime}}(b,r)-\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)|\leq\beta^{\prime\prime}
for
i>>1
and
r\in[1/16,2]
, and therefore combined with the previous paragraph we get that
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner B_{4}(0)\setminus B_{1/2}
is a
(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime\prime},\tau)
-cone
*
region for
i
large. This contradicts (
153
), so we must have
\lambda\neq 0
.

Now
V^{\prime}=[b+q(S_{\lambda})]
is smooth, multiplicity-one, and hence by Allard’s theorem
V_{i}^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
in
C^{2}
with multiplicity-one. Since
|b|\leq 1
and
\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime\prime}\cap B_{1}\neq\emptyset
, we have an upper bound
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{0}(\mathbf{C})
, and hence there is an
R(\mathbf{C},\epsilon^{\prime})
so that

(155)

(b+q(S_{\lambda}))\cap A_{\infty,R}=G_{q(\mathbf{C})}(v^{\prime}),\quad|x|^{-1}|v^{\prime}|+|\nabla v^{\prime}|+|x||\nabla^{2}v^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

For
i>>1
, by
C^{2}
convergence we can find
u_{i}^{\prime}:(b+q(S_{\lambda}))\cap B_{2R}\to S_{\lambda}^{\perp}
so that

(156)

\mathrm{spt}V_{i}^{\prime}\cap B_{2R}=\mathrm{graph}_{b+q(S_{\lambda})}(u_{i}^{\prime}),\quad|x|^{-1}|u_{i}^{\prime}|+|\nabla u_{i}^{\prime}|+|x||\nabla^{2}u_{i}^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

If we let
b_{i}+q(S_{\lambda_{i}})=\eta_{a_{i},\rho_{i}}^{-1}(b+q(S_{\lambda}))
, and ensure
\epsilon^{\prime}(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
is sufficiently small, then (
152
), (
155
), (
156
) imply (
8
), (
9
) hold with
b_{i},q,\lambda_{i}
in place of
a_{i},q,\lambda_{i}
. This is a contradiction, and finishes the proof of Theorem
2.8
for quadratic cones.

For general strictly-minimizing and strictly-stable
\mathbf{C}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and
\mathrm{spt}V
lying to one side of
\mathbf{C}^{n}
, the proof proceeds in a similar but even simpler fashion. Take
V_{i}
,
g_{i}
the counter-example sequence as before, and then let
\rho_{i}
be the least radius so that
V_{i}\llcorner A_{9/10,\rho_{i}}(0)
is a
(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime})
-cone
*
region. Our convergence
V_{i}\to[\mathbf{C}]
implies
\rho_{i}\to 0
, and ensuring
\beta^{\prime}(\mathbf{C},\epsilon)
is small, we can apply Theorem
13.1
to deduce (
152
) holds with
a_{i}=0
.

Let
V_{i}^{\prime}=(\eta_{0,\rho_{i}})_{\sharp}V_{i}
, and
g_{i}^{\prime}=g\circ\eta_{0,\rho_{i}}^{-1}
. Then for every
R>2
and
i>>1
we have

(157)

1=\inf\{\rho:V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner A_{R,\rho}(0)\text{ is a $(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime})$-cone$*$ region}\},

V_{i}^{\prime}
is stationary in
(B_{R}(0),g_{i}^{\prime})
,
\mathrm{spt}V_{i}^{\prime}
lies to one-side of
\mathbf{C}^{n}
, and
g_{i}^{\prime}\to g_{\mathrm{eucl}}
in
C^{2}(B_{R}(0))
.

As before, by monotonicity and standard compactness we can pass to a subsequence and assume that
V_{i}^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
, for
V^{\prime}
a stationary integral varifold in
(\mathbb{R}^{n+1},g_{\mathrm{eucl}})
such that
\theta_{V^{\prime}}(0,\infty)\leq\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
, and
\mathrm{spt}V^{\prime}
lies to one side of
\mathbf{C}^{n}
.
[
Sim21a
, Lemma 7.6]
implies that
V^{\prime}=[S_{\lambda}]
for some
\lambda
. If we had
\lambda=0
, then we would have
\theta_{V_{i}^{\prime}}(0,r)\to\theta_{V^{\prime}}(0,r)=\theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0)
for every
r>0
, and hence by monotonicity
V_{i}^{\prime}\llcorner A_{4,1/2}(0)
would be a
(\mathbf{C},\beta^{\prime})
-cone
*
region for
i>>1
, contradicting (
157
). So we must have
\lambda\neq 0
, and then the proof proceed in the same way as above.
∎
